# CucCut System Architecture Design

> One external business entry point, and one writer for video. The Node Runtime serves all clients; the Rust video engine has exclusive ownership of video transactions and persistence.

This document defines CucCut's modules, processes, state ownership and runtime mechanisms. User-visible behaviour is in the [product design](../product/product-design.md); persisted formats, DTOs and numeric rules are in [`spec/`](../spec/video-format-spec.md); acceptance criteria are in [acceptance and testing](../acceptance/acceptance-spec.md). For terms and scope markers, see the [document conventions](../README.md#conventions-for-this-document) and the [glossary](../glossary.md).

Types and interfaces in this document are the target contract, and are outlined in TypeScript syntax.

## 1. Overall architecture

Tech stack: Electron, React, TypeScript, Node.js, Rust, WebAssembly. UI components use React Spectrum 2 (`@react-spectrum/s2`), and the Renderer's client state uses Zustand (§11.1).

### 1.1 Three business modules, one external Runtime

```text
Desktop app (Home / Space) / CLI / external agents
                 |
          @cuccut/client / Tools
                 |
      WebSocket: Command / Query / Subscribe
                 |
        Node Runtime Worker
        Auth / Policy / API Gateway
                 |
   +-------------+----------------+
   |             |                |
Agent Harness   Video            Models
AgentManager    VideoService   ModelCatalog / Router
TaskController  Review / Render  Online Providers
Context / Tools Artifact management Local model management
   |             |                |
   +-------------+----------------+
     Jobs / Storage / ProcessHost
     (including Space directories)
                 |
     +-----------+----------------------+
     |           |                      |
Rust Engine Host  Native compute worker  Code Runtime
VideoEngine       Render / Media / ML    Browser / Remotion
VideoStore
```

The Runtime's business modules call each other directly through in-process interfaces. They do not wrap internal calls in HTTP just for the sake of uniformity. The Engine Host is private infrastructure of the Video module; it is not a second backend for the UI to connect to.

### 1.2 Module responsibilities

| Module | Sole responsibility | Not responsible for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Agent Harness | User tasks, sessions, context, tools, approval coordination, delivery packaging | Exact cut points and rounding, video database writes, execution of model weights |
| Video | Video command facade, change review, engine connection, preview, export and media references | Native agent sessions, model provider protocols |
| Models | Capability catalog, routing, provider adapters, model installation and execution plans | Directly editing the timeline, declaring a video delivered |
| VideoEngine / VideoStore | Domain validation, time and editing semantics, video locks, transactions, receipts, video events | Chat, purchasing, outbound model requests |
| JobManager | Execution ledger, application ledger, input freezing, retries and reconciliation, resource admission | Treating model text as the truth of a task, holding a video locked for a long time |
| Storage | Runtime Store, Artifact / Blob Store, Space directories | Changing video versions independently |

### 1.3 Each piece of state has exactly one authority

| State | Authoritative owner | Other components may hold |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Video entities, versions, transactions and undo records | VideoEngine / VideoStore | A read-only projection for the UI, a frozen context for the agent |
| Video event `eventSeq` and change outbox | VideoStore, persisted together with the transaction | The Runtime's delivery cursor, the UI's de-duplication cursor |
| Sessions, Tasks, Runs, approvals, authorisations, video memory | Runtime Store | Projections on conversation cards |
| Jobs, external task IDs, budget reservations, Application state | Job Ledger | Progress cards, review pages |
| Bytes of source media, documents and code packages | Immutable Artifact / Blob Store | Content digests and restricted resource handles |
| Video references of assets and code packages | VideoStore | Resource manifests and cache indexes |
| Space entries | No independent authority: derived from the items above (§5.7) | A rebuildable index |
| User marks on Spaces (favourites, trash, display names) | Runtime Store | — |
| Drag state, selections, playhead, drafts, panel layout | Renderer | `ContextSelection` frozen at submission time |
| GPU textures, decoders, loaded models and browser sessions | The corresponding executor | Lifecycle-managed handles |
| Windows, menus, system permissions and service ownership | Electron Main and the Supervisor, each for its own | Host state summaries |

Node does not add video versions on its own from cached copies; Rust does not copy model task state into a second ledger. Between persisting task outputs and applying them to a video, idempotent receipts are used for reconciliation; no cross-database distributed transaction is required.

### 1.4 The TypeScript and Rust boundary

**TypeScript** implements the UI, the Runtime, the agent drivers, online model providers, tasks and budgets, the protocol gateway and the video service facade.

**Rust** implements the VideoEngine / VideoStore, media timing, editing, subtitles and anchor semantics, the RenderGraph, compositing, encoding and decoding, and local inference.

Pure semantic modules may be compiled to WASM so the UI can make interaction predictions; predictions have no write authority. Pure-semantic WASM and the stateful native Engine Host are different things. Time-consuming edit pre-checks, decoding, inference and rendering must not block Node's control thread.

Whether to optimise small native capabilities through embedded bindings requires separate measurement and verification; this must not be used as a reason to restore dual writes between Node and Rust.

### 1.5 Domain objects

| Object | Definition | Authority |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Project | A project: one directory, which is also the agent's working directory; contains one or more videos | The directory itself, identified by `.ccut/project.json` inside it (§5.1); the Runtime Store holds the registration (an index from identifier to path) and session bindings |
| Video / Revision | A video: a subdirectory under a project, with an editable timeline and its committed versions | VideoStore |
| Conversation | A collaboration record between a user and an agent; may belong to a project or to none | Runtime Store |
| AgentSession | A driver's native session | The driver; the Runtime Store holds the resume handle |
| Turn | One model round | The driver |
| Task / TaskContract | One delegated result and its agreed terms | Runtime Store |
| Run | One execution attempt of a task, with an execution generation `runGeneration` and budget state | Runtime Store |
| Job | One computation: recognition, generation, building, rendering and so on | Job Ledger |
| Artifact | An immutable result published by a Job | Artifact Store |
| Application | The action of applying a result to a video, with its own idempotency key and receipt | Job Ledger |
| ChangeSet / Transaction | A pending change / a contract executed atomically after validation | VideoEngine |
| Candidate | A proposed option. Not applying it does not make it pass as the working draft | Artifact Store + Runtime Store |
| Checkpoint / Variant | A named historical state / an explicit configuration for a derived output | VideoStore |

On the interface, a Task is shown as “task”; a Job is shown to users as a concrete step. The end of one generation Job cannot mark the whole delegation as done.

### 1.6 Key design decisions

| ID | Decision | Rationale and constraints | Details |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D01 | Node's VideoService is the only external business entry point; Rust's VideoEngine / VideoStore is the only authority for video transactions and persistence | Node does not write video entities directly and does not add versions itself | §4.3, §5 |
| D02 | The Engine Host is a stateful host privately managed by the Runtime | It exposes no peer-level product API to the UI or CLI; each video is locked independently and kept resident on demand | §2.3 |
| D03 | Task, Run, Turn, Job and Application are modelled separately | Result delegation, execution attempts, model rounds, computation and result application each have their own ID and state | §1.5, §7.1 |
| D04 | The main stop button executes `tasks.stop` | First forbid new calls and automatic submissions, then request cancellation of child Jobs; `agents.interrupt` and `jobs.cancel` are explicit secondary actions | §7.4 |
| D05 | P0 strictly validates video versions; conflicts are not automatically rebased | The readSet is retained; P1 opens rebasing only for changes verified to be mutually independent | Command and protocol spec §3 |
| D06 | Version numbers and `eventSeq` are decimal strings; `commandId` is the public idempotency key; `actor` is injected only by the trusted Runtime | Avoids losing precision for 64-bit integers in JSON; prevents clients from forging identity | §4.2 |
| D07 | Job execution, Artifact readiness and Application each have their own records | Rendering and pure analysis may have no video application; one artifact may be applied to several videos | §7.1 |
| D08 | P0 has at least one Driver that completes both closed loops | Multi-driver capability boundaries are kept; the others are opened according to how well they are verified, without claiming equivalent functionality | §3.3 |
| D09 | Visuals drawn by code are persisted under the single instance type `composition` | The browser contract and the Remotion engine are adapted separately; framework names are only hints and do not expand the instance types | §8.1 |
| D10 | The new video format does not depend on any old format | Importing legacy projects is a separate one-way conversion; no long-term dual writes or compatibility layer exists | §13.4 |
| D11 | Closing a view does not cancel tasks | When quitting the desktop app, the user explicitly chooses to continue in the background or stop the service; service stop, hibernation and remote billing are explained separately | §2.2 |
| D12 | “Done” is determined by the delivery stage specified in the TaskContract | Preview tasks deliver candidates; apply tasks must have a receipt; final-cut tasks must have a validated, published file | §7.1, §9.11 |
| D13 | Home and Space are two views over the same Runtime and the same objects | Changes from either side go through the same command entry and follow the same version checks, protection and permissions; there are not two sets of business logic | §11.2 |
| D14 | The Space directory is a derived index, not a second store | Entries come from VideoStore, Artifact Store and the Job Ledger; the index is rebuildable; deletion follows the reference graph | §5.7 |
| D15 | Every entry point that CucCut opens to other programs is a managed “external service” within the Runtime | Each has its own listening surface, authentication and access policy, and is off by default; external requests enter the same command entry as a service principal, with no separate business logic | §4.8, §12.8 |
| D16 | Cross-video reusable user data lives in the user library under the Runtime Home and is copied when entering a video | A video does not change after entries in the library change (§5.9); editing or deleting library entries changes no existing video | §5.9 |
| D17 | Computation not initiated by an Agent is also a Job | Tool pages, external services and fixed-flow submissions go through the same JobManager, only without a Task; the rules for freezing, validation, publishing and application stay the same | §7.9 |

---

## 2. Processes and lifecycle

### 2.1 Process tree

```text
Electron Main
  +-- Renderer(s): React + WASM prediction and preview
  +-- Discovers or starts the CucCut Supervisor (can run without the UI)
        +-- Runtime Worker: Node, three business modules
              +-- Engine Host: Rust, single writer for video
              +-- Agent processes: one per session
              +-- Render / Media Workers: pooled by task
              +-- Model Workers: one process per resident model, started on demand (§6.5)
              +-- Browser / Remotion Workers: reused per code package and isolation domain
```

One Runtime Home corresponds to one Supervisor. Multiple windows reuse the same Runtime. When different Homes open the same video, they must still compete for the video-level operating-system lock; a different Home must not be a way around the single-writer rule. Read-only opening may be negotiated separately; a video containing unknown required capabilities must not be opened writable.

### 2.2 Startup, Ownership and Exit

**Discovery**. Electron manages only the services it created whose identity still matches. Discovery information includes `instanceId`, `home`, `supervisorPid`, `startedAt`, `endpoint`, `runtimeVersion` and `protocolVersion`. Startup goes through `acquiring-lock`, `starting`, `ready`, and `degraded` / `failed`; a running process does not mean it can accept work.

**Supervision**. The Supervisor starts the Worker, publishes ready, and handles restarts and stops, without depending on video or model code. Runtime crashes may restart within bounds, with backoff; startup failures report the error and are not relaunched indefinitely. A successor may acquire execution and write rights only after the old Worker and Engine Host are confirmed to have lost write and execution capability. PID, startup identity and generation must be verified together.

**Exit**. Closing a view only unsubscribes; closing a session does not archive videos or cancel tasks. When the desktop app quits while tasks are active, the user chooses “Keep running in background” or “Stop service and related local execution”, and the app shows the tasks that may still be running or billing remotely. Background mode depends on the service process and the device staying alive; it does not promise that local rendering continues across shutdown, sleep or service stop. When the desktop starts the Runtime, it sends `{ type: 'stop' }` over the IPC channel it passed in, asking the Runtime to stop per §2.4 (the same on all platforms; on Windows, `SIGTERM` is equivalent to killing the process directly and cannot be handled). It waits 8 seconds and force-kills on timeout. The Runtime also has its own 15-second fallback exit. Once exit has begun, the desktop no longer starts the Runtime: its window may still be open after its own Runtime stops, and on reconnect the UI gets an “app is exiting” error, so no Runtime is started that nobody will stop and that would become an orphan after the main process exits. In development, the Runtime echoes logs to the terminal; if the terminal is closed or the pipe is broken and output cannot be written, it only stops echoing, and logs are still written to the file. Terminal close (hangup, `SIGHUP`) asks the Runtime to wind down per §2.4, the same as `SIGINT` and `SIGTERM`. If the desktop main process's diagnostic output cannot be written (`EPIPE`, `EIO`), it is ignored and not treated as an uncaught exception. A channel disconnect (desktop crash, main-process restart in development) is not a stop request: the Runtime keeps running, and the next startup recovers it through the discovery file.

**Runtime launched by the CLI**. When the CLI needs to connect to a Runtime and none is running under `CUCCUT_HOME`, it launches one in the background by default (detached from the terminal, output written to `<CUCCUT_HOME>/logs/cli-runtime.log`), then connects once it writes the discovery file. `--no-start` does not launch and instead reports `RUNTIME_UNAVAILABLE` with exit code 3. The entry points, in order, are the environment variable `CUCCUT_RUNTIME_ENTRY`, the installed CucCut app (running the Runtime from the package with `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE`), and `apps/runtime` in the repository. The launch passes `--launched-by cli --idle-exit`: the discovery information and `launchedBy` in `runtime.info` are recorded as `cli` (`null` for the desktop and for `npm run runtime`), and when it has been idle for the full `runtime.idleExitMinutes` (default 10 minutes, §5.10) it stops itself in the order of §2.4. Idle means: no desktop, CLI or agent connection in use on the local gateway, no queued or running tasks, and no open external services. HTTP requests on the gateway port (agents in a session calling tools through the tool bridge, media and attachments) also reset the idle timer. An authenticated desktop or agent connection counts as in use as soon as it connects. A CLI connection counts only from its first request other than `runtime.status` and `runtime.info`, so a connection that only checks status (`cuccut runtime status`) does not change the idle timer on arrival or departure, and the `idleSince` reported by `runtime.status` is the value from before the query. When an in-use connection disconnects and nobody else is using the Runtime, idleness counts from the disconnect. `runtime.status` reports connection counts, task counts, open services and the idle-exit status (for `cli` and `desktop` connections only). `runtime.stop` stops only the Runtime the CLI launched: if the CLI did not launch it, it returns `forbidden` (`RUNTIME_NOT_OWNED`); if it is still in use, it returns `conflict` (`RUNTIME_IN_USE`): a desktop is connected, another CLI besides the caller is connected, or tasks are queued or running. On idle exit, it first withdraws its own discovery file and then stops per §2.4, so new CLIs no longer find it. A CLI that has read the discovery file but whose connection fails while the process is exiting looks again or launches once more when it sees the process gone (or the discovery file no longer points to it). If a newly launched instance exits because the instance lock has not yet been released, the CLI launches once more after the lock is released. If the desktop finds a CLI-launched Runtime at startup, it connects through the discovery file as usual and reuses it; while connected, it does not idle-exit. CLI commands are `cuccut runtime ensure|status|stop` (Agent capability-surface design §5.6).

### 2.3 Engine Host Ownership Barrier

VideoEngine is a stateful host that opens videos on demand, not a command started once per tool call. Each video holds one operating-system file lock, and each open obtains an `ownerGeneration`. The Runtime and the Engine Host communicate over a private IPC or a restricted socket; no public product port is exposed.

When the Runtime loses contact, the Engine Host stops accepting new modifications and enters a restricted shutdown. A replacement host must not take the write lock on the same file merely because the lease has timed out. It must first prove that the old process has exited, or revoke that process's write capability through an isolation mechanism that actually works, and only then acquire the video lock. If this cannot be proven, writes are blocked and reported, rather than gambling on “both sides believe they have expired.”

### 2.4 Order of a Normal Stop

```text
Stop accepting new work
  → Mark Task / Run as stopping; set up barriers for calls and applies
  → Settle video transactions still inside the commit boundary
  → Request cancellation or interruption of local Jobs and Agent Turns
  → Persist tasks, candidates, approvals and external task IDs
  → Close executors and Engine Host
  → Worker exits; Supervisor releases the instance lock
```

Exceeding the graceful-exit deadline may escalate to killing the process tree, but records of “unconfirmed cancellation” and “pending reconciliation” must be kept. Crash recovery of the service is not general task checkpoint-resume; see the recovery matrix in §7.5. Process isolation is a stability boundary and does not automatically constitute a security sandbox (§12).

### 2.5 What Each Lifecycle End Means

| Object | Result of ending | Does not mean |
| --- | --- | --- |
| UI connection | Stops observing; service state is kept | Cancelling all tasks |
| Agent Turn | The model output for this turn ends | Reverting applied changes |
| AgentSession | Releases the agent process; a native resume handle may be kept | Deleting videos and artifacts |
| Run / Task | Stops subsequent steps and automatic application | Cloud billing having stopped |
| Job | Compute is settled or pending reconciliation | Artifacts having been applied |
| Engine Host | Releases the video's write right and lock | The video being deleted |
| Runtime service | Local work and executors shut down | Remote tasks necessarily being cancelled |

Idle agent sessions may be released when it is safe, to control resource usage. Whether one can be released depends on whether the Driver has pending approval requests, background tools, or non-recoverable native work. Recovery only promises the persisted context, not resumption of the original execution stack. This policy must be made explicit for each Driver's capabilities.

### 2.6 App Updates

The desktop app's updates are handled by Electron Main: it checks the update source, downloads in the background, and verifies the signature and digest. The Runtime takes no part in downloading and does not replace binaries itself. The v3 app identity is `com.cuccut.app`. The update manifest is the published-version pin `apps/desktop/releases/appcast-<target>[-<variant>].json` in the repository, read via `raw.githubusercontent.com/hieudinh/cuccut/main/`; archive addresses point to the immutable version/build files on GitHub Releases. On publish, the public archive is verified first, then the manifest is committed and pushed. The historical skill's GitHub Latest stays as it is; new app releases use `--latest=false`. The old `com.jimliu.cuccut` official-site update source is not pointed at the new identity.

- **One distribution channel only.** The desktop app is distributed directly through GitHub Releases. macOS builds are signed and notarized, and self-update is built in. No app-store version is offered. The app store's sandbox does not allow launching user-installed agent runtimes, installing Skills into other agents' directories, or downloading and executing managed external tools (§12.9), and the design keeps no fallback path for it.
- **One set of binaries per version.** `engine-host`, `model-worker` and the Runtime ship with the desktop app at the same version and are not updated separately. Model weights, managed external tools (§12.9) and built-in Skills are not part of this set; each has its own version.
- **Pass the stop barrier before installing.** Installing requires replacing the running Runtime, so a normal stop (§2.4) comes first. When tasks are active, “Restart and update” lets the user choose “Install after tasks finish” or “Stop now and install”, and shows the tasks that may still be running or billing remotely.
- **Install on quit, never restart automatically.** When an update is downloaded, a normal quit installs it after the Runtime and background tasks have stopped as usual. The app is not reopened after installation, so the next launch runs the new version. Quitting already stops background tasks, so no extra confirmation is added. This only happens when the package can be swapped automatically, and it has a 5-second limit. If it cannot be done or fails, only a log is written and the app quits as usual. An install still goes ahead on quit if a silent check is in progress.
- **“Downloaded” means verified and in place.** After the download's size and sha256 are checked, the platform verification runs immediately, and only a pass moves the update to “Downloaded”. In the UI this stage appears as “Verifying” at 100% progress; it can be cancelled, and cancelling discards the installer package and the unpacked directory. If the cache already holds a complete installer for the same build at startup, it also goes through this stage before counting as “Downloaded”.
- **Package swap by platform.** On macOS, the verification stage unpacks the new package into the cache directory (`unpack-<build>`) and checks the bundle identifier, version, signature and notarization. At install time it only confirms that the unpacked `.app` is still there and its identifier is unchanged. A script then replaces the `.app` once the app has exited. “Restart and update” reopens the app after the swap; a quit-time install does not reopen it. If the unpacked package is gone, “Restart and update” reruns the verification stage, and a quit does not install. On Windows, only the copy installed by the installer is auto-updated. The installer is started silently only after the app has exited normally and the Runtime has stopped, and it replaces the files. “Restart and update” reopens the app after installing; a quit-time install does not reopen it. Where a swap is not possible (zip builds, a `.app` in a non-writable directory, etc.), the app shows the installer package and opens the download page. Packages built without a build number do not check for updates.
- **Keep checking after a download.** Automatic checks run silently both when a newer version exists and when an update is already downloaded, and they do not show “Checking”. If the update source still points to the same build, or a newer build exists but this machine's OS is too old for it, the update stays “Downloaded”. When another build appears, the old download is discarded (installer package and unpacked directory deleted) and the new version is handled. If the update source no longer has anything newer than the current version, the download is deleted and the app returns to “Up to date”. A failed check leaves things as they are.
- **Never run mismatched versions together.** If a new desktop shell finds a Runtime whose `runtimeVersion` differs from its own, it only reads that Runtime's status and prompts a service restart; it sends no business commands. When `protocolVersion` is incompatible, the connection is refused (§13.3). An old Runtime left running in background mode is not silently taken over by the new shell.
- **Staged rollout (optional).** The manifest may carry `rolloutHours` (hours, greater than 0) and `releasedAt` (release time, ISO 8601 with a time zone); older app versions ignore them as unknown keys. On an automatic check, the rollout opens linearly from `releasedAt` by elapsed time (0 before, 1 once `rolloutHours` has passed). The update is offered only if this machine's position is below the opened fraction; otherwise the check does not offer it and logs that. Silent checks (a new version found, or already downloaded) stay as they are. Other cases, including errors that carry a version, are treated as up to date. The position is generated randomly the first time it is needed, derived from an install identifier stored in the user data directory, and fixed for each installation. Manual checks are not restricted. If the pre-check state already carries the same build (a manual check found it, downloaded it, or the download failed), it is still offered, so an update the user already has is not withdrawn. If either field is missing or malformed, the manifest is treated as not staged and read as usual: it is better to push to everyone by mistake than to have nobody receive the update.
- **Update preferences** (automatic check and automatic download) belong to the preferences (§5.10). A download that fails verification is discarded and reported, and not installed.

---

### 2.7 Startup migration of historical versions

After the Runtime acquires the Home instance lock, it detects v1 / v2 data: on macOS, `~/Library/Application Support/CucCut`, the legacy `cli` subdirectory inside it, and the sibling `ccut`; on Windows, only v2 exists, so it detects `%APPDATA%/ccut` and `%APPDATA%/CucCut`; on Linux, `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/ccut` (or `~/.config/ccut` if unset). The desktop app's default development Home (`.dev/cuccut-home` under the repository) and the production Home are both detected automatically: the main process passes `CUCCUT_LEGACY_AUTO_DETECT=1` to the Runtime, so the development Home is not mistaken for a test sandbox. When the caller explicitly specifies another `CUCCUT_HOME`, isolation is the default; the default production `~/.cuccut` is still detected automatically. When `CUCCUT_LEGACY_ROOT` is set, only the specified directory is migrated, and the real UserDefaults / Keychain are not read.

Settings and service configuration are imported first, only if they have not been migrated yet, before the model catalog, node sharing and external services are constructed. Startup does not read the project index and does not enumerate project directories. Project discovery, cloud credentials, and credentials of paired remote nodes run after the Runtime is ready. Discovery results are written atomically to `store/legacy-upgrade/<source-digest>.json`; if startup fails and restarts, the manifest is reused and only unfinished items are processed. The importer and its engine run serially at below-normal priority, and before starting the next item they wait for any background task such as a model or export job. Projects whose assets are still missing only asynchronously check the missing paths from the last report, and do not start the engine. While migration is in progress, the CLI does not trigger idle exit; time spent waiting for the user to answer the import prompt is not counted. On exit, migration is cancelled first, the import child process is awaited, and only then is the instance lock released. Project conversion is performed by `legacy-import-worker.js`, which ships with the desktop build; in source mode it is started from `packages/legacy-import`. Writing video still goes only through the public protocol of `engine-host`. Settings, service configuration, cloud credentials and remote nodes continue to migrate in automatically; projects wait until the user confirms in the import prompt. All clients share this one Runtime migration.

- **Project discovery**: merges the old `projects.json`, `archive/projects.json`, the `projects/` directory at the root, and the configured `projects.dir`; external project paths in the index are also read; deduplication is by real path. If a directory has `project.json`, it goes through v2 conversion; macOS `doc.json` goes through the v1 adapter and is then converted; on Windows, v1 documents are not scanned, UserDefaults are not read, and the v1 converter is not run. Windows paths keep drive letters, UNC shares, spaces and backslashes, and the project index may still point to other volumes. v1 word moments are restored to source time, and transcripts, translations, subtitles, cut points and recognizable visual elements are imported. If the source media is absent but `audio16k.pcm` exists, a permanent WAV is generated in `legacy-media/` inside the new video directory; the old PCM is kept.
- **Import prompt**: when projects that have not yet been imported are found and the completion marker has no decision, the Runtime does not import on its own. It raises a prompt on the `legacy-import` topic, showing the project list (old title and last edit, most recent first) and the default directory. The desktop UI asks once (see [command protocol §4.1](../spec/command-protocol-spec.md#41-methods)). “Import” comes with a directory; the default is `CucCut` under the system Documents folder (the desktop app passes `app.getPath('documents')` through `CUCCUT_DOCUMENTS_DIR`, which recognizes known folders moved on Windows; if absent, `~/Documents` is used). The directory must be an absolute path, must be creatable and writable, and must not be inside the Runtime Home or a legacy root. “Skip” with “Don't remind me again” checked answers `never`: nothing is imported from now on, and the project parts of each source are recorded as complete directly and are no longer discovered. Only pressing “Skip” or closing the dialog sends no request: the prompt stays in the Runtime, the UI does not show it again in this startup, and it is asked again on the next startup; the Runtime does not record a skip. When there is no desktop UI to answer (CLI only), projects keep waiting and nothing is imported. The Web client does not ask: a remote browser cannot choose a local directory, so `legacyImport.*` and this topic are not in the Web service allowlist (§4.8).
- **Placement**: newly imported projects go into the import directory the user chose, and this directory itself is registered as a project (named after the directory, default `CucCut`). Each legacy project is converted into a video directory `legacy-<source-real-path-digest>/` inside it, and the video's display name keeps the old title; across legacy roots, one shared project is used, and identical titles do not collide. The project is registered only after the first video imports successfully. If an early version has already started importing (the marker contains project records) without a decision, the prompt is no longer shown: the `Imported/` project under the current project default directory is used (in production usually `~/CucCut/Imported`; `CUCCUT_PROJECTS_DIR` and isolated Homes follow the default-directory rules in §2.1), and that is recorded as the decision. Materials are linked at their original paths; old projects and old configuration are not moved, deleted or rewritten. Conversion warnings, missing materials and fields that were not imported are written to `import-report.json` in each video directory. A video's recent activity follows the old project: after import completes, the modification time of `video.db` and its WAL is set to the latest of the old index's last-opened time (v2 `lastOpenedAt`, v1 archive `modifiedAt`) and the project file's modification time; only if neither exists is it the import time. Otherwise, the moment of import would become every video's recent activity, and the order in Space would be completely scrambled. Existing migration records keep their original target directory and layout; already imported or edited videos are not moved. Conversion boundaries follow the [importer notes](../../scripts/legacy-import/README.md).
- **Import progress**: after the user answers “Import” (or, when something was left unimported last time, when startup automatically retries), the Runtime reports this startup's import on the same topic (`LegacyImportRun`; `run.updated` carries the full new state): each project is queued, importing, imported, not imported, or skipped; when other tasks are running and the import is paused, the overall state is `waiting`. Projects that were not imported carry a reason: the external drive or network volume holding the missing files is not connected (`offline`; macOS recognizes `/Volumes/<name>`, Windows recognizes drive letters and UNC shares, Linux recognizes `/media`, `/run/media` and `/mnt`; it counts only if the volume root does not exist); files were moved or deleted (`missing`, listing the first few missing files and the total); the old project cannot be read (`unreadable`); an error occurred during import (`failed`, with the path to `import-report.json`). Projects not imported can be retried (`legacyImport.retry`, all by default, queued if other tasks are running) or skipped (`legacyImport.setSkipped`). A skip is written to the completion marker and the project is not automatically imported later, and it can be undone; retrying a project by name clears its skip record. Projects neither imported nor skipped are automatically retried on the next startup. For how the UI presents this, see [product design §8.5](../product/product-design.md#85-input-compatibilityset-02).
- **Settings**: reads `config.json` (preferred) and `config.toml`, and the v2 app preferences in `app-v2-settings.json`; on macOS it also reads the relevant v1 preferences in `VoiceInk.plist` and `com.jimliu.cuccut.plist`. Migrated items: the model mirror, the default save directory (the v2 app's `vk-url-savedir` takes precedence; this is where “Download video” saves, migrated to `downloads.directory`), language, automatic update downloads, and the Agent's default provider, model, reasoning effort and access mode. Paths are expanded from `~/` and validated against the new settings schema; valid user values that the new version already has take precedence. If the save directory is the legacy default `Downloads` under the home directory (v2 wrote `~/Downloads`), it is not migrated: it is left empty, and the new version uses the host's download folder (which recognizes known folders moved on Windows; §7.9). Model mirrors migrate to an HF or compatible HTTP(S) base URL; ModelScope migrates to `https://www.modelscope.cn` (§6.3). The old CDN has no repositories in the new version, so it is recorded as not migrated. Legacy UI preferences that have no Runtime counterpart, such as theme and window size, only have their field names recorded; they are not forced into the new UI.
- **Cloud models**: the old `secrets.json` / `secrets.debug.json`, the macOS v2 `CucCut/__provider-vault-v1`, and the early standalone entries are written through the current `CredentialStore`. For v1, the credential helper enumerates the account attributes of only the two legacy services `CucCut` / `VoiceInk` (the `legacy-accounts` request; `key` may only be one of these two services; the response is an array of `accounts` strings that contain no keys). Selection follows the `vk-keyinuse-<provider>` tag, service priority and stable tag order, and then the password is read precisely. API endpoints, the MiniMax China access point, declarations of supported custom OpenAI-compatible models, and cloud model defaults are kept; an existing v3 Provider takes precedence. Before reading the old Keychain, providers that are already migrated, that already have a new Provider, and credentials already obtained from the old plaintext files are excluded; only cloud providers that still lack credentials read the shared vault. Remote node credentials are handled separately. On macOS, the account enumeration and password reads are performed by the credential helper with interaction disabled: the `legacy-read` request is `{ op: 'legacy-read', service, key: account }`, where service may only be `CucCut` / `VoiceInk` / `ccut`, and the response follows the format of `get`; the `security find-generic-password` command, which can pop up a dialog, is not called. When authorization or unlocking is needed, the item is recorded as pending migration and silently retried on the next startup; no password window is opened automatically. The user can reconfigure credentials in the new model settings. If vault access fails but reading the standalone entry succeeds, that Provider is still recorded as complete. Keys do not enter the migration report or logs; old Keychain entries are not deleted; no model API requests are sent. Keys that cannot be read or written are left for a retry on restart, and the credential backend is not fallen back. On Windows v2, keys are located via `key-masks.json` and read by the helper's read-only `legacy-get` operation from the `ccut` service (target name `<provider>.ccut`, keeping keyring's UTF-16 JSON encoding), and then written to the new Windows Credential Manager; old entries are not deleted, and there is no fallback to plaintext. If the helper is missing, the old entry does not exist, or access fails, the item is not recorded as complete and is retried on restart.
- **Services and remote compute**: reads the v2 `runtime/services.json`, `integrations-policy.json` and the `worker/` state. Migrates the auto-start and ports for MCP / Web; older Web settings are filled in from `serveEnabled` / `servePort` in `app-v2-settings.json`. Where the new version already has a corresponding service entry or a shared state file, the new value is kept. Shared compute migrates the enable switch, node identity, display name, last port and transcription capability switch; other v2 task switches, concurrency limits, the open pairing mode and node selection have no equivalent v3 configuration, so only their field names are recorded. MCP keeps the `read` / `ask` / `auto` levels; an old project scope cannot be treated as a video ID; if there is a scope restriction or disabled tools, it is first tightened to an empty video allowlist, disabled tools also tighten the tool to read-only, and permission items that need to be set again are recorded. The old MCP / Web login and the token formats of the shared-end client are incompatible, so they must be re-authorized / re-paired on the new service; old runtime management tokens are not imported. `remote.nodes.<alias>.*` migrates the node identifier, alias, name, HTTP host, port and pairing time; a v3 node with the same identifier takes precedence. Node tokens are written from the old credential `remote-<nodeId>` through the current secure storage (reading the old macOS Keychain also disables interaction); they do not go into node configuration, reports or logs; if reading fails, it is retried on restart. No probing, pairing or tasks are sent to remote nodes; whether an old node is compatible is decided by the normal protocol gate during ordinary use. The old `remote.default` refers only to a node and lacks the model ID that the v3 default model requires, so it is recorded as not migrated, and the user reselects it.
- **Completion marker**: `<runtime-home>/store/legacy-upgrade.json` (0600, written atomically) records, per source, the completion status of settings, cloud configuration, service configuration, remote nodes and each project. The top-level `projectImport` records the decision on project import (`import` with a directory, or `never`). Each source's `skipped` lists the projects the user skipped, and these are not queued for import during discovery. For a completed earlier marker that lacks the service / node checkpoints, only those configurations are migrated; projects are not rediscovered or imported again. After settings and cloud configuration succeed, a project failure does not cause them to be overwritten again. A project is complete only when engine conversion has no failures, there are no missing materials, and it has been registered. A legacy root that does not exist is also recorded as checked. Once all sources have completed fully, the global `complete: true` is written. From then on, each startup reads only this one marker; it no longer opens the settings file, reads the old index, enumerates directories, queries UserDefaults or the Keychain, or starts a migration child process. Resetting settings, deleting keys or deleting new projects later by the user will not revive old data. Unfinished projects continue after restart on the same target directory and transaction ID; `--replace` is not used automatically, so user edits are not overwritten. An unknown marker version is not overwritten; a failure only records that migration is pending a retry and does not block normal startup.

## 3. Agent Harness

### 3.1 Modules and Driver ABI

```text
AgentManager / DriverRegistry / SessionStore
ConversationStore / TaskController / ContextBuilder / VideoMemory
ToolCatalog / ApprovalService / ConversationProjector
CapabilityRegistry / GuidanceRegistry
Quality checkpoints / Completion inbox
```

`AgentDriver` connects one complete agent runtime; `ModelProvider` executes one structured model capability. The two are registered separately and are not mixed into one Provider table.

The driver uses a machine protocol and does not parse terminal screens. The unified operations are: create or resume a session, `startTurn`, `steer`, `interrupt`, `respondToApproval`, `subscribe`, `describePersistence`, and `close`.

```ts
interface AgentSession {
  id: Id;
  capabilities: VerifiedAgentCapabilities;
  startTurn(input: AgentInput, context: RunContext): Promise<{ turnId: Id }>;
  steer?(turnId: Id, input: AgentInput): Promise<'accepted' | 'unavailable'>;
  interrupt(turnId: Id): Promise<InterruptReceipt>;
  respondToApproval(id: Id, response: ApprovalResponse): Promise<void>;
  subscribe(listener: (event: AgentEvent) => void): () => void;
  describePersistence(): AgentPersistenceHandle | null;
  close(): Promise<void>;
}
```

The driver maps native events into driver-independent session entries (`AgentEvent`). When a file-changing tool call (`tool-call`, `tool: 'file-change'`) succeeds, `output` is a unified diff: if the native event carries a diff, it is used as is; if not, the driver synthesizes one from the tool input. On failure, `output` is the error text. `detail` is still the action and path.

A driver declares its capabilities: image input, native tools, approvals, resume, background work, and steer. A capability is opened only after it passes integration tests.

The driver id is an open string (`^[a-z][a-z0-9-]*$`, at most 63 characters). The built-in ids are `claude`, `codex`, `copilot`, `pi`, `opencode`, `gemini`, `cursor`, `grok`, and `kimi` (`BUILTIN_DRIVER_IDS`); the remaining ids are reserved for ACP agents that users add (§3.11). A built-in id is registered only once it has a driver implementation; an unregistered id is treated the same as a nonexistent one. Implemented drivers: Codex (the machine protocol of `codex app-server`), Claude Code (Claude Agent SDK), Pi (`pi --mode rpc`, JSON-line commands and events over stdio), OpenCode (the v2 HTTP API and event stream of `opencode serve`), and GitHub Copilot, Gemini CLI, Cursor Agent, Grok, Kimi Code, and user-added agents, connected through the Agent Client Protocol (ACP, JSON-RPC over stdio). These ACP agents share one implementation. Presets differ in their mapping of launch command, environment variables, login method, and access mode. GitHub Copilot's session mode id is in URL form; “全部放行” (allow all) is the config option `allow_all`: full access takes `#agent` and turns it on, and other modes turn it off. `#autopilot` is broader than full access and is not used. A user-added preset has only a launch command and environment variables; it does not check the version and has no install or login command; its model table and access modes come from the session response. Specific interfaces are confirmed against each product's official documentation; the Driver ABI does not take on any engine-specific concepts because of this.

The shared ACP implementation has run a complete session on a real machine. Under D08 it counts as verified, and sessions may be opened (§3.11). This verification used the claude-code-acp adapter (which wraps the locally logged-in Claude Code as an ACP agent) and covered probing, new session, approvals, follow-up questions, continuing after an interrupt, `session/load` recovery, and one full conversation turn through the Runtime (command and file-change approvals, the CucCut tool channel, and events written into the session store). The built-in presets and user-added agents have not yet been tested on CucCut themselves (`tested` is false); they are hints only and do not block. When a file-change step succeeds, `output` is a unified diff generated from ACP `diff` content blocks (`oldText` → `newText`); when the agent gives only changed fragments, line numbers are relative to the fragment. CucCut's own tools (the MCP service passed in when the session is opened), when the native side asks about them, are allowed directly, and the tool channel gates them by access mode (§3.12), consistent with the Claude Code driver. Two tool-name forms are recognized: `mcp__<service>__<tool>` (measured with claude-code-acp) and `<tool> (<service> MCP Server)` (Gemini CLI, inferred from source, not measured). Unrecognized names still prompt as usual. Known limitations: the tool channel (§3.5) is given only to agents that declare HTTP support (`mcpCapabilities.http`); in a session that does not support it, CucCut's tools are unavailable, and a notice appears at the start. Developer instructions are attached before the first-turn input of a new session (`session/new` has no such field). There is no steer. Access modes map to the agent's session modes, with the stricter of the two taking effect. “本会话放行” (allow for this session) is recorded by the driver itself and does not use the agent's native “always allow”. Restricted one-off calls (§6.9) are not supported.

The Pi driver's protocol path has been verified with a real pi (1.0.4) and a scripted model service (write, edit, command, read; steer, interrupt, resume; MCP and developer instructions), and is opened under D08 (`verified: true`). It has not yet been tested with a real model account (`tested: false`). Minimum version 0.84.4: the latest command used is `clear_queue`. `steer` and `agent_settled` have fallbacks: if the reply is “Unknown command”, steer is reported as unsupported, and without `agent_settled` the turn ends on `agent_end`. `get_available_models`, `--extension`, and `registerMcpServer` were not found in earlier versions and were tested only on 1.0.4. It has no approvals (`approvals: false`): pi never asks a person before running commands or changing files, and CucCut cannot control its native permissions, so it can run only under “完全访问” (full access). Other modes are rejected by the Harness with `AGENT_ACCESS_MODE_UNSUPPORTED` (§3.12). CucCut's own tools still go through ApprovalService, gated by access mode. Each session runs one `pi --mode rpc` process. The resume handle is pi's session file (`--session <file>`). pi writes this file only after its first reply; if the file is absent, a new one is created and a notice is shown. Login state is judged by whether `get_available_models` is empty, since it lists only models with usable credentials. pi creates an `auth.json` containing `{}` on every launch, so the file existing does not mean logged in. Capabilities: steer (the `steer` command, handed to the model after the current batch of tool calls), resume, and image input (when the model's `input` does not include image, the local path is attached instead). Developer instructions are passed through `--append-system-prompt`. The tool channel (§3.5) is registered through a temporary extension using `registerMcpServer` (`exposure: 'direct'`), with a notice if it cannot connect. Pi's `turn_start` / `turn_end` is one model call; one CucCut turn is one `prompt` through to `agent_settled`. Requests from the extension that need the user's answer (`extension_ui_request`) are always cancelled, with a notice. Restricted one-off calls (§6.9) are not supported.

OpenCode accepts only 2.x (1.x is reported as version too old). Each session starts one `opencode serve` that listens only on 127.0.0.1 with a random password, and is driven through its v2 HTTP API and event stream. Access modes are written as the session's permission rules, and native asks are answered by the Harness from a lookup table. MCP services and developer instructions are registered on that session. Verified on a real machine (opencode 2.0.24) with a free model (`verified` and `tested` both true). Known limitations: after one tool is rejected, OpenCode ends the whole turn (recorded as complete, with a notice); `external_directory` is treated as out of bounds; in plan mode, even reading files outside the working directory is rejected, which is stricter than Codex's read-only sandbox; 3.x is reported as outdated (version mismatch); restricted one-off calls (§6.9) are not supported.

- When steer is absent, supplementary input is queued, or runs after the user explicitly interrupts.
- While the outcome of a native cancel is unknown, a replacement Turn must not be allowed to start immediately.
- When the driver crashes, report its real state. Duplicate wake-up and completion notifications are deduplicated by event ID.

### 3.2 TaskContract

```ts
interface TaskContract {
  taskId: Id;
  revision: number;                     // starts at 1; +1 on each change
  conversationId: Id;
  videoId: Id | null;
  baseVideoRevision: Revision | null;
  goal: string;
  scope: ContextSelection;
  constraints: ConstraintRef[];
  protectedRefs: ProtectionRef[];
  deliverables: Array<{
    kind: 'preview' | 'video-change' | 'video-file' | 'subtitle' | 'audio' | 'package';
    requiredStage: 'candidate-ready' | 'committed' | 'published';
    language?: string;
    variantId?: Id;
  }>;
  autonomy: 'plan' | 'ask' | 'autoAcceptEdits' | 'auto' | 'fullAccess'; // §3.12
  permissionScopeRef: Id;               // project bound to the session; for a session not in a project, the session itself
  budgetPolicyRef: Id | null;           // task budget (§7.8)
  acceptanceChecks: CheckDefinition[];
  supersedes: { taskId: Id; previousWork: 'stop' | 'keep' } | null;
  change: { by: 'user' | 'agent' | 'runtime'; reason: 'created' | 'updated' | 'mode' | 'goal'; fields: string[]; at: string };
}

interface ContextSelection {             // what “这个” (this) and “这里” (here) refer to
  videoId: Id | null;
  videoRevision: Revision | null;       // video revision the user saw when issuing the task
  sequenceId: Id | null;                // none given means the root sequence
  itemIds: Id[];                        // selected timeline instances
  timeRange: { fromSeconds: number; toSeconds: number } | null; // output time; none means the whole video
}

interface ConstraintRef {               // natural-language constraint, passed to the agent
  constraintId: Id;
  kind: 'content' | 'style' | 'duration' | 'format' | 'language' | 'other';
  text: string;
}

interface ProtectionRef {
  protectionId: Id;
  videoId: Id;
  target:
    | { kind: 'video' }
    | { kind: 'entity'; entityId: Id }
    | { kind: 'property'; entityId: Id; propertyPaths: string[] }  // dot-separated: text, style.fontSize
    | { kind: 'interval'; sequenceId: Id; span: FrameSpan; trackIds?: Id[] };
  origin: { by: 'user'; revision: number; at: string };          // revision in which the user added it
  note?: string;
}

interface CheckDefinition {
  checkId: Id;
  kind: 'review' | 'quality';           // quality is left to QualityService (§14)
  description: string;
  required: boolean;
}
```

Goal, scope, content constraints, style and pacing, duration and aspect ratio, language and voice, delivery stage, permission budget, and “不要改动” (do not change) are organized from natural language; defaults are visible to the user.

**Creation.** The contract is created with the task (Run). `tasks.create` and `conversations.send` are the same entry point and can carry contract input. Anything not given is filled with defaults by the Runtime: the goal is the user's message; the scope comes from the editor context at send time; if a video is open, delivery is written into the video (`video-change` / `committed`); there are no constraints, protections, or checks; the budget is unlimited. The default contract is recorded with `change.by = 'runtime'` and is visible to the user through `contract` on the task entry in the session and `contractRevision` in the task center. Tasks that predate contracts get a default contract added when they are read.

**Storage and revisions.** Contracts are stored per session in the session record (`tasks[taskId]`: all revisions and acceptance check results), and are written to disk together with the session's entries. Revisions are append-only: each change produces a new `revision`. `change` records who made it, why, and which fields changed, and older revisions can be looked up by `revision`. A change must carry `expectedRevision`; if it is not the latest revision, it is rejected with `CONTRACT_REVISION_CONFLICT`. After the task ends (or while it is stopping), the contract can no longer be changed (`TASK_NOT_RUNNING`). Steps not yet started adopt new constraints: the protection scope is taken at the revision current at each commit, and the budget cap is taken at the revision current at each admission. A Job whose inputs are already frozen does not swap content while it runs.

**Access mode is `autonomy`.** The contract has no separate autonomy level: `autonomy` is the session's current access mode (§3.12). Changing the contract's `autonomy` is the same as switching the session's mode. When the session's mode is switched, the contract of any in-progress task records a revision with `reason: 'mode'`.

**Who can change what.** The user (the UI, RPC entry points other than the CLI, and non-read-only Web) can change all fields. Agents in a task read their own contract through tools (`tasks_contract`, `tasks_update_contract`, `tasks_record_check`) and may only refine scope, constraints, deliverables, and acceptance checks. Their revisions are recorded as `change.by = 'agent'` and are visible to the user. `autonomy`, budget, protection scope, and permission scope are decided only by the user; if any of them is included, the whole change is rejected with `CONTRACT_FIELD_READONLY`. Permission scope follows the project the session is bound to and is not changed through the contract. The CLI only views (`cuccut tasks contract`), Web read-only mode can only view, and the MCP service does not expose contracts (the external service has no tasks).

**Changing the goal.** `tasks.changeGoal` chooses how old work is handled. `stop` stops the old task and cancels its Jobs that are committed but not yet finished. `keep` only halts the old task's turn; Jobs already committed finish normally, and their outputs are kept as candidates. Once the old task ends, a new Run is created with the new goal. Its contract is derived from the old one: scope, constraints, protections, deliverables, checks, and budget cap are carried over, with budget usage starting from zero. `supersedes` points to the old task.

**Acceptance checks.** The contract defines checks, and results are kept in the order they were recorded (`tasks.recordCheck`, marking whether the user or the agent recorded it; recording is possible even after the task ends). The first version only defines and records; QualityService does not run them automatically (§14).

**Protection scope is domain state and applies only to this task.** `ProtectionRef` decomposes into video / entity / property / interval. It records its authorization source (with its revision), and its effective scope is this task, until removed. When the Runtime writes within the task, it hands the task contract's protections for this video, as of that moment, to VideoEngine together with the transaction. Before committing, the engine checks the transaction's entity changes against them. If any is touched, the whole transaction is rejected and nothing is written:

- `video`: any change to this video;
- `entity`: creation, modification, and deletion of this entity itself, excluding sub-entities;
- `property`: several properties on the entity (dot-separated paths in the entity JSON); deleting the entity counts as a touch;
- `interval`: a frame interval on a sequence; timeline instances that intersect it before or after the change (audio uses exact start and end times); `trackIds` restricts it to tracks.

The rejection error code is `TASK_PROTECTED` (`after-user-action`). `entityIds` are the touched entities; `details.protections` lists the touched protections (`protectionId`, `target`, and the entity).

**Writes within a task versus unrestricted writes:**

- Checked: agent-driven commits and undos within a task (undo only considers whether the actor is an agent); Job and pipeline results applied to the video under a task (the task is found through the submitter, same as the task budget in §7.8). The actor for the latter is still `system:jobs` and `system:pipeline`, and the transaction carries `taskId`; the engine checks non-user writes that carry `taskId`.
- Unrestricted: user and manual changes; Runtime writes not in a task (Jobs and pipelines submitted from the UI, CLI, Web, and external services); the user's decisions on task results (`jobs.reconcile apply`).

Protections take the latest revision of the contract. If the task no longer exists (the session was deleted), there are no protections. Applies that are re-run after a restart wait until the Harness is ready before reading the protections.

When a background write is rejected, the Job's apply is recorded as `rejected` with error code `TASK_PROTECTED` (`details.protections`). The Job fails with `APPLY_FAILED`, and `details.reason` is `TASK_PROTECTED`. The output is kept as a candidate, and the user decides whether to apply it with `jobs.reconcile apply`. A pipeline has no apply record: it stops at the step that writes the video and fails with `TASK_PROTECTED` (`details.step`); the outputs of earlier steps are kept. `pipelines.retry` stays in the original task and is likewise protected. Agents may only start imports from a link (§7.9).

QualityService compares differences in unauthorized properties. Unavoidable linked changes are first offered as a minimal change proposal. Re-rendering frames because a render cache was invalidated does not mean content may be redesigned.

The user-visible semantics are described in the product design §6.

### 3.3 One execution subject, multiple controlled steps

P0 is owned by one execution subject that the user can understand, and that subject is responsible for the result. Internal analysis and asset generation may run in parallel; video submissions are ordered per video. The plan is organized by artifact stage and does not require the user to understand the framework or multiple agents.

Capability selection prefers existing assets, native objects and currently verified models, avoiding repurchasing generation just to change a title. The first version only requires one end-to-end verified Driver; the other Drivers are integrated into the same contract step by step.

When switching execution engines, give the new engine a summary of the goal, constraints, current video state and recent receipts; do not claim that a native session can be migrated verbatim.

### 3.4 Context and check coverage

`ContextBuilder` provides: `videoRevision`, `sequenceId`, the selected item / occurrence / word IDs, the effective script, the current source time and output time, the necessary frames and the relevant constraints.

The editor context at send time (`EditorContext`: the open video and version, the selected clips, the playhead) additionally carries the user's UI language `uiLanguage` (a BCP 47 tag, optional), attached in `<cuccut-editor-context>` after the user message. For the AI tool's “direct model call”, the `uiLanguage` parameter of the `ai-tool` flow writes it into the system prompt. The skill decides the output language from it: the language the user asked in takes priority, then the UI language, and finally the language of the script.

When a message carries slide annotations (`slideAnnotations`, slide design §9), the Runtime replaces each one's deck with a path relative to the session working directory. The Harness groups them by deck, with one `<cuccut-slide-annotations>` segment per deck that lists each entry's position, anchor and comment, appended to the end of the text handed to the agent. Anchors are for the editor only and are not handed to the agent. Design annotations (`designAnnotations`) are handled the same way: the canvas becomes a relative path, there is one `<cuccut-design-annotations>` segment per design draft placed after the slide annotation segment, and the numbering of each starts from 1. Design-system annotations (`designSystemAnnotations`) follow the same pattern: one `<cuccut-design-system-annotations>` segment per design system, placed after the design annotation segment. For the format and limits, see the protocol spec `conversations`.

- Summarize and search first, then expand as needed; do not send the full video or full text out by default.
- Reading only file names does not justify claiming to understand the content. `ContextEvidence` records the range actually read and the method used.
- A check record contains `method`, `inputRefs`, `coveredRanges`, `uncoveredRanges`, `findings` and `confidence`; it is not the internal reasoning that is shown.

The agent's loop is: understand → review the material → plan → make or edit → watch and listen → correct → deliver. Automatic quality repair defaults to at most two rounds, after which the work passes to a human; this is a product default that can be changed. Paid retries are additionally limited by a separate budget. Repairs cannot cross the protected scope, and subjective style must not be automatically treated as a hard error.

### 3.5 Tool catalog

`ToolCatalog` uses domain-specific inputs and outputs and adapts four access modes: native tools, MCP, SDK and CLI. Each catalog entry writes out all of the following, and every surface is derived from it without a separate copy of the list: a title; a description whose first sentence is a one-line summary; an input schema where every field has a description and every enum value is written into the description; `effect` (`query` is read-only, `mutation` is a synchronous write, `job` returns a `jobId` immediately, `destructive` is a deletion or an irreversible action), consistent with `annotations` (`query` means `readOnlyHint`, and `destructive` must carry `destructiveHint`); one to three `examples` that pass the schema; `surfaces`, meaning which surfaces expose it (`agent` for the agent session's tool bridge, `mcp` for the outward MCP service, `cli`), written out in full without abbreviation; and an optional `positional`, for the CLI's positional arguments.

```text
videos.list / videos.create / videos.inspect / videos.history / videos.frames / videos.shots / videos.importPackage / videos.derive / videos.delete / timeline.query / speech.search / previews.capture
documents.read / documents.put / assets.import / captions.create
edits.propose / edits.prepareBatch / edits.applyPrepared / edits.apply / edits.ops / edits.undo
models.capabilities / speak / image
transcribe / translate / dub / transcode
code.build / code.publish / code.inspect
jobs.inspect / jobs.wait / jobs.list / jobs.cancel / jobs.retry / artifacts.save / downloads.save / export / download
tasks.contract / tasks.updateContract / tasks.recordCheck / tasks.stop / grants.request
models.list / models.install / models.test
projects.list / projects.create / skills.list / skills.read / library.list / library.show
space.list / space.search
```

- Edit tools generate or submit legal `EditOperation`s.
- `videos_create` creates a new empty video in the same location as a video created by the fixed flow (§7.9 “creating a video is a step of the flow”). After a successful call the session gains one `video-created` entry (§11.3). Creating a video is not an edit, so it has no change card and no undo. The optional `project` chooses which registered project to create it in (a project ID, or the path of the project directory). If the session supplies one, it must be the project the session belongs to. The outward service has no session, so it must be supplied there; when it is omitted, the result is `INVALID_ARGUMENTS` (with `next`, §4.8).
- Model tools return `jobId` immediately; querying the status does not trigger a new computation. The `provider` and `model` parameters are optional; when they are omitted, the default for that capability is used (§6.2). `models.capabilities` lists the providers, models and defaults available for each capability, so the agent can check first and then choose. `generateText` is listed there but has no tool that submits it: the agent is itself a text model, and `generateText` is only for CucCut's own flows to call in-process (§7.9).
- When a capability is not configured, model tools return `CAPABILITY_NOT_CONFIGURED` with a remedy (§6.2), and the agent explains this to the user instead of finding a way around it. The result given to the agent carries the `hint` of the `remedy`, the same set of remedy commands the CLI prints, and a `next` sentence: tell the user to enable it in the model configuration in settings or with `cuccut models configure`, and do not bypass it with shell commands, scripts or another API provider. A disabled provider also returns `CAPABILITY_NOT_CONFIGURED` (the remedy is to re-enable it). A missing voice (the model has no default voice, or does not have the voice given) is rejected at submission as `invalid-request`, with a list of voices for the model; the agent chooses from that list or asks the user for a voice ID from their account. Agent tools cannot enable or configure providers, cannot set defaults, and have no tools for reading or writing credentials. Enabling a provider is the user's ongoing authorization for data to be sent to external services (§6.8), so only the user does it.
- Generation tools (`speak`, `image`) use the same parameters as the CLI and the UI: text or a prompt, and optionally a voice, size, count and `videoId`. When text exceeds the model's single-call limit, it is rejected at submission (agent tools return `INPUT_TOO_LONG` with the length and the limit). The agent splits the text into segments and submits them itself; the Runtime does not truncate or chunk. When `videoId` is given, the result is imported as a candidate asset; placing it on the timeline is a separate edit. `jobs.inspect` returns the frozen provider, model, parameters and each output (`artifactId`, media facts, `assetId`; for transcription, `documentId`). When no video is given, the result is only an artifact (`artifactId`):
  - To place it in a video later, `importAsset` in `edits_apply` takes `artifactId` in place of `path`. The Runtime resolves the file from the artifact library and passes it to the engine's existing import-by-path; the bytes are collected into the video (`managed`, §5.1). The provenance is the same as an import made at submission time when a video was given (task, provider, model, a parameter summary and the input hash). It is not regenerated and does not call the provider again. The same bytes already in the video reuse the existing asset. Only generated media and media that was only downloaded from a link can be imported; transcription results cannot be imported as assets. Downloaded media does not enter the artifact library: `artifactId` is the content digest of that file in the download directory. The Runtime finds the file by the `path` registered for the task output, and uses it only if the size and digest both match (if it was moved, deleted or changed, the result is `ARTIFACT_NOT_FOUND`). The provenance is the same as when the flow imports it itself (`origin: 'link-import'`).
  - When the file itself is needed, `artifacts_save { artifactId, path, overwrite? }` copies the artifact to a file inside the scoped write-file directory (the session's working directory, or `cwd` for the terminal; the outward service has none). The path is checked by its real location: it cannot write outside the directory through `..`, absolute paths or symbolic links; a target that is itself a symbolic link is rejected; it cannot write into the video directory (including `video.db`) or `.ccut`; an existing file is replaced only with `overwrite`; the extension matches the artifact (it is appended when omitted). It only copies one file from the artifact library; it is not a general export.
  - Both act only on artifacts of tasks the caller can see (the same as `jobs_inspect`, see the table below). Everything else returns `ARTIFACT_NOT_FOUND`. A task that failed but kept its artifact (importing into the video failed) also counts.
- `downloads_save { path, name? }` copies a file in the session's working directory into the download directory (the same one used for importing from a link: set `downloads.directory`, otherwise the host's Downloads folder, §7.9) so it is handed to the user. A working directory that does not belong to a project is not visible to the user, so results the agent produces itself (for example a translated subtitle file) are handed over through this tool. The source is checked by its real location: it cannot point outside the working directory through `..`, absolute paths or symbolic links; it must be a regular file; it cannot be inside the video directory (including `video.db`) or `.ccut`; and it cannot exceed 200 MB. It only creates new files and never overwrites; on a name clash a sequence number is added. The file name is cleaned by the same rules as downloaded files, and the extension is kept from the source. This is a write operation and is rejected in planning mode. The directory entry's `surfaces` is only `agent`: the MCP service and the CLI have no session working directory, so neither has this tool. A file handed over is registered as a Space deliverable: when it is copied, the sha256 of its content is computed and written into the artifact record by the same path as files downloaded from links (flow name `downloads-save`, not through the Job Ledger, the output's `media` is `{ kind: 'file' }`, §5.7). The entry `id` is that digest and `file.path` is the real path in the download directory. The file link in the reply opens the entry in the files tab. A registration failure does not affect the result of the copy.
- The read and write operations on objects have several conveniences that compile into `edits_apply` operations and go through the same submission path (artifact resolution, seconds conversion, the asset path restriction of the outward service, translation alignment completion), returning the same receipt. `documents_put { video, document? | kind, name?, language?, sourceDocument?, sourceAsset?, body, revision? }` compiles into a single `putDocument` (when replacing, `kind` is taken from the original; creating a new translation requires `language` and `sourceDocument`); the receipt also carries `documentId`. `assets_import { video, path | artifactId, name?, storage?, place?: { track?: 'main' | <trackId>, at?: <seconds> | 'end' }, revision? }` compiles into `importAsset`; when `place` is given, the same transaction adds `addItem` (`main` and `end` stand for omitting `trackId` and `at`: the first unlocked track of the same kind, appended at the end); the receipt also carries `assetId` and `itemId`. `assets_prune { video, assetIds?, apply?, revision? }` lists unreferenced assets; with `apply: true` it compiles into `removeAssets` (a code package and its pre-rendered stand-in are paired). `chapters_adopt { video, asset?, document?, outline?, label?, revision?, dryRun? }` snaps the chapters that come with the asset source (or an explicit outline) to sentence starts in the transcript's paragraphs, then projects them from source time onto the timeline, compiling into a single `setChapters` that replaces all chapters; the receipt also carries the per-chapter snap result `sourceChapters` (rules in §7.9 “adopt source chapters”). All three surfaces have them ([command and protocol spec §4.2](../spec/command-protocol-spec.md#42-edit-operations)). When `revision` is not given, the latest version is used. `edits_ops { op? }` lists, by operation family, each `edits_apply` operation's description, the JSON Schema of its parameters and one example; the operation descriptions of `edits_apply` are assembled from the same table.
- `videos_history { video, limit? }` lists the edit history (20 entries by default) and the checkpoints. `videos_import_package { file, project?, name? }` opens a portable package (the same entry point as `videos.importPackage`, completing synchronously, `mutation`). Its location rules are the same as `videos_create`; it is only on the `agent` and `cli` surfaces.
- **Deriving a sub-video.** `videos_derive { video, start, end, name?, width?, height?, fit?, crop?, crops?, captions?, translations?, chapters?, hook?, reason?, notes? }` (gateway method `videos.derive`, all three surfaces, confirmed by `edit`) duplicates the parent video and cuts away everything outside the range; the cut parts can be restored in the sub-video. `start` and `end` are seconds on the timeline after editing, snapped first to frames (the start rounds down, the end rounds up, clamped to the duration; if the range is empty the result is `DERIVE_RANGE_EMPTY`). A boundary may fall in a pause between sentences: the parent's transcript words are projected onto the edited timeline through the instance that plays them. If rounding the end up would cross the start of the next word (the first word that starts after the end, when the pause between sentences is shorter than a frame and would take the first word of the next sentence), the end is rounded down instead. If rounding the start down lands before the end of the previous word, the start is rounded up instead. A change is not made if it would leave the range empty. The result's `range` is the range actually used, and the idempotency key is computed from it. The sub-video is a new video directory built next to the parent video (the same source directory, the same level). The directory name is the name (default “<parent name> · mm:ss–mm:ss”) plus `-<idempotency key>`. The canvas defaults to the parent's; `fit` and `crop` only change how the video clip is placed. When the parent video is itself a source directory there is no place to put the sub-video, and the result is `DERIVE_PARENT_UNPLACED`.
  - **Copy** (first transaction): tracks (except caption tracks), all instances (except caption instances; not shifted or trimmed, and all changed to `follow-cuts`, with locked instances copied unlocked), assets (the same file: an asset collected inside the parent is collected inside the sub-video too; a linked asset stays linked to the original file, with its provenance kept), the whole transcript and translations (including sentences and chapters), the root sequence's chapters and background, and the derivation source document (`kind: 'derivation'`, video format spec §4.6). It is rejected when there are code frames (`composition`) or linked assets that cannot be delivered through the media channel (`DERIVE_UNSUPPORTED_ITEM`).
  - **Finish** (second transaction, using the IDs created by the first): track hiding and muting; transitions (both sides are found again by track, kind and position); translations (`sourceBasis` is replaced with the sub-video's transcript and sequence; the transcript is copied whole, so the fingerprint of the edit view is unchanged. A `cuccut.translation/1` imported from an old project is first converted to `/2` against the transcript's current sentences (video format spec §5.3: sentences are matched by their shared words; identical words are kept, changed ones are marked stale, and the alignment is written at sentence level; display rewrites carry no mark). A translation that cannot be converted is not copied, and `warnings` records `translations-skipped` (with `documentId` being the translation in the parent video). This covers a translation with no language, a transcript whose sentences cannot be read, a sentence WASM that is not built, no sentence matched at all, or a body in another format); then the parent's existing set of cut points (the cut points are kept as they are, with their ranges changed to the sub-video's instances); then `addCuts` on the main asset records the cut points outside the range; and finally the parent's locked tracks are locked again. A cut point is the source range that the parent's main asset plays in `[0, S)` and `[E, duration)`, minus the source range that also plays inside the range. The source ranges behind the parent's existing cut points are not in the instance, so they are not recorded twice. Restoring all cut points outside the range returns the sub-video's timeline and script to the parent's state.
  - **Caption layer**: the parent's caption instances are not copied.
