For each copied transcript, a caption layer is rebuilt on the sub-video's edited timeline (`planCaptionLayer`, default style; `captions: false` builds none). If the parent has a translation whose caption layer exists, the matching translation in the sub-video also gets a layer, with cues aligned sentence by sentence. When the parent's original-text caption layer is displayed alongside it, the result is bilingual. If a layer cannot be built, `warnings` records `captions-skipped`.
  - **The first version only supports a timeline with a single main asset.** `addCuts` records per asset and only acts on the instances that play that asset, so cut points are recorded only on the main asset (the one with a linear positive-rate source clock that plays the most on the timeline; ties are broken by ID). Other assets and graphic or text clips move and shorten with the cut points. Those that fall entirely inside the cut range are deleted, and they cannot be restored when the cut points are restored. The result's `warnings` explain: `multiple-assets` (more than one media asset), `range-not-exact` (the number of frames outside the range that were not cut), `items-not-restorable` (the number of clips that will be deleted), `anchor-dropped`, `locks-dropped`, `animation-bindings-dropped`, and, for segmented framing, `crops-adjusted` and `animation-split` (see below). Not related to the timeline are `captions-skipped` and `translations-skipped` (see above).
  - **Segmented framing**: `crops` are crop segments by shot (`[{ from, to, left, top, right, bottom }]`; `from` and `to` use the same clock as `start` and `end`; at most 64 segments, not overlapping; `shots` from `videos_shots` can be used directly). Each segment's start and end snap to the nearest frame (if exactly between two frames, the earlier one is taken) and are cut to the aligned range. A segment that is truncated, or emptied by truncation, is dropped and reported as `crops-adjusted` (`count` is the number of segments). When, after alignment, the first segment starts later than the range's start, or the last segment ends earlier than the range's end with a gap under 0.5 seconds (the range's boundaries fell in a pause and were snapped, so the range is a few frames wider than requested), those segments are extended outward to the range's boundaries. No extra clip is generated and they are not counted in `crops-adjusted`. Larger gaps still use `crop`. On copy, the main asset's video clip (linear positive-rate source clock) is split into several parts at the frames where the framing changes, each with its own `crop`. The right part's `sourceIn` is computed exactly with rationals, so that if the two parts' crops are later made identical they can be merged back continuously. The entrance animation stays only on the first part and the exit animation only on the last; the fade-in of embedded audio stays only on the first part and the fade-out only on the last. Loop animations and volume envelopes are recalculated from the start of each part. This is reported as `animation-split` (`itemId` is the parent's clip). The parts before and after the range take the framing of the first and last segments, so when the head or tail is restored, the picture follows that segment. (If that part is actually another shot, the framing is wrong: a known limitation.) When a cut point in the middle of a clip that crosses shots is restored, two parts with different crops are not merged, and the restored part takes the crop of the left part. Where no segment covers the time, `crop` is used (or the clip's original framing is kept if there is none). Other video clips are not split; each takes the framing of the frame at the middle of its range as a whole. The sides of a transition connect to the split last and first parts. The range of the parent's cut-point set is expanded to all segments. The caption layer's range already includes all clips of the same asset. The derivation source document's `settings.crops` records the aligned segments, and `framing` records where they sit on the main asset's source clock (video format spec §4.6). The result's `framing` is the aligned segments, and `itemCount` counts the parts after splitting. `crops` takes part in the idempotency key only when it is given; without it, the key is the same as before. The engine does not need to change: preview and export both read `crop` per clip.
  - **Selection notes**: `hook` (the opening line), `reason` (why it was chosen) and `notes` (sign-style reminders, a few short lines) are written as they are into the derivation source document (leading and trailing whitespace trimmed; empty values are not written). The result page's card for the short video shows them (product design §5.10). If no `hook` is given, the UI uses the first sentence of the kept segment. Like the name, they do not take part in the idempotency key, and they are not rewritten when an existing sub-video is recognized.
  - **Idempotency**: the key is the first 12 hex digits of the sha256 of the parent video, its version, the aligned range and the settings (the name and selection notes are not counted). Recognition runs in this order: in the Space index, a sub-video derived from this parent video with the same key; a directory at the same level that ends in `-<key>` and has a database; receipts persisted in the sub-video under command IDs (`derive:<key>:apply` / `:trim` / `:captions:<n>` / `:translation-captions:<n>`), so an interrupted run resumes where it stopped. When recognized, `reused: true`.
  - Result: `video` (the same shape as `VideoCreated`), `reused`, `key`, `range`, `primaryAssetId`, `cuts` (seconds on the main asset's source clock), `itemCount`, `framing` (when `crops` is given), `derivationDocumentId`, `hook` / `reason` / `notes` (the selection notes recorded in the derivation source document, echoed as they are and omitted when absent; when recognized, it is the original document), `speech`, `translations`, `captions` (each caption layer's `documentId`, `sourceDocumentId` and `cueCount`: a caption layer is cut into cues for the whole document, and `cueCount` counts only the caption cues that overlap the source ranges played within the range and are visible in the sub-video; null when an existing layer is recognized), and `warnings`. When created in a session, one `video-created` entry is recorded (not when it is recognized).
- `videos_frames { video, at? | range + count?, format?, maxWidth? }` gets frames ([Agent surface design §4.3](../design/agent-surface/agent-surface-design.md#43-noun-groups-reading-and-writing-objects)). A moment on the timeline is mapped to a frame using the root sequence's frame rate. The topmost enabled video clip on a visible track that covers it is chosen (a visual track with a larger `order` is higher; within the same track, `paintOrder` decides). Its time mapping converts the moment to asset time, and that frame is taken from the asset (the same cache and ffmpeg as the asset library thumbnails). No compositing is done: captions, text, overlays, picture-in-picture placement and effects are not in the frame. The result's `composited: false` and `note` say so. `file` is null for moments with no video clip. At most 24 frames per call; PNG by default, with the long edge no larger than 1280. Each frame with a file carries face boxes `faces: [{x, y, w, h, score}]` (top-left corner and width and height normalized to 0–1 by that frame's width and height, sorted by score from high to low), and a top-level `detector: 'yunet' | null`. The YuNet model shipped with the app (`packages/models/assets/face-detection/`, resolved through `CUCCUT_MODEL_ASSETS_DIR`) is run by the `face-detect` process (`crates/face-detect`, pure Rust with tract, JSON lines over stdio; one process per call, with the model loaded once; the executable is found via `findBundledBinary`, and `CUCCUT_FACE_DETECT` overrides it). If the process or the model is missing or fails, `detector` is null and `faces` is an empty array, without an error. Files are written to `.cuccut-out/frames/<videoId>/at-<ms>ms.<png|jpg>` inside the scoped write-file directory (the session and terminal use `cwd`; the outward service uses the `exports/` of the video's project). Real paths must not leave that directory, and nothing enters the video directory or `.ccut`. It does not modify the video. Its risk level is `read` and it works in planning mode too; because it writes a file, its `effect` is `mutation` (idempotent).
- **Shot cuts.** `videos_shots { video, range?, threshold?, minShot? }` (gateway method `videos.shots` takes `start` and `end`, all three surfaces, query, risk `read`, usable in planning mode) finds where the picture switches within a range of the edited timeline (`start:end` in seconds, snapped to frames; the default is the whole timeline, at most 20 minutes; longer gives `SHOTS_RANGE_TOO_LONG` and an empty range gives `SHOTS_RANGE_EMPTY`). Using the same rules as `videos_frames` (the topmost enabled video clip on a visible track), the range is split into segments. Where adjacent segments change clip, or the source picture does not continue (different asset versions, different rates, or source times differing by half a frame or more), there is a `kind: 'edit'` cut (`score` is null). Going from blank to picture also counts. Each segment's linear positive-rate source range is passed to ffmpeg (`MediaAnalysis.sceneCuts`: an input-side `-ss` and `-t` decode only that segment, the picture is scaled to 160 wide, and `select='gt(scene,T)'` with `metadata=print:file=-` writes the time and scene score to stdout; after the seek, pts starts at 0 and the seek start is added back. It does not use `scdet`, which needs 4.4, so an older ffmpeg on the user's PATH also works). Frames whose score exceeds `threshold` (0–1, default 0.3) are mapped back to timeline time through the segment's time mapping and snapped to frames; they are `kind: 'scene'`. Still and reversed clips are not checked. Shots shorter than `minShot` seconds (default 0.8) are merged into the previous shot (the first one is merged into the next). The result returns `range`, `cuts [{at, score, kind}]` (`at` is the time of the first frame of the new shot, in seconds), `shots [{start, end, sampleAt}]` (`sampleAt` is the frame at the middle of the shot at the 20%, 50% and 80% points, as a decimal seconds string that can be passed directly to `at` in `videos_frames`), `threshold`, `minShot` and `detector: 'ffmpeg-scene'`. ffmpeg and waveform peak extraction share concurrency slots (because they decode the whole segment), the timeout is relaxed by duration, and results are cached in the media cache by the asset's content digest, source range and threshold. It only looks at abrupt picture changes and does not recognize people: dissolves and pans may be missed, while flashes and fast motion may be over-reported. Its purpose is “cut into short videos”, framing each shot separately.
- `documents_read` for a transcript (`speech`) additionally carries `translationBasis`: the `sourceBasis` used when writing a translation, and the sentences cut by the subtitle and translation core rules (ID, fingerprint, text, words, and `start` and `end`: seconds on the source clock, from the start of the first visible word to the end of the last visible word, kept to 6 decimal places). These are added fields, so the old way of reading is unaffected. When the agent translates itself, it fills these in as they are (video format spec §5.3), without cutting sentences or computing fingerprints itself. Finding sentence boundaries (for example, to choose a range for `videos_derive`) also uses this, so there is no need to read the whole body. `videos_derive`'s `start` and `end` are seconds on the edited timeline. When the parent plays this asset at original speed from 0 with no cut points, the two are the same; otherwise they must be converted through the timeline. Each sentence's `alignment` is written as a sentence-level alignment `{ basis: 'natural', correspondence: 'sentence', blocks: [], sourceWordIds: <that sentence's wordIds> }`; the `textHash` is filled in by the Runtime. When `putDocument` in `edits_apply` (or `documents_put`) writes `cuccut.translation/2`, the Runtime finds, before handing it to the engine, the sentence in the transcript the unit was translated from, using the unit's `sourceSentenceId` (the same rules). If `alignment` is null or lacks `sourceWordIds`, it is filled in as a sentence-level alignment; for a sentence-level alignment with no blocks, `textHash` is recalculated from the translation. Sentences that cannot be found, whose fingerprint differs from the one recorded on the unit, or whose unit has expired are left alone. When the transcript cannot be read, or the sentence WASM is not built, only the missing `textHash` on the alignment object is filled in. The receipt's `filledAlignments` is how many sentences were filled. A translation written this way gives each sentence its member words for the bilingual subtitle export and for building caption layers. Reading the transcript is for the agent to translate itself, and then pass `translateTo` (the target language, BCP 47). The Runtime registers an `agentTranslate` task record (`submitter` is this session and turn; `translation` records the transcript translated from, the target language and the number of sentences cut; there is no Worker and no queue, and `progress` is null), so the video card, the subtitle panel and the task page can show “translating”, and the result also carries `translationJob`. Within the same turn, the same transcript in the same language reuses the same record. When a translation in that language is written (`putDocument` with `kind: 'translation'` and `sourceDocument` set to this transcript), the record completes and points to the translation. A record for which nothing was written by the time the turn ends is marked interrupted; one the user stopped is marked cancelled. When translation continues in the next turn, the previous record becomes interrupted and a new one is registered. It follows the turn: cancelling this record does not stop the agent, so the UI offers no “cancel”. `translateTo` is only for transcripts; for other documents it gives `INVALID_ARGUMENTS`. It states an intent to write, so it is not registered in planning mode.
- `captions_create { video, documentId, bilingual?, layoutProfileId? }` makes a transcript or translation into a caption layer (§7.9 “caption layer”). It uses the same algorithm (`planCaptionLayer`) as the `captions` step of the fixed flow, as one undoable transaction. A transcript produces an original-text caption layer. A translation takes the timing of each original sentence's member words from the unit's sentence-level alignment, and cuts cues by display width (the same as the editor's “place on screen”, not through the Speech Worker). A unit with `alignment` null rejects the whole call with `TRANSLATION_UNALIGNED`. `bilingual` is only for translations, with the translation as the primary line and the original as the secondary; `bilingual: true` on a transcript gives `INVALID_ARGUMENTS`. `layoutProfileId` is `default` or `two-line` (default `default`); both have the same source-side cue parameters, and the value is echoed in the result. The caption layer does not record the profile yet, because the `CaptionProgram` in video format spec §5.4 is not saved to disk. When the same document already has a caption layer, it is not overwritten: a new layer is created anyway (“no overwrite”), and the result's `existing` and `note` explain this. If the asset is not on the timeline the result is `CAPTIONS_NOT_ON_TIMELINE`; if there are no displayable cues it is `CAPTIONS_EMPTY`. When `bilingual: true` is given and the timeline has no original-text caption layer for this transcript yet, the same transaction first creates the original-text caption layer for the transcript (cut and placed the same way as the transcript's `captions_create`), then creates the translation's layer. The two layers share a newly created style and are undone together. When a translation's layer is already shown (the new one is disabled and placed on top), no paired layer is created. It returns `transactionId`, `revision`, the new caption document's `documentId`, `trackId`, `itemIds`, `cueCount` and `enabled`. A translation also has `bilingual` and the paired original-text caption layer `pairedOriginal: { documentId, itemIds, created } | null` (`created` is true when it was created along with this call; when it is null, `bilingualNote` explains that only the translation was shown). If there is a version conflict before submission, it is recalculated against the new state, at most three times. All three surfaces have it: the outward service creates caption layers for videos within its scope, the same as in a session.
- The first-level verbs (Agent surface design §4.2) each start one fixed flow (§7.9) and return `jobId` immediately. Their parameters follow the flow's, rewritten in tool form: `transcribe` (transcription; the input is one of three: `video`, which may give `asset`; `file`, a local media file; or `url`, which goes through the same segment as `download`. By default it creates a new video and a caption layer; `noVideo` writes only the TXT and SRT transcripts; `noCaptions` builds no caption layer. When `video` already has a transcript, `target` chooses the destination: `'new-video'` (the default) creates a new video in the same project that links the same asset, named “<original name> · re-transcribe”, which can be given another name with `name`; `'replace'` swaps in the transcript (§6.6). `translations: 'carry' | 'discard'` applies only to `replace` (default `carry`). `acceptEdited: true` gets past the manual-edit gate (`TRANSCRIPT_EDITED`). A video with no transcript gets it written directly in either case. The tool's `target` is the flow parameter `destination`. The CLI spells them `--replace`, `--discard-translations` and `--accept-edited`; without `--replace` it creates a new video), `translate` (translation with the configured text model: `video` + `to` writes a new translation and creates a caption layer; `file` + `to` translates an SRT or VTT file; with no text model it is rejected with `CAPABILITY_NOT_CONFIGURED`, and `next` points to the path where the agent translates itself), `dub` (translation and dubbing; when `translation` is given, no text model is called), and `transcode` (file-to-file compression, merging and audio track extraction; the only input is a local file, and it is not on the MCP surface). `file`, `files` and `outDir` are resolved against the scoped file base (the session's working directory, or `cwd` for the terminal). The outward service has no local paths, so giving them is rejected with `INVALID_ARGUMENTS`. Outbound-data authorization is checked by the flow (`GRANT_REQUIRED` follows the unified `next`), and the confirmation does not grant the authorization on the user's behalf. Agents in a session prefer the first-level verbs, as the guidance states (§3.8).
- Model tools and video tools go through the same entry point (the tool bridge and the MCP service are MCP endpoints; the terminal uses `catalog.call`) and are invoked by the same principal. Most tool names are gateway methods with the dot replaced by an underscore (`jobs_inspect`, `models_capabilities`). A few common ones use short names (`speak`, `image`, `export`, `download`, plus the top-level verbs above). Parameters match the gateway methods, except that video parameters refer to videos by scope, as video tools do (see the table “Referring to a video” below); anything outside the scope is rejected. `jobs_inspect` only shows jobs within scope (see the table below); everything else returns `JOB_NOT_FOUND`. In a session, a Job submitted by an agent is recorded with `submitter: { kind: 'agent', id: <session ID>, taskId }` (§7.9). `jobs_cancel` only cancels jobs submitted by this session; a main stop also cancels all unfinished jobs of this session (§7.4). The external service only cancels jobs it submitted; the terminal can cancel any job. Submitting, cancelling and `artifacts_save` are write operations and are rejected in planning mode; viewing capabilities and jobs is read-only.
- `jobs_wait { jobId, timeoutSec? }` blocks until the job reaches a terminal state or times out, and returns the same record as `jobs_inspect`, plus `settled`. `timeoutSec` is 1 to 50 seconds (default 50); the cap stays under the 60-second request timeout common in MCP clients. A timeout is not an error (`settled: false`); if it has not settled, call it again. To wait on a job, use this rather than polling `jobs_inspect` repeatedly. `jobs_list { video?, state?, limit? }` lists summaries of the jobs you can see (newest first; steps of a fixed pipeline are folded under their parent job): in a session, the jobs `jobs_inspect` can see; for the external service, the jobs it submitted; for the terminal, all jobs. `jobs_retry { jobId }` reruns a fixed pipeline from the failed step (the gateway's `pipelines.retry`, same `jobId`, `attempt` plus one). It can only rerun pipelines it submitted that have already ended, and is confirmed per `command`.
- Registry queries (available on all three surfaces, and also usable in planning mode): `models_list` lists local model packages and their install state (local paths are not given); `projects_list` lists visible projects (in a session, only the session's own project; for the external service, the projects of videos in scope, without paths; for the terminal, all projects); `skills_list` lists each skill's id, name, one-line description and switch; read the body with `skills_read`; `library_list` / `library_show` read the user library's (§5.9) glossary, voices and brand assets; writes, imports and cloning are not in the catalog. `models_test { bundleId }` checks an installed model package (a job, confirmed per `command`); `projects_create { path, name? }` creates and registers a project in a directory (relative to the session's working directory or the terminal's cwd; inside the working directory it is confirmed per `edit`, outside it per `high`). The session does not switch to the new project because of this. These two are only in the tool bridge and the CLI. `projects_adopt_session { name? }` is only in the tool bridge: when the user explicitly asks to create a project, it gathers a session that does not belong to a project, together with its videos and files, into a new project (named after the video by default, confirmed per `edit`, §3.10); if the session already belongs to a project, that project is returned as is.
- Space tools are read-only (`space_list`, `space_search`, §5.7, §5.11). Within a session, the agent only sees entries and videos in the session's source directory; the external service only sees videos within the access policy's scope and the entries generated or exported from them (§4.8); the terminal and the Space UI see the same. Results in a session do not include absolute paths of the source directory; entries are identified by relative path and `videoId`. Search hits carry the video, document, time and segment. For exact locating, use `videos_inspect` / `documents_read` to read the current version. There is no `space_get` in the tool catalog; for entry details, filter with `space_list`.
- Candidates, receipts and check reports are all returned with stable IDs.
- Local tools call in-process interfaces directly; the agent process enters the same Runtime via the native tool bridge or MCP. The CLI is a thin client and does not start the heavy video host on every call.
- Agents in other applications use the tools in the catalog whose `surfaces` include `mcp`, via the external MCP service (§4.8). They do not belong to any session, and their permissions are decided by the service's access policy.
- Tool permissions check the calling principal, the video, the TaskContract, `runGeneration`, authorization and budget at the same time; they do not rely on the model choosing to comply.

The same catalog serves three kinds of principal. The tool implementations are identical; only the principal and scope (`ToolScope`) differ. The Runtime has only one `ToolCatalog`; tool groups are routed by `ScopeRouter` to their scope according to the principal's kind, and each access path gets its own view (`ToolCatalogView`). The third is the user themself in the terminal: the CLI (or the desktop UI) gateway connection calls by name via `catalog.list` / `catalog.call` (§4.1). The principal is `LocalPrincipal { connectionId, client, cwd, projectDir }` and the scope is `LocalScope` ([Agent capability surface design §3](../design/agent-surface/agent-surface-design.md#3-principals-and-permissions)).

| | Agent in a session (tool bridge) | Client of the external service (MCP service) | User in the terminal (`catalog.call`) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Principal and scope | `AgentPrincipal`, `AgentScope`: the session's source directory and TaskContract | `ServicePrincipal`, `ServiceScope`: the access policy's video allowlist and level | `LocalPrincipal`, `LocalScope`: everything the user can access; relative paths (materials, export targets, video directories) resolve against `cwd`, absolute paths are accepted, no directory restriction |
| Referring to a video | Path relative to the source directory, or `videoId` | `videoId` or `<projectId>/<relative path>` | Video directory (absolute, or relative to `cwd`) → `videoId` → Space entry id; a video that is not open is opened on the connection that made the call, and released when that connection closes |
| Creating a video | The session's project, or the session's working directory | `project` (a registered project) must be given; when the access policy is a video allowlist, the new video is added to the list (§4.8) | `projectDir` (given explicitly, or found by walking up from `cwd` to `.ccut/project.json` and registered when used); if neither exists, it goes into the `CLI` project under the default project directory, explained in the result's `note` |
| Actor for writes | `agent:<session>` | `external:<serviceId>` | `user_local`, shares the undo stack with the desktop UI |
| Confirmation | Waits for the user in the session, according to access mode and risk | Under `ask`, generates a service approval | Does not go through CucCut's approval; confirmation is handled by the agent host. Outbound sends not covered by a grant are rejected with `GRANT_REQUIRED`, and no grant is issued on the user's behalf |
| Job submitter | `{ kind: 'agent', id, taskId }` | `{ kind: 'service', id, clientId }` | `{ kind: 'connection', id: <connection> }`, same as jobs submitted from a desktop connection |
| Jobs and artifacts visible | Those submitted in this session, and those on open videos in the source directory | Those it submitted | All (same as the job center); all cancellable |

### 3.6 Capability snapshot and contract fingerprint

The Runtime provides a `CapabilitySnapshot` filtered by principal and video:

```ts
interface CapabilitySnapshot {
  contractHash: string;             // identifies the contract shape; not an authorization credential, and does not prove runtime health
  generatedAt: string;
  driverVersions: Record<string, string>;
  adapterVersions: Record<string, string>;
  entries: Array<{
    capability: string;
    level: 'declared' | 'verified' | 'effective';
    parametersSchemaRef?: string;
    limits?: JsonValue;             // batch size, context budget, check sample cap, etc.
    verifiedEnvironment?: string;
    unavailableReason?: 'not-configured' | 'not-installed' | 'not-connected' | 'no-permission' | 'unsupported' | 'resource';
    requiredPermissions?: string[];
  }>;
}
```

Capabilities have three levels:

| Level | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Declared | The implementer claims support |
| Verified | Passed testing in a specified environment |
| Effective | Installation, resources, policy and authorization all allow it right now |

Model capability entries come from `models.capabilities` (§6.8): one entry per capability, with the default Provider and model in `limits`.

Tool definitions, UI controls and CLI help are derived from the same registry, but the three need not expose exactly the same set of features. The snapshot is updated after a new Driver or Adapter is published, or after configuration is reloaded. A call using an expired contract returns an explicit contract or capability-change error, and does not silently change how parameters are interpreted.

**CLI derivation**. The `cuccut` tool commands are not hand-written: the command tree, flags, `help` and `spec` are all derived from the tools in the catalog (§3.5) whose `surfaces` include `cli` ([Agent capability surface design §5](../design/agent-surface/agent-surface-design.md#5-cli-conventions)). A tool name `<noun>_<verb>` is `cuccut <noun> <verb>` (multi-word verbs `_` → `-`); a name with no underscore is a top-level verb. Input schema fields become flags (`camelCase` → `--kebab-case`; booleans `--x` / `--no-x`; arrays repeatable; objects and arrays also accept JSON, `@file`, or `-`). A catalog entry's `positional` is the only positional argument; `effect: 'destructive'` requires `--yes`, and `job` waits for a terminal state by default. When a Runtime is running, the commands read its `catalog.list` (same version as it); `help` and `spec` do not start a Runtime for this. When none is running they use an offline snapshot, and a mistyped command also reports `UNKNOWN_COMMAND` per the snapshot, without starting one. The snapshot is generated at build time, in-process, by `tools/catalog-snapshot.ts`: it uses `ToolCatalog(allToolSets())` (with empty dependencies, reading only schemas and catalog entries), lists by the `cli` view, has the same shape as `catalog.list`, and additionally carries the operation catalog of `edits_ops`. It is written to `apps/cli/src/generated/catalog.json` (a generated artifact, not checked in). When the snapshot and the Runtime's interface version (`interfaceVersion`) differ, the Runtime wins. Real calls always go through `catalog.call` and never interpret arguments by the snapshot. Local management commands for people (credentials, defaults, settings, the external service, etc.) are not in the catalog; they are a hand-written admin bucket in the CLI (`apps/cli/src/admin/`). A call using an expired contract returns an explicit contract or capability-change error, and does not silently change how arguments are interpreted.

### 3.7 Read by scope, edit in batches by intent

**Reading**. `videos.inspect`, `timeline.query` and `speech.search` support field selection, time ranges, object types, pagination cursors and byte budgets. By default they return segments relevant to the current task, with stable IDs; reading the full text is an explicit call. Responses state the coverage, whether they were truncated, the version, and the cursor for continued reading. Data truncated by the budget must not be treated as the complete video.

**Tool surface**. For agents, keep a small set of stable domain tools. Complexity goes into schema-defined operation families and typed artifacts, rather than adding a set of near-synonym tools for each visual style.

**Batching**. Adjacent low-risk edits may be combined into one `EditBatch`, pre-checked, then submitted once (command and protocol spec §7). Long-running model computation and cross-video writes must not be packed into one long transaction. Batch size, context budget and check sample cap are returned as versioned limits, and are configured after testing.

### 3.8 Layered, progressively loaded authoring guidance

`GuidanceRegistry` is versioned and separates capability guidance from the implementation of business tools:

| Layer | Content | When loaded |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Entry | Task routing and reading rules | Every task |
| Workflow | How to handle the current production stage | When entering that stage |
| Capability guide | How to use a type of material or technical capability | When that capability is needed |
| Tool manual | Exact call contract | Before calling that tool |

`ContextBuilder` records the IDs, versions, content hashes and purposes of the entries actually loaded.

- Changing one word in an existing video does not load the full video production workflow.
- When a voiceover is provided, do not automatically switch to speech synthesis.
- Do not re-ask about aspect ratio, sound or style that is already clear.
- Build evidence first from the existing transcript and a few relevant frames; expand to video or audio analysis only when that is not enough.
- When comparing versions of different lengths, align by narrative anchors, not by equal seconds.

README files, comments or user materials in external code packages are not trusted guidance entries; reading them does not expand permissions. When a model is unavailable, keep only the paths that can actually be executed, and do not use guidance text in place of execution facts.

**Agent skill**. A skill is a folder that teaches the built-in Agent how to do something in a given way: its root holds a `SKILL.md` (front matter with `name` and `description`, optional `version`; the body is the method), and it may include reference files. Types and limits are in `skill.ts` in `@cuccut/protocol`; the method is `skills` in command and protocol spec §4.1.

- **Two-layer directory**. The built-in layer ships with the app (`skills/cuccut/built-in-skills/` below the manual: in packaged resources and at the repo root it is at this same relative location; the environment variable `CUCCUT_SKILLS_DIR` can specify it, and after packaging the desktop app tells the Runtime about it via the main process; for the index of existing built-in skills and how to add new ones, see [`skills/cuccut/built-in-skills/README.md`](../../skills/cuccut/built-in-skills/README.md)). The user layer is `<Runtime Home>/skills/<id>/`, written by adding and importing (§12.9). The directory is re-read on every call and not cached.
- **ID and skipping**. The id is derived from the folder name: Unicode NFC, lowercase, consecutive non-alphanumeric characters replaced by a single `-`, at most 64 characters, not limited to Latin letters. Non-compliant folders (missing `SKILL.md`, missing front matter fields, containing symbolic links or special files, or exceeding limits) are skipped, with the reason given in `diagnostics`. Within one layer, all skills with the same id are skipped. When the user layer and the built-in layer share an id, the built-in one takes effect. Symbolic links are not followed: if the folder itself or any entry inside it is a symbolic link, the whole folder is skipped. Files and directories starting with a dot do not count as skill content.
- **Source and switches**. The source is `builtin` (built-in), `personal` (added from a local folder), or `third-party` (imported from GitHub); the source is recorded in `.cuccut-skill.json` in the skill folder. Default switches: built-in and personal are on; third-party are off until reviewed. A switch stores only the difference from the default, in `store/skill-prefs.json` under the Runtime Home; when a skill is removed, its switch is forgotten too.
- **Handed to the agent**. Each time the harness creates a native session (a new one, and recovery after the session was idle and collapsed, the Runtime restarted, or the execution engine was switched), it appends the skill index (id, name and a truncated description) after the developer guidance: first the manual page from the section “Manual for external agents” below, then the enabled skills. The body is not in the index. The agent uses the read-only tool `skills_read { id, path? }` to fetch `SKILL.md` or a text file in the same directory; risk level `read`; usable in planning mode too. The external MCP service also provides this read-only tool (§4.8). Skills the user selects on a message (`skills` of `conversations.send`, one or several, in the order attached; the single `skill` is the older form; enabled and disabled both work) are each wrapped by the Runtime with a body and file list, appended in order after the text the message gives the agent (after the template section), and the session records the skill markers in the same order. Restricted one-shot calls (the agent acting as a Provider, §6.9) do not get skills appended.
- **Effective time**. Switches, additions and removals change the index from the next native session creation onward. Native sessions already running keep the index they started with, and their guidance is not rewritten midway. Selecting and `skills_read` always follow the current directory: a removed skill returns `SKILL_NOT_FOUND`; a disabled skill can still be selected and read.
- **Trust boundary**. A skill is guidance, not authorization. User-layer skills come from folders the user added or imported, and are treated by the rule in the previous paragraph of this section; reading one does not expand permissions, and in a conflict with access mode, approval and CucCut's rules, the rules win. The index and selection sections state this for user-layer skills. CucCut never executes any file in a skill.

**Manual for external agents**. The single source for “how to use CucCut” (startup, capability catalog, hard rules, delivery) is `skills/cuccut/` at the repo root (`SKILL.md` + `references/`; the `built-in-skills/` in the same directory is the built-in layer above and does not count as a manual page, so it is skipped when rendering). Within this, `references/craft/<id>.md` is synthesized from `built-in-skills/<id>/` by `tools/sync-agent-skill.ts`; generated blocks are filled from the tool catalog and the CLI exit-code table; `build:agent-skill` (`--check` to verify). `packages/runtime-core/src/skills/agent-skill-renderer.ts` renders the same body into two surfaces (markup notation in [Agent capability surface design §8.6](../design/agent-surface/agent-surface-design.md#86-one-manual-two-readers)). The CLI surface is passed via `catalog.agentSkill` (command and protocol spec §4.1) to `cuccut skill install`, which writes it into the external Agent host's skills directory. The tool-bridge surface is for the agent in a session: the Runtime renders it once at startup from the tool bridge's catalog (`agent-tools/guidance.ts`), and the body of `SKILL.md` is the developer guidance for each native session. `references/catalog/*.md`, `workflows.md` and `conventions.md` become built-in manual pages (`cuccut-catalog-<page>`, `cuccut-workflows`, `cuccut-conventions`, with descriptions taken from each page's front matter `description`), given only to the agent in a session: they are always in the session's skill index and readable via `skills_read`. The terminal and MCP service get `SKILL_NOT_FOUND` when reading them; there, use the installed CLI surface. They are not in `skills.list` and cannot be switched. If the built-in or user layer uses the same id, that one is not loaded and `builtin-conflict` is recorded. If the manual directory cannot be found or rendering fails (unclosed markers, tool names the tool-bridge surface does not have, etc.), the Runtime fails to start rather than starting a session with empty or stale guidance. The lookup order is the same as for the built-in layer: `CUCCUT_AGENT_SKILLS_DIR` → `skills/` in packaged resources → `skills/` at the repo root.

### 3.9 Video memory and next-turn context

`VideoMemory` holds the confirmed direction, terminology, reference drafts, voice choices and protection scope, along with the stable object references they point to.

- Video facts take precedence over the old chat.
- Memory can be viewed and deleted, and can be overridden per task.
- Reusing a brand, voice or terminology across videos requires an explicit choice.

### 3.10 Binding sessions to projects

`ConversationStore` keeps, in the Runtime Store, the sessions, messages, the events needed for the card projection, and the binding of sessions to projects. A session is one independent agent task: it may belong to a project (a directory) or to none. A session does not bind or lock any one video: one session may create several videos, may modify any video under its project, or may touch no video at all.

- Between a session and videos there is only a factual record: which videos a session's task created or modified is derived from the task's receipts, and is used to jump between the session header and Space. This record is not write permission and does not restrict what the session later does to other videos; each write's target is given by that tool call, and permissions are checked against the TaskContract (§3.5).
- A video can be modified by multiple sessions. When starting a conversation from a video, by default a new session belonging to that project is created and the video is carried in as a reference; continuing an existing session is the user's choice.

**Persistent form**: each session is one append-only log, `store/conversations/<id>.jsonl`. The first line is the file header `{"op":"header","formatVersion":2}`, the second line is a `snapshot` (full record: `conversation`, `items`, `seq`, `agent`, `tasks`), and after that lines are replayed in order: an `item` line replaces in place by `item.id`, and unseen ones are appended to the end (streaming replies and tool-call state updates are both replacements); a `meta` line replaces the given `conversation` / `seq` / `agent` as a whole, and `tasks` is replaced as a whole by taskId. Memory is primary; when the 150 ms coalescing window expires, only changed lines are appended, with no fsync. A new session, an item deleted or reordered, a task deleted, and a log exceeding its threshold (line count above 4 × (items + tasks) + 64, or bytes above 4 × record size + 256 KB) all trigger an atomic replacement with "header + one snapshot line" (compaction); appends and compaction are on the same serial write chain for the session. Reading: a trailing partial line is skipped, is not recorded as a disk copy this time, and the next save writes the whole thing as a snapshot; unrecognised lines in the middle are skipped and logged; if the header or snapshot is unrecognised, the file is renamed `.corrupt-<time>` and preserved, and skipped; an unrecognised `formatVersion` / `schemaVersion` is left as is and skipped. The old whole-file `<id>.json` is imported as a snapshot at startup and renamed `.json.migrated`.

- `Conversation.projectId` may be empty. It can be bound once from empty (on the next message), and afterward is not rebound to another project. When the project directory is moved or renamed, the session stays under the project's identity, and the working directory switches to the new path; a copied directory is a different project and does not take the session with it (§5.1).
- A session without a project works in its own working directory `<session dir>/<session id>/`. The session directory (`RuntimeHome.scratchDir`) lives in the OS application data directory: on macOS `~/Library/Application Support/CucCut/Sessions`, on Windows `%LOCALAPPDATA%\CucCut\Sessions` (not synced with roaming profiles, since it holds media), elsewhere `${XDG_DATA_HOME:-~/.local/share}/CucCut/Sessions`; when the Runtime Home is not the default `~/.cuccut` (development, testing) it is `<runtime-home>/scratch/`, and the environment variable `CUCCUT_SESSIONS_DIR` can override it. Creating a new video does not create a project or bind one: the agent's `videos_create`, the `create` target of link import and transcription, flows, and `videos.create` / `videos.importPackage` given a session all build the video inside the session's working directory, where it stays (Space lists it by session), scoped to that session.
- Sessions sharing a working directory: when opening a new session from a video that does not belong to a project (AI tool handed to Agent, the floating session beside the video, product design §5.10), the new session uses the working directory of the session that the video belongs to (`conversations.create { folderOf }`), and the `folderOf` on the record is the id of that session; reopening from a shared session still shares the original one, and if that session belongs to a project the new session is created directly in the project. Its scope is recorded as that session: the video and file locations are written as `{ conversationId: <that session> }`, Space does not list a separate source for it, and the agent can open and derive videos there. When moved into a project, what is moved is that session; sessions sharing it are bound together (`projectId` and `cwd` switch along, `folderOf` is removed, and the cards point to the project instead). Whichever session's agent has a video open, it is rejected on the next turn; deleting a shared session does not touch the working directory; deleting the one being shared first moves it into a project (same as when the working directory has videos), without losing the directory. At startup, shared sessions follow that session: one already belonging to a project moves into the project along with it; when it is gone (deleted before the sessions sharing it could be changed), the earliest-created one (ties by id) takes over the directory (renamed to its own working directory), and the others come to share it.
- Only two cases bind a session without a project to a project: when the user explicitly asks to create a project, the agent calls `projects_adopt_session { name? }` (only in the tool bridge, confirmed by `edit`); and before deleting a session that still has videos in its working directory (see the last item below). `Harness.ensureConversationProject` creates and registers a project under the default project directory; the name is the given `name`; if none is given, it takes the name of the earliest-created video in the working directory, or the session title if there are no videos, and otherwise the default name; then it binds the session to it (`projectId` changes from empty to that project, `cwd` switches to the project directory, persisted to disk), and moves what already exists in the working directory into the project as-is by relative path (video cards in the session that pointed to the old directory are changed to point to the project). The project registration and the session binding are records in the Runtime Store. The idempotency key is the intent file `.cuccut-binding.json` in the working directory: it is written after the project directory is chosen and before the directory is created; if a crash occurs and a retry finds it, the same directory is reused and no second project is created; concurrent calls for the same session are merged into one pass. When binding during a turn in progress, the old path is first replaced by a link pointing to the project directory (a junction on Windows); after the turn ends the link is deleted, what was newly written in the turn is moved over, and the native session is closed, so the next turn starts in the project directory (native CLIs store sessions by directory, so context is lost, the same as moving a project). Before binding, videos in the working directory that no one else uses and that are only waiting in the grace period are closed (when called by the agent, the ones it has open itself are also released first; this and the idle native sessions in sessions sharing that working directory are closed first, and videos open in their agents are released along with them); videos still held open by the editor, a task or another connection must not be moved (the engine reads and writes by the original path, and renaming fails on Windows), and in that case binding is refused (`conflict`, `VIDEO_IN_USE`, with the names of the open video directories), without creating the project, changing the session, or moving anything: binding first and leaving it would place it outside both the session's working directory and the project, where Space cannot find it and the editor cannot open it. A video directory opened after the check but during the move (a race) is left in place, retried every 30 seconds, and moved once it is closed (if a turn is still using the old directory, wait for the turn to end).
- At startup (before the client connects), session directories are tidied: `scratch/` in the Runtime Home from the old version (when it is not the same place as the session directory) is moved over first; the whole working directory of a session without a project is moved into the session directory, with `cwd` switching along; leftover links or directories from sessions already belonging to a project are cleared, and the remaining contents are moved into the project; for directories with no corresponding session, those containing videos have their non-hidden entries moved into a project named "Recovered videos" (named by the UI language; an existing one with the same name is reused), then the directory is deleted; those without videos are simply deleted. The working directory of a session without a project is left alone. An error in one directory does not affect others; it is logged and retried next time.
- When a new session continues an old project, context comes from the current state of its videos and `VideoMemory`, not from other sessions' chat history.
- Deleting a session deletes only the session record in the Runtime Store and its working directory under the session directory; if the working directory still has videos, first a project named after the video is created and they are moved in, per the rules above, before deletion, so videos do not disappear with the session; if any video in it is still open (the session's turn is stopped first, and the agent's open ones are released first), deletion is refused (`VIDEO_IN_USE`), and the session record and working directory are left as they are. It does not delete projects, videos, Artifacts or committed transactions; records still referenced by tasks or authorization audits are handled under the retention policy.

### 3.11 Driver discovery and health

The Driver ABI (§3.1) describes an agent runtime that is already usable. Whether it is usable is given by `DriverRegistry`, which probes each registered Driver:

```ts
interface DriverStatus {
  driverId: string;
  state: 'ready' | 'not-installed' | 'outdated' | 'signed-out' | 'error' | 'disabled';
  version?: string;
  minVersion: string;                 // below this, not opened; reason: machine protocol incompatible
  latestVersion?: string;             // hint only; a newer version does not affect use
  executable?: string;                // path found by probing; the user can specify it
  account?: { plan?: string };        // only the summary the Driver itself reports; no credentials
  models: Array<{ id: string; label: string; efforts?: string[] }>;
  detail?: string;                    // reason when state is not ready
  verified: boolean;                  // can start sessions (D08); a fact about this Driver version, not changing with local installation or login
  tested: boolean;                    // this Driver itself has completed a full real-device session in CucCut
}
```

- **Probing is read-only.** Probing runs the Driver's own version and status commands, reads its machine protocol, does not read its credential files, and does not parse terminal screens. For an ACP Driver, a temporary process is started to do `initialize` and `session/new` (no tools connected, no models called), and the model list is read from the reply; when `session/new` answers "login required" (-32000), it is recorded as `signed-out`. Claude Code's model list requires starting a Query that sends no message and asks `supportedModels()`, which is far heavier than the two short commands for version and login status: the Driver remembers it in memory keyed by the executable file, version and the modification time of `settings.json`, with no expiry; the same claude is asked only once per Runtime; after an upgrade or a settings change, the next probe asks again; if the query fails, it falls back to the built-in trimmed table, and the failure is not recorded.
- **Results are cached per Driver, with no expiry.** The local facts from each Driver's most recent probe (state, version, executable and its real location, account, model list, models in the config, latest version, problem description, probe time, and the user-specified executable used in the probe) are stored in `store/agent-probes.json` under the Runtime Home; the name, command, minimum version, install method, `verified`, `tested`, and capabilities of this Driver version are constants not stored, and are filled in by the Driver when read back (for ACP, capabilities come from the `initialize` reply, so when read back they start as conservative values and are replaced by real ones once the background probe finishes). At Runtime startup, the last result is used first, and at the same time each Driver is re-probed in the background. Each Driver is probed independently and takes effect on its own: when one finishes, it is written to the cache and saved to disk, and a new view is pushed on the `agents` topic; a slow one (cold start of an ACP agent can take more than ten seconds) does not hold back fast ones; a probe failure is recorded as that Driver's `error` and does not affect others; during a refresh the old result continues to be displayed. When a cache file is corrupted, a Driver is not registered, or an entry's executable does not match the current settings, that part of the cache is discarded.
- **When to re-probe.** Only at these times, with no periodic refresh: Runtime startup (a background round); `agents.detect` ("Re-detect", forced, not using the Driver's own model or login cache); when the user changes a Driver's executable (only that one is probed, and the old result is dropped immediately); when an in-app install or upgrade command finishes (only that one is probed, §12.9); when a runtime error shows that the cache is stale — a session start or turn fails with `AGENT_AUTH_REQUIRED`, `AGENT_NOT_INSTALLED`, `AGENT_OUTDATED`, `AGENT_MODEL_UNAVAILABLE`, or a native session fails to start (`driver-unavailable`) — and when the cache says unavailable but a turn ran successfully: the Harness forces a probe of that Driver in the background, and does not rewrite the status in the cache directly; the probe is the only source of truth. There is one more cheap correction: during `agents.list` and `conversations.create`, a Driver whose result is not `ready` and was last probed more than 60 seconds ago is probed again in the background (probes that would launch the agent process are not done this way: ACP, Pi, OpenCode, which declare `slowProbe`); the desktop UI requests `agents.list` once when the window regains focus, so that after a user logs in or installs in the terminal and comes back, this triggers it. Concurrent probes arriving for the same Driver share one run; a forced probe replaces an in-progress ordinary probe, and the replaced result is discarded.
- **`agents.list` does not wait for slow probes.** A Driver with a result returns its cache immediately; a Driver with no result yet (first startup, just changed executable) is waited on if its probe is fast (first start of Claude Code and Codex, about 1 second), while those that must launch the agent process (`slowProbe`: ACP, Pi, OpenCode) are not waited on and are listed in `AgentsView.checking`, pushed via the `agents` topic when done. `agents.detect { driverId? }` forces a probe (only that one if `driverId` is given), returns only after the probe finishes, and pushes once more before returning. Session creation validates model and effort against the cache without waiting for a probe (if there is no result yet, no validation); when attaching an image, judging whether the Agent supports it likewise reads the cache, and waits for the probe only when a Driver that has passed integration tests has no result at all.
- **Push.** The snapshot of the `agents` topic is the `agents.list` view; each completed Driver probe, and any change to Agent preferences (enable, default model and effort, executable, permission policy, additions and removals of "always allow" rules, the default agent `agent.defaultDriver` in preferences), pushes a whole new view (`agents.updated`), which the client replaces wholesale.
- **Login happens in the Driver's own interface.** CucCut does not receive or store agent runtime accounts and passwords; when not logged in it only offers ways to log in. Authentication failures and unavailable models during a run are structured errors (`AGENT_AUTH_REQUIRED`, `AGENT_MODEL_UNAVAILABLE`); the session is kept, and it can continue after the fix.
- **Install and upgrade** are managed install actions in §12.9, not the Driver's responsibility.
- **Defaults.** The default Driver, and each Driver's default model and reasoning effort, belong to preferences (§5.10); a session freezes its own choice at creation, and later changes to defaults do not affect existing sessions. Model and reasoning effort are passed to the Driver via `RunContext`; values the Driver does not support are rejected at session creation.
- **Default model and recommended model** (user decision of 2026-09-29). When a new session does not specify a model, the order is: the default model set in this Driver's Agent preferences, the default Driver's preference `agent.defaultModel`, then the recommended model. The recommended model is taken by the order of this CLI version's own model list (`recommendedDriverModel`): for Claude Code, the first id containing `sonnet`; for Codex, the first id ending with `-sol` (hidden models are not in the list); for the others (and when that family is absent from the list), the first marked `balanced`, else the Agent's marked default, else the first; when the model list is not yet available no model is passed. This way the default does not fall to the most expensive models such as Opus or Fable based on CLI config. Only when the user explicitly chooses "Agent default model" is no model passed and the CLI config is followed: on the session it is `model: null` (`conversations.create` explicitly given `model: null`), in preferences it is `agents.configure { defaultModel: null }`, stored as `AGENT_DEFAULT_MODEL` (`__agent-default__`, which the preference `agent.defaultModel` also recognises); a null in storage means never set, and a null in an old file is also treated as never set, using the recommended model. If a set model is not in the current model list, a new session uses the recommended model instead. `DriverInfo.defaultModel` is the model a new session starts with: a set one is given as-is (even if not in the list, shown in the interface as no longer in the model list); if never set, it is the recommended model; if "Agent default model" was explicitly chosen, it is null.

- **User-added agents.** `agents.addProvider { id, name, command, env? }` adds an ACP agent to the registry: `command` is the program and arguments that start it in ACP mode (not run through a shell), and `env` holds extra environment variables. The id cannot be a built-in id, and cannot duplicate an existing one (`conflict`, `AGENT_PROVIDER_EXISTS`). The list is stored in `store/agent-providers.json` under the Runtime Home (`{ schemaVersion, providers: [{ id, name, command, env?, addedAt }] }`, permissions 0600, serialised atomic writes; if the file is corrupted or an entry is invalid, it is treated as absent); at Runtime startup they are registered first, then the probe cache is read. Once added, it is probed immediately in the background. `agents.removeProvider { id }` removes only user-added ones (built-in ones are rejected with `invalid-request`, `AGENT_PROVIDER_BUILTIN`): in-progress tasks using it end in failure, native sessions are closed, and its Agent preferences, probe cache, and any `agent.defaultDriver` pointing to it are all cleared together; afterward, sends to those sessions are rejected with `driver-unavailable`, while the sessions themselves are kept. Both methods return a new `AgentsView` and push it. Each Driver in the view carries a `source` (`builtin` or `custom`); user-added ones also carry `custom` (command, names of environment variables, and the time added), and environment variable values do not leave the Runtime. These two methods are not exposed to the browser or external services. They are separate from `agents.configure` because the latter changes only the preferences of registered Drivers, whereas these change the registry itself and the commands that can be executed.

The `agents` namespace provides `list`, `detect`, `setDefault`, `configure`, `addProvider`, `removeProvider` for this; `setDefault` accepts only registered Drivers. The openness of multiple Drivers still follows D08: a Driver not passing integration tests (`verified: false`) only shows probe results and cannot be used to start a session; on send, the Harness rejects with `driver-unavailable`. When one agent on a shared protocol implementation (such as ACP) has completed a full session on a real device, that implementation counts as verified (see §3.1 for ACP's verification): its other presets and user-added agents can all start sessions; those not personally run through with their own agent (`tested: false`) are labelled "not yet tested in CucCut" in the interface and are not blocked.

### 3.12 Access modes

An access mode is the permission setting of the Agent Harness. It decides when an agent's actions need the user's confirmation. Each session has its own access mode, which can be switched at any time and applies to actions from then on:

| Mode | Name | Behaviour |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `ask` | Ask each time (supervised) | Asks for permission before running a command or modifying a file or video |
| `autoAcceptEdits` | Auto-accept edits | Changes to files and videos inside the working directory are approved automatically; other actions ask first |
| `auto` | Auto (default) | Routine actions are approved automatically; high-risk actions still ask |
| `fullAccess` | Full access | No per-action confirmation |

`plan` (plan first) is a separate tier: read-only, and every modification is refused (the tool returns `PLAN_ONLY`). The agent explains what it intends to change and waits for the user to switch modes. It shares the same field and the same decision table as the four tiers above.

**A Driver with no per-action approval channel can only use `fullAccess`**. A Driver whose `capabilities.approvals` is `false` in the probe result (or in the capabilities the Driver declares when the probe result is unavailable) never asks, so CucCut has no way to enforce any other mode. When a session is created or switched, an explicitly requested mode other than `fullAccess` is refused with `conflict`, and so is an effective mode at send time (explicit, the session's, or the one from settings) that is not `fullAccess`. The `details` carry `code: 'AGENT_ACCESS_MODE_UNSUPPORTED'`, `driverId`, `mode`, and `supportedModes: ['fullAccess']`. No task is created, and the user's mode is not changed on their behalf.

**Risk levels**. Every action waiting for approval first gets a risk level:

| Level | Meaning | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `read` | Read-only | Query tools; no approval needed |
| `edit` | Reversible change; the result stays in the working directory or the video | Editing and undoing edits to a video, creating a video, exporting, saving an output as a new file, generation by a local model, file changes inside the working directory |
| `command` | Runs a command, or sends data to a service that is already authorised | A Driver command, cancelling a job, generation sent to an enabled online or node Provider |
| `high` | High risk | Going outside the sandbox or working directory, irreversible overwrites, outbound data transfer that needs new authorisation, and paid calls (§12.5) |

**Decision table**. Mode × risk gives auto-allow, ask, or refuse. `read` runs directly in every mode:

| Mode | `edit` | `command` | `high` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `plan` | Refuse | Ask | Refuse |
| `ask` | Ask | Ask | Ask |
| `autoAcceptEdits` | Allow | Ask | Ask |
| `auto` | Allow | Allow | Ask |
| `fullAccess` | Allow | Allow | Allow |

Under `plan`, commands still ask instead of being refused. The Driver needs to run inspection commands even in a read-only sandbox, and the user decides each one.

**Risk of CucCut tools** (§3.5):

| Tool | Risk |
| --- | --- |
| `edits_apply`, `edits_undo`, `captions_create`, `videos_create`, `export` | `edit`; `export` is `high` when it overwrites an existing file |
| `artifacts_save` | Saving as a new file is `edit`; overwriting an existing file is `high`. The path is checked first, and an invalid path is refused directly without creating an approval |
| `speak`, `image` | A local model is `edit` (the result is a candidate asset or output that does not change the timeline and can be deleted). Handing off to a paired node, or to an online or agent Provider whose Grant covers this outbound transfer, is `command`. An enabled Provider with no Grant covering the transfer (no Grant, revoked or expired, or a cost cap that cannot be estimated) is `high`, and the approval carries the data to be authorised (§12.5). If the Grant covering it has run out of quota, the call is refused with `BUDGET_EXCEEDED` before approval, in every mode. A Provider that is not enabled or cannot be resolved is `command`, and is refused at submission with `CAPABILITY_NOT_CONFIGURED`; no data is sent out |
| `grants_request` | `high`. Requests, in one approval, the several outbound transfers the task needs (each one a capability, Provider, video, count, and purpose) (§12.5). Its `surfaces` is only `agent`; the MCP server and the CLI do not have it |
| `jobs_cancel` | `command` |
| `downloads_save` | `command`. It writes a new file outside the working directory, so it still asks under `autoAcceptEdits`. But it only writes into the download directory and never overwrites (duplicate names get a number suffix). It is on the same level as `download`, which puts newly downloaded files into the download directory. It is not `high`, because it is not an out-of-bounds write or an irreversible overwrite. The path is checked first, and an invalid path is refused directly without creating an approval |
| `tasks_contract`, `tasks_update_contract`, `tasks_record_check` | No confirmation; also available under `plan`. They only read and write this task's own contract and check results, do not touch the video, and send nothing out. Changes are recorded as the agent's revisions and are visible to the user. Fields that cannot be changed are refused with `CONTRACT_FIELD_READONLY` (§3.2). Only for the agent in the session; not in MCP |
| (`jobs.reconcile`) | Not a tool. Rated `high` and decided only by the user (§7.5): `retry` is a new outbound call (a new reservation, which may be billed again), `discard` gives up a result that may already have been billed, and `apply` writes the user's video. The agent can see `needs-reconciliation`, `applications`, and `cancellation` in `jobs_inspect`, and `next` tells it to inform the user |
| `transcribe`, `translate`, `dub`, `transcode` | `command`: starts a fixed pipeline (§7.9). The pipeline checks the outbound authorisation. Without a covering Grant it is refused with `GRANT_REQUIRED`, and the confirmation does not grant authorisation on the user's behalf. When `transcribe` is given a `url`, it is confirmed as `download` |
| `download` | Downloading with the yt-dlp the user has already consented to is `command`. It runs a tool the user has agreed to, does not send out video or transcripts, and its result is a new file in the download directory. Downloads for a `project` and downloads served by the external service go into the owning project's `downloads/`. `newVideo` does not raise the risk, because creating a new video is `edit`. If yt-dlp is not yet installed, the action becomes an approval for `external_tools_install` (`high`: downloads and runs third-party software, and the approval summary carries the source, version, size, and licence). If consent is missing or was withdrawn, the approval is `external_tools_consent` (`high`). If the link is not allowed, strict offline mode is on, or the tool cannot download, it is refused directly without creating an approval (§7.9, §12.9) |
| Other query tools | `read` |

"Is it authorised" is determined by `GrantService.plan` (§12.5). It matches Grants by the kind of data sent out in this call, the recipient, the video, and the task, and checks the quota, without reserving anything. Local models and paired nodes need no Grant. When an online Provider is enabled, a default ongoing Grant is issued (the migration rule in §6.8), so the risk of calls that were previously “usable once enabled” does not change. After the user revokes that Grant, these calls return to `high`.

**Approvals that send data out**. A `high` approval for a model tool carries `grants` (`GrantRequestItem`: capability, data kind, recipient, video, purpose, why authorisation is needed, and the cost status and estimate). When `approvals.respond` allows, it can carry a `grant` (`ApprovalGrantChoice`). Leaving it out or passing `{ persist: false }` means “this time only”, which issues a Grant covering only this call, with an unknown amount. `{ persist: true, scope?, maxCalls?, budgetCap?, expiresAt? }` also issues an ongoing Grant (by default it covers only this video). An approval that sends data out has no “don't ask again in this task” option. `accept-for-session` and `forSession` do not apply to it. A tool that was already exempt from asking in the session still asks when an outbound call is not covered. An approval that does not send data out and is given a `grant` is refused with `invalid-request`. Under `fullAccess`, approvals are allowed automatically, and only a “this time only” Grant is issued. It removes the confirmation, but the budget still applies: when the quota runs out, the result is `BUDGET_EXCEEDED` in every mode. CLI: `cuccut approvals` lists the data that approvals need authorised, and `cuccut approvals allow <id> --persist [--scope all] [--max-calls N] [--budget amount --currency currency] [--expires time]` allows the approval and issues an ongoing Grant.

**Executed by `ApprovalService`**. It lives in the Harness and is shared by sessions and external services (§4.8):

- Driver approvals and CucCut tool calls in a session are looked up in the table first. Actions that are automatically allowed or refused leave a decided approval card in the session (`decidedBy: 'auto'`). Actions that must ask leave a pending approval card and join the unified pending list, waiting for the user.
- **Session approvals have no time limit**. They end when the user handles them, when the task is stopped, when the reply is stopped (`agents.interrupt`), or when the task ends. Stopping or interrupting cancels all pending approvals. The Driver receives the cancellation, and waiting tool calls return `APPROVAL_CANCELLED`. A refusal returns `APPROVAL_DENIED` (with `mode` and `risk`, showing whether the user refused it or the mode did not allow it). Service approvals do have a time limit (§4.8).
- **All pending approvals are listed in one place**. The `tasks` topic snapshot carries `approvals` (`PendingApproval`: the subject is a session or a service and client, plus the action, target, summary, risk, the mode or service level it was based on, and the expiry time). Appearance and removal are sent as `approval.upsert` and `approval.removed`. `approvals.list` and `approvals.respond { approvalId, decision: 'allow' | 'deny' }` read and handle them. The original entry points still work and handle the same approvals: session approvals use `agents.respondToApproval`, and service approvals use `services.respondToApproval` and the `services` topic.
- CLI: `cuccut approvals` lists pending session and service approvals, and `cuccut approvals allow|deny <id>` handles one. `cuccut chat --mode ask|auto-accept-edits|auto|full-access|plan` switches the session's mode, and when an approval comes up during a turn, the terminal asks.
- For a Driver, `accept-for-session` from `agents.respondToApproval` is passed through unchanged. For CucCut tools it means “don't ask again for this same tool in this task”. It exists only in memory and expires when the task ends.

**Driver approvals follow the same table**. The Driver's reported actions are rated like this: a file change inside the working directory is `edit`, any file outside the working directory is `high`, and a command is `command`. When the Driver says the request is an escalation beyond the sandbox (which is what Codex asks under the `on-request` policy), it is `high`. The Driver's own permission settings are derived from the mode, and when the two disagree, the stricter one applies. CucCut cannot intercept actions the Driver does not ask about, so the Driver side must never be looser than CucCut's mode. The Codex mapping:

| Mode | `approvalPolicy` | `sandbox` | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `plan` | `untrusted` | `read-only` | Read-only sandbox; untrusted commands all ask |
| `ask`, `autoAcceptEdits` | `untrusted` | `workspace-write` | Commands and file changes both ask; under `autoAcceptEdits`, changes inside the working directory are accepted automatically by the Harness |
| `auto` | `on-request` | `workspace-write` | Runs directly inside the sandbox; asks when going outside the sandbox (`high`, left to the user) |
| `fullAccess` | `never` | `workspace-write` | Never asks; the sandbox still applies |

When CucCut tools are called through Codex's MCP, the call waits for approval, and Codex gives up a tool call after 60 seconds by default. So the session configures `tool_timeout_sec` for CucCut's MCP server to one day.

**Switching**. The `accessMode` on the session record is the mode this session has switched to. `null` means it follows the preference `agent.defaultAccessMode` (§5.10, default `auto`).

- `conversations.update { accessMode }` switches the mode, and `null` returns to following the setting. `conversations.send` with an explicit `accessMode` (formerly `autonomy`) is the same as switching first and then sending. When none is given, the session's mode is used, and if the session has never been switched, the setting is used.
- A switch takes effect immediately for actions that follow, including while a task is running. CucCut tool calls and Driver approvals are looked up against the new mode. Approvals already waiting for the user are unchanged. The Driver's own permission settings change only when the next native session is created (the next message).
- When the effective mode changes, a hint is recorded in the session (`notice`, with `modeChange { from, to }`). If the session has no content yet, it is recorded only on the session.

**Recording the mode at the time of an action**. A task card carries `autonomy { mode, source }` (`source`: `request` if given when sending, `conversation` if the session was switched, `setting` if from settings, `default` for the built-in default). An approval card carries `risk`, `mode`, and `decidedBy` (`auto` or `user`). A CucCut tool result carries `approval { mode, risk, decidedBy }`. All of these are in the Runtime record and are not written into the video's receipt. The engine does not need to know the mode.

**Legacy values**. The three tiers of earlier versions (`plan`, `controlled`, `authorized`) are mapped to the new values: `controlled` → `ask`, and `authorized` → `fullAccess`. Protocol parameters (`autonomy` and `accessMode` in `conversations.send`, `conversations.update`, and `settings.set`) still accept the old values. Old values stored in the settings file and in session records are converted when read. The Runtime always emits the new values. Older interfaces (the desktop UI and the Web client that mounts the same UI) send one of the three legacy tiers as `autonomy` with every send (chosen in the input box, default `controlled`). So a session they send messages in is always `plan`, `ask`, or `fullAccess`, and never reaches `autoAcceptEdits`, `auto`, or the follow-setting.

- **The definition of high risk does not loosen to nothing across modes**: except under `fullAccess`, actions that still ask under `auto` include writes outside the project directory (creating a new file in the download directory is excluded: `download` and `downloads_save` are `command`), deleting a video or project, irreversible overwrites, and outbound data transfer and paid calls that need new authorisation (§12.5).
- **A mode is not authorisation**. No mode grants permission for outbound data transfer, budget, voice cloning, or public publishing. These are still granted item by item through Grants. `fullAccess` only removes per-action confirmation. It does not change the scope of protection, the version checks, or the note on isolation in §12.4.

---

## 4. Business protocol

### 4.1 One public SDK, one business entry point

`@cuccut/client` exposes these namespaces:

| Namespace | Contents |
| --- | --- |
| `videos` | Creating, opening and range-based reading of videos |
| `timeline` | Reading the timeline by sequence, time range and object type |
| `speech` | Searching words, sentences and occurrences in the script |
| `edits` | Edit transactions: proposals, batch preflight, commit and undo |
| `code` | Building, publishing and checking code packages |
| `conversations` | Creating, listing, messages, binding and archiving conversations |
| `tasks` | Task contracts, stop, status |
| `agents` | Drivers, sessions, approvals |
| `models` | Capability catalog, provider configuration, local models |
| `jobs` | Step status, cancellation, reconciliation |
| `artifacts` | Artifact queries and resource handles |
| `previews` | Frame fetching, inspection, preview sessions |
| `exports` | Export drafts, creation, status |
| `space` | Querying and organizing the Space directory, and the entry point for secondary editing |
| `projects` | Registering, opening, renaming, pinning and archiving projects |
| `library` | User library: glossaries, voices, brand libraries (§5.9) |
| `settings` | Preferences (§5.10) |
| `services` | Switches, configuration and status of external services (§4.8) |
| `nodes` | Discovery, pairing and local sharing of LAN nodes (§6.7) |
| `externalTools` | Status and install actions for managed external tools (§12.9) |
| `catalog` | Tool catalog for the agent surface (§3.5): `list` returns the tools (their parameter JSON Schema, descriptions, annotations and risk) and the interface version; `call` invokes a tool by name as the terminal principal (`LocalPrincipal`). Only available to the `cli` and `desktop` connections; not on the Web service allowlist |

The UI, the CLI and external agents all send Commands, Queries and Subscriptions through the same path. The renderer may forward to the local socket or pipe via Electron IPC, but this does not add another business API.

The command envelope, command surface and error contract are in the Command and Protocol Specification.

### 4.2 Trusted identity

Public requests must not carry `actor`, `trusted=true` or any `budgetApproved`. The Runtime builds a `TrustedPrincipal` from the authenticated connection and maps `commandId` to the in-video `idempotencyKey`. `transactionId`, `actor`, available permissions and `runGeneration` are determined by the server.

The same video / key / payloadHash returns the same receipt; submitting a different payload with the same key must be rejected.

### 4.3 Commands and the internal engine contract

`VideoService.execute()` is the only public write entry point. After authorization it forwards to the private `VideoEngine.applyTransaction()`.
