The transcript is written as a new version. `cuccut.translation/2` translations are re-split on the new speaker boundaries and written as a new version (not re-translated). The caption layer is not regenerated; the UI marks it as stale, the same as when the source text is edited. If the transcript, or the translations involved in the trial, were edited after recognition, application is refused (`STALE_JOB_INPUT`) and recognition must be redone. Parameters, summary and refusals are in the commands and protocol spec §4.1 (`pipelines`, `edits`). This step runs only locally. Agents and the MCP service do not expose it for now; the “Hand to Agent” action on the tools page sends an intent sentence.

**Task spec**. The JobSpec for `models.transcribe` is frozen with: a source-version reference and content hash; audio-track selection; an optional time range; language (assert or prefer); provider and model package; whether to diarize (`diarize`; if not given, it follows the model: `speakers` is `native` or `pack` for diarization); a terminology hint (empty when the model does not accept hints); and `outputContract: 'cuccut.asr-result/v1'`. The input hash is computed from these fields. A published Artifact with the same input hash is reused directly (§7.3).

**Output contract `cuccut.asr-result/v1`**. Fields and validation rules are in Model Worker protocol spec §6. Key points: all times are integer ticks, `clock: 'source-asset'`; `outcome` distinguishes `transcribed`, `no-audio-track` and `no-speech`; each word carries a `timingQuality`; the result carries `coverage`, structured `warnings` and full `provenance`. The Runtime validates before publishing, and failures are handled per §6.5.

**Cloud recognition**. Online providers (§6.4) are implemented in TypeScript and emit the same contract. Times are mapped back to the source clock as integer ticks (both the slice start and the requested range start are added back). Where the vendor gives word timings, they are marked `provider`. Where only segments or only text are given, words are interpolated within segments by character length and marked `estimated`, as with local models that have no aligner. Where only words are given, segmentation is by pauses (> 0.8 seconds), sentence-final punctuation and segment length (> 30 seconds). The monotonic correction and warning rules are the same as for local. Once the `align` implementation exists (P1), `postAlign` in the JobSpec decides whether a local `align` Job is appended. An online result has `bundleId` null, `backend: 'online'` and `device: 'remote'`. If slice encoding fails or ffmpeg times out while preparing audio, it is treated as a "runtime crash" and retried automatically once. If the media's audio cannot be decoded, it is an input error and is not retried. If ffmpeg cannot be found, the task fails and prompts installation.

**Applying to the video**. Application (§7.1) converts `asr-result` into a `speech` document: `PutDocument`, kind `speech`, schema `cuccut.speech/1`, `sourceAsset` pointing to the source version, `clock: 'source-asset'`. The raw result is written content-addressed to `<runtime-home>/artifacts/`, and its ID is written to the document extension (`rawResultArtifactId`). The summary records word count, speaker count and language. When the video already has a `speech` document for this source, the destination is set by the transcription flow's `destination` (§7.9 "Transcription"), and two copies are not kept side by side in the same video. `new-video` (default) first has the flow create a new video in the same project and link the same source; the document is written into the new video, while the original video and its translations, captions and dubbing are left untouched. `replace` is **swapping the transcript**: in one transaction, it writes a new version of the existing document, switches the video to use it, and carries over dependent content. Each language's translation gets a new TranslationDocumentVersion (rules in video format spec §5.3, "carry-over on source version replacement"; when the flow parameter `translations: 'discard'` is set, nothing carries over and translations stay on the old version). Caption layers derived from this transcript and its translations are re-split on the new transcript (same algorithm as §7.9 "Caption layer", profile unchanged). Caption pins are re-anchored by time (video format spec §5.5). Dubbing plans carry over with the translations (video format spec §7.2). The receipt for this transaction, `impact`, gives for each language the number of cues kept (of which reviewed), stale and unmatched; the number of re-anchored and `orphaned` pins; and the dubbing units kept and stale per language. Its fields match the `translations`, `captionPins` and `dubs` of the transcription flow summary (commands and protocol spec §4.1 `pipelines`). Undoing returns everything to the state before the swap. When the current transcript's full-text fingerprint does not match `stages.asr` (video format spec §5.5), the user has edited the source text: `replace` refuses with `TRANSCRIPT_EDITED`, unless the caller passes `acceptEdited`, which is the accept-warning of §10.3. The acceptance record is bound to the transcript fingerprint and video version at commit time, and is checked again before application; if the fingerprint has changed again, it is still refused. For a video without a `speech` document, both destinations write directly into it. The unification of `cuccut.speech/1` with the model in format spec §5.2 is described in format spec §9; this section defines no fields of its own.

**Live transcript**. During recognition, the Worker pushes a `job.segment` after each completed segment (Model Worker protocol spec §3). JobManager converts it to seconds on the source clock, records it against the running task, and incrementally pushes it to clients as `job.segments` on the `jobs` topic. The snapshot's `liveSegments` fills in clients that subscribe midway (commands and protocol spec §10.4). The editor uses this to draw recognized paragraphs first in the timeline, transcript panel and caption panel, without speakers or word timing. For a first transcription, the preview also stacks them as a temporary caption layer in the sequence sent to the rendering core (in the preview engine only; export does not go through it). Live paragraphs are a projection of the task event stream, not project content: they do not enter documents, the undo stack or disk, and they are discarded once the task leaves `running` (completed, failed, cancelled or interrupted). On completion they are replaced by the `speech` document from applying to the video. After a crash, the automatic retry recognizes from the beginning, and the paragraphs start over as well. Non-streaming sources show nothing until then: an online provider gives all paragraphs at once when it finishes; remote nodes do not forward paragraphs; self-segmenting models (MOSS) give segmentation only after the whole recognition finishes.

**Cancellation and resumption**. Cancellation takes effect at segment boundaries. Recognized segments are written to the result, but a cancelled Job is not published. P0 crash recovery reruns the whole Job (the one automatic retry in §6.5). The P1 Worker writes results segment by segment to append-only JSONL, each entry with segment id, input hash and Worker version. After a crash, the Runtime checks that the JSONL prefix matches the current input and resumes from the next segment (§7.5). An invalid checkpoint means the whole Job is rerun, with no splicing.

**Phases**. P0: local `transcribe` (single backend), cloud `transcribe`, validation and Application, whole-Job retry, idle unload, staging cleanup. P1: `align`, speaker diarization, segment checkpoint resumption, memory-pressure eviction, multi-backend selection.

### 6.7 Remote nodes (capability sharing)

**Scope.** A machine can share its local model capabilities with other machines on the LAN or VPN. What is shared is **capabilities** (`transcribe`, `align`, `synthesizeSpeech`, …), not video. The submitting Runtime is always the only writer of the video; a remote node receives only media and parameters, returns the same output contract as the local Worker (§6.6), and the initiator validates and applies it. A remote node is not MCP: MCP and the native tool bridge are how agents enter the **local** Runtime (§3.5) and listen only on loopback. Sharing a capability with another machine goes only through the node protocol in this section.

**Two roles, one implementation.** The node side is a separate listening surface inside the Runtime, the “node service”. It has its own port and authentication, separate from the local gateway (§4, §12.2), and the user enables it explicitly. Tasks it submits enter the same JobManager and `ResourceScheduler` (§7.6) as local tasks, and use the same Model Workers, staging, and cleanup rules (§6.5). The initiating side is a Provider in the Models module (the “verified remote node” in §6.1), listed alongside online Providers. Parameters, submission, events, cancellation, result download, and error normalization are all implemented in TypeScript.

**Protocol.** Fields and endpoints are in the [node protocol spec](../spec/node-protocol-spec.md). HTTP/1.1 + JSON. Media is uploaded as a stream and verified by content digest. Progress is an NDJSON event stream with an increasing `seq`, resumed after a disconnect with `since`. Results are fetched per file with sha256 and support Range resume. Each task has a `clientJobId` as its idempotency key; resubmitting returns the same task. Version gate: the request header carries `nodeProtocolVersion`, and a request below the minimum version is rejected outright, with the required version reported. The health endpoint needs no authentication. It reports only node identity, version, backends, each capability's on/off switch and busy level, and nothing about the local machine beyond models.

**Trust.** A pairing code (6 digits, valid for 10 minutes, locked for 10 minutes after 5 wrong attempts) is exchanged for a long-lived Bearer token. The node stores only a salted hash and compares in constant time. On the initiating side, the token is kept in the credential store (§6.8). A request first passes the source-IP gate (by default only private ranges, link-local, and loopback; it looks only at the socket peer and does not trust forwarding headers), then the version gate, and then token verification. Management operations (pairing codes, revocation, switches) go only through the local gateway; the node service has no management endpoints. The first version is not encrypted, and its threat model is the same as a LAN printer. Public exposure is an explicit non-goal. `allowAnySource` is for VPN use only and provides no tunnel. Discovery uses mDNS (`_cuccut-node._tcp`). TXT records are for display only and confer no trust. On macOS, it uses the system DNS-SD.

**Routing and failure.** The user explicitly chooses which machine to use (a model ID with a node alias, or a task parameter naming the node). There is no automatic local-first or remote-first preference and no automatic failover. If the node is lost, the task fails with `REMOTE_NODE_LOST`, and the UI offers a one-time “use this machine instead” (改用本机) action. It must never silently load a large model on a weak machine. Preflight before submission: healthy, version matches, capability switch on, model ready. If the event stream disconnects and reconnection still fails after 60 seconds, the node counts as lost. Cancellation is sent to the node and waits for confirmation. Afterwards three separate facts are shown: “local side no longer waiting” (本地不再等待), “node cancelled” (节点已取消), and “resources released” (资源已释放) (§7.4).

**Node-side resources and cleanup.** A remote task is an ordinary Job. It is admitted according to the capability switch and is bounded by queue depth and a per-client concurrency limit (default 1 running per client, queue of 8). It is rejected when disk space is below a threshold. Uploaded media is written to the task's staging directory and deleted when the task ends. Results are deleted after retrieval or after the retention period (default 10 minutes). The node does not record transcript text: a remote task's results do not enter the artifact library and are not written to any video. After a node restart, in-memory remote tasks are all marked `interrupted`, and the initiating side decides whether to retry. The node service's switch state is persisted with the Runtime's switch state and restored at startup. If the node service itself crashes, it goes through the Runtime's bounded restart (§2.2) and is not restarted indefinitely on its own.

**Phases.** P1: the `transcribe` node service and Provider, pairing, mDNS, and the UI's “compute node” (计算节点) and “share this computer” (共享这台电脑) entries. P2: other capabilities, OpenAI-compatible endpoints, TLS, and certificate pinning.

### 6.8 Model service configuration

Model service configuration is persistent state in the Runtime Home, and it is read and written only through the Runtime:

- **Provider configuration**: for each online Provider (an API provider, §6.4), whether it is enabled, provider-level endpoint rewriting, the display name and declared models of custom endpoints, and its accounts. Keys are stored apart from the rest of the configuration and are read and written through the credential store below. The method that reads configuration reports only a mask and “configured” (已配置) and never echoes a key. The configuration file is `<runtime-home>/store/model-services.json` (`formatVersion: 2`: switches, enable time, endpoints, each Provider's `accounts`, and each capability's defaults). It is written with mode 0600, via a temp file and rename. When a Provider is enabled, its enable time is recorded and one default persistent Grant is issued (migration rule, §12.5). The recipient is this Provider. The data category is what its capability sends outward by default: transcription is audio; synthesis and image generation are text; text generation is text and manuscripts, and the translation flow hands manuscripts over through it. The scope is all video, counted per call, amount unknown, with no limit on count. The source is recorded as `provider-enable`. This keeps the earlier behaviour of “enabling means consenting to outbound sending”, but the Grant is now visible and revocable. After the user revokes it, it is not reissued, and later calls go back to requiring authorization. Disabling or removing a Provider revokes it, and re-enabling it (with a new enable time) issues one again. Configurations from earlier versions, which have no enable time, are issued one once as well. This Grant belongs to the Provider, not to accounts. Adding or removing accounts, or changing their order, neither issues nor revokes it (§12.5). A custom endpoint's `providerId` is `custom:<name>`, where the name is lowercase letters, digits, and hyphens. Agent Providers (§6.9) have only a switch and an enable time. They use no key and have no accounts, and the configuration view reports their `credential` as `none`. `models.configure` rejects a key, endpoint, model, or `verify` for them, and the account methods reject them too. `models.removeProvider` on a custom endpoint deletes the whole configuration. On a catalog provider, it disables the provider and deletes all its accounts and credentials, returning it to the not-added state. In both cases, defaults pointing to it are kept and reported as unavailable (see “Capability defaults” below).
- **Accounts**: an account is one API provider's key. `accounts` is an ordered list. Each account stores `accountId` (a random short id), `label` (a user-given name, may be empty), `masked` (the mask), `enabled`, an optional `region` (for catalog providers with both a China and an international base URL, §6.4), an optional `endpoint` (the account's own base-URL rewrite, rare), `addedAt`, `lastUsedAt`, and `status`. The configuration view's `ProviderConfigView.accounts` (`ProviderAccountView[]`, in order) reports the same fields, without the key.
  - **Masking** is computed when the key is written and stored: the first 3 characters, `…`, then the last 4 characters (`sk-…ab12`). Keys of 8 characters or fewer are replaced entirely with `•`. Afterwards only the stored mask is shown. No method, event, or log echoes the key.
  - **Selection**: one call uses the first enabled account that has a key. Bounded retries within the same call (§6.4) use the same account. A failed call does not switch to another account. Invalid keys, rate limits, and exhausted quota fail with the error codes in §6.4, consistent with §6.2's rules of no automatic fallback and no switching on retry. Order is decided by the user. The UI's “set as preferred” (设为首选) action is `models.arrangeAccounts`.
  - **Status** records only the result of the most recent real call or verification. Success is `ok`. 401/403 is `invalid-key`. 429 rate limiting is `rate-limited`, with `until` derived from `Retry-After` (empty when absent). Exhausted quota (`insufficient-quota`) is `quota-exhausted`. Never called is `unknown`. There is also a time `at` and a key-free `detail`. Status is for display only: it does not cause an account to be skipped and does not change selection.
  - **Availability**: the configuration view's `credential` is kept. It means whether at least one enabled account with a key exists (`set` or `missing`; `none` for Agent Providers). If there is none, the Provider reports as unavailable, and calls are rejected with `CAPABILITY_NOT_CONFIGURED` (`missing-credential`). Deleting the last account does not remove the API provider: it stays enabled, with `credential: 'missing'`.
  - **Methods**: `models.addAccount`, `models.updateAccount`, `models.removeAccount`, and `models.arrangeAccounts` each return the Provider's view (`ProviderView`, description plus configuration; parameters and errors are in the commands and protocol spec §4.1). `addAccount`, and `updateAccount` when the key changed, verify the new key first when called with `verify: true` (see “Connectivity test” below), and do not save if verification fails. `models.configure`'s `credential` is still accepted for the CLI and earlier callers. When accounts exist, it replaces the first account's key. When none exist, it creates an account with `accountId` `main`. Account changes, like other configuration changes, are delivered through the `models` topic as `capabilities.updated`. Which methods the browser may call is in §4.8.
  - **Migration**: when a version 1 `model-services.json` is read, each Provider with a `provider:<providerId>` credential gets one account (`accountId: 'main'`, empty `label`, with the mask computed from the key read out). The key is first written to the new key. After that write succeeds, the version 2 configuration file is written. Finally, the old key is deleted. If the old key cannot be deleted, it is left until the next open and deleted then, without affecting use. Providers without a credential get no account. The file and keychain backends are handled the same way. If it fails midway, the old key and the version 1 file both remain, and migration reruns on the next open.
- **Credential store**: keys for online Providers and tokens for nodes (node protocol spec §11) are read and written through `CredentialStore`, of which there is one instance in the Runtime. The interface has only `get`, `set`, `delete`, and `has`, with no method that lists keys. Keys are namespaced: `provider:<providerId>/<accountId>` (custom endpoints use `provider:custom:<name>/<accountId>`; version 1's `provider:<providerId>` is read only during migration) and `node:<nodeId>`. Removing a Provider deletes the credentials of all its accounts. Removing an account deletes only that one. Removing a node deletes its token. Writes store the credential first, then change the rest of the configuration. The backend is determined by the build type, not by a user setting or a runtime switch:
  - **File** (development builds): `<runtime-home>/store/model-credentials.json`, with content `{ formatVersion: 2, credentials: { "<key>": "<secret>" } }`. It holds only secrets, is written with mode 0600, via a temp file and rename. Version 1 files (keyed by `providerId`, containing only Provider keys) are read as they are.
  - **System secure storage** (release builds; the build parameter is still named `keychain`): on macOS it uses generic password keychain items, with service fixed to `com.cuccut.runtime` and account set to the key. On Windows it uses Credential Manager generic credentials, with target name `<key>.com.cuccut.runtime`, read and written through keyring's Windows native backend. Entries do not distinguish Runtime Home. The Runtime is a plain Node process and reaches the keychain through the native component `credential-helper` (§13.1), which is signed with the app. Each operation starts one helper. stdin receives one line of JSON request (`{ op, key, secret? }`, where `op` is `get`, `set`, `delete`, or `has`, and only `set` carries `secret`). stdout returns one line of JSON response (success is `{ ok: true }`, with `get` also carrying `secret` and `has` carrying `exists`; failure is `{ ok: false, error, message }`). The error codes are a closed set: `not-found`, `denied`, `unavailable`, `unsupported`, `internal`. On platforms other than macOS and Windows, the helper answers `unsupported` to every operation. The helper is located in this order: `CUCCUT_CREDENTIAL_HELPER`; then `CUCCUT_BIN_DIR`, the directory where the natively shipped programs live (with `.exe` on Windows; if set, the search stops here); then the build output in the cargo target directory during development. In release builds of the desktop app, the main process sets `CUCCUT_BIN_DIR` to `<resources>/bin` and passes it to the Runtime. Workers search in the same order.
  - **Migration**: on each startup, the keychain backend first writes each legacy credential from the plaintext file into the keychain, one at a time. Entries that succeed are removed from the file, and once all are migrated the file is deleted. Entries that cannot be migrated stay in the file until the next startup, and the log records only the key and the reason. Node tokens that earlier versions stored in `nodes.json` are migrated away the same way (node protocol spec §11).
  - **Unavailable**: when the keychain is unavailable (helper missing, access denied, timeout), it does not fall back to the plaintext file and does not read keys left by migration. Providers that depend on it report as unavailable (`unavailableReason: 'missing-credential'`, with `detail` starting with “credential unavailable” (凭据不可用) followed by the cause). Nodes report that the token cannot be read (probe result `unpaired`). Methods that write or delete credentials (`models.configure`, `models.addAccount`, `models.updateAccount`, `models.removeAccount`, `models.removeProvider`, `nodes.pair`, `nodes.remove`) are rejected with `conflict`, where `details.code` is `CREDENTIAL_UNAVAILABLE` and `details.reason` is the error code above. The rest of the configuration is unchanged.
- **Capability defaults**: each capability has a default `providerId` and a default `modelId`, either of which may be empty. When a Provider is disabled, a node is removed, or a model pack is uninstalled, defaults pointing to it are kept but reported as unavailable. They are not silently repointed elsewhere. The exception is deleting a local model pack (`models.remove`, §6.3): defaults pointing to it for capabilities without a factory default (`synthesizeSpeech`, `generateImage`, `generateText`, §6.2) are cleared and return to unset. `transcribe` and `separateAudio` have factory defaults, so defaults pointing to a removed pack are kept and reported as unavailable, and do not fall back to the factory default.

- **Capability parameter defaults**: call parameters that belong to only one capability, such as the reasoning effort and concurrency limit of `generateText`. Set them with `models.setCapabilityParameters`: the fields given replace the current values, and null restores the default. `models.capabilities` reports the current values for that capability. An empty reasoning effort defers to the model's own default. The concurrency limit defaults to 4 (1–32) and is counted per Provider. Tasks and in-process calls share it, and changes take effect at the next slot acquisition.
- **Connectivity test and model discovery**: when `models.configure`, `models.addAccount`, or `models.updateAccount` is called with `verify: true`, a read-only request (listing models) is sent to the vendor with the new key and endpoint before saving. If it fails, nothing is saved. It proves only that the credential and endpoint work, not that a particular model or voice is available, and it runs only when the user asks. `models.refreshProvider` fetches the vendor's list of available models and voices and caches it in the configuration with a timestamp. When the fetch fails, the built-in list is kept and marked as such. The fetched list is used only to annotate built-in models and does not replace them. Built-in models not in the list are reported as unavailable (`unsupported`, with `detail` stating the refresh time), and defaults pointing to them are likewise unavailable without being repointed. Voices in an ElevenLabs account are added to models that have no preset voices. This applies to online catalog Providers and custom endpoints (OpenAI and compatible providers list `{base}/models`, Anthropic lists `{base}/v1/models`), using the first enabled account with a key. `local`, node, and Agent Providers are rejected with `invalid-request`. When the fetch fails (`ok: false`, with a key-free reason), the previous result is also discarded and the built-in list is restored. It never changes switches, credentials, or defaults.

`models.capabilities` is a read-only view of this state. For each capability, it gives each Provider's models, limits, defaults, whether each is currently available, and the reason if not. The local `generateImage` Provider is ordered after online and agent Providers, the same order as the UI model menu. The settings page, the CLI, and agent tools all read the same view. It is also the source of the model capability entries in the capability snapshot (§3.6). Configuration changes are delivered through the `models` topic (`capabilities.updated`, carrying the complete new view). Tasks in progress are unaffected, since they have already frozen their selection (§6.2). The CLI's `cuccut models`, `models configure` (the key is read from standard input only), and `models default` use the same set of methods. `cuccut speak`, `cuccut image`, and `cuccut music` submit generation tasks, wait for them to finish, and save the artifact to `--out` via `artifacts.openHandle`. `cuccut text` likewise submits `models.generateText`. Without `--out`, the body goes to standard output and the Provider and usage go to standard error. `--json-schema <file>` requires structured output. `cuccut models refresh <providerId>` and `cuccut models parameters generateText [--effort] [--concurrency]` correspond to the two methods above.

Local `synthesizeSpeech` is provided by the `local` Provider (§6.1), with models that are local synthesis model packs (§6.3). Like online Providers, it must be explicitly selected or set as default. Local `generateImage` is likewise provided by the `local` Provider, with models that are local text-to-image model packs. It has no factory default and no “auto-select”. Local `generateMusic` is available only from the `local` Provider (music model packs, including the audio.cpp engine component). It likewise has no factory default. `provider: 'local'` without `model` uses the first available music model pack. Node sharing of these capabilities belongs to P2 (§6.7).

### 6.9 Agents as providers

Some capabilities can be run by agent runtimes the user already subscribes to, with no API key needed. The first version has only one: generating images with Codex, where `providerId` is `agent:codex` and the capability is `generateImage`.

`AgentDriver` and `ModelProvider` are still registered separately (§3.1). An agent provider comes from the fourth provider source (`kind: 'agent'`), alongside the local, node and online sources; it is listed only when its corresponding Driver is registered. It is an ordinary `ModelProvider`: internally it makes one restricted call through the same Driver's machine protocol (no separate protocol implementation), and externally it honours the full `generateImage` contract:

- **One call uses one dedicated native session.** It belongs to no user session, carries no video context or conversation history, and does not resume an old thread. Its input is only the prompt, the reference images and the output requirements (the developer instruction states the task and the output file; the turn input is the prompt text verbatim). The working directory is the Job's staging directory; no MCP servers are given and there are no CucCut tools. The access mode is fixed to "write only to the working directory" (the Driver's `confinement: 'cwd-write-only'`): Codex uses the `workspace-write` sandbox, with the default writable `/tmp` and `$TMPDIR` removed, no other writable directories added, and no network access for commands; the approval policy is `never`. Approval requests the session still sends are always declined, because no one is there to approve them. A call sends only one turn. When the turn ends (completed, failed, timed out or cancelled), the native session closes; when a Job is cancelled, the turn is interrupted before the session is closed. A turn's deadline is 10 minutes; at that point the turn is interrupted and the session closed, and the Job fails with `PROVIDER_UNAVAILABLE`. Session records that the native runtime keeps itself (for example Codex's session directory) are not managed by CucCut.
- **Output is validated the same way.** The agreed output is `output.png` in the working directory. If it is absent, the single image in staging is taken (subdirectories are searched too, only regular files are accepted, and symbolic links are not followed); if there are several, all are handed over and the count check rejects them. The declared media type is the requested format. After that, as with other providers, JobManager checks the digest and length and validates by file header and ffprobe decoding (§6.4, §7.1). The actual pixel dimensions are recorded in the output as facts, and the result is then published as an artifact. If a video was given, the image is imported as a candidate asset, the same as for other providers. The agent's text reply is not output: without an image the Job fails with `PROVIDER_REJECTED` (`details.reason: 'no-image'`, with an excerpt of the reply). A failed or interrupted turn is also `PROVIDER_REJECTED` (`turn-failed`, `turn-interrupted`). A written file that is not a valid PNG gives `MODEL_OUTPUT_INVALID`. There is no retry and no switching to another provider.
- **The description declares its limits honestly.** One model (`image-gen`; the actual image model is decided by the agent runtime and the account), `maxCount` 1, only `png`, `sizes` and `aspectRatios` empty, `defaultSize` null, `acceptsSeed` false, and cost status `subscription` ("included in subscription, quota unknown"). `notes` gives one sentence on serial execution, duration and quota. Unsupported parameters are rejected at submission, not marked as ignored: a size or aspect ratio, a seed, more than one image, or any other format each return `invalid-request`, and no Job is created. The queue is partitioned by provider, and only 1 task runs at a time.
- **Availability comes from the Driver's status** (§3.11), checked in this order: not installed (`not-installed`), not signed in (`signed-out`), otherwise unavailable for the Driver (`unsupported`), version below this provider's minimum (`outdated`; `agent:codex` requires 0.158.0, the version the protocol was checked against), and not enabled by the user (`not-configured`). The remedy for the first four is `setup-agent`: install, sign in or upgrade in the agent runtime's own UI, with `hint` giving the steps. The last one's remedy is `enable-provider`. `models.capabilities` probes only the Driver for this provider. A result in the Driver registry (§3.11) less than 10 seconds old is used directly; otherwise only this one Driver is re-probed and awaited, and the result is written back to the registry's cache (and also pushed through the `agents` topic), without waiting for other Drivers' probes. Selection and views probe only when the user has enabled the provider; when it is not enabled, they use the most recent result, and no external command is launched for a provider that is not enabled. The check is repeated before execution: if the provider was disabled, signed out or uninstalled while the Job was queued, no session is opened and the Job fails with `MODEL_LOAD_FAILED`.
- **Enabling is the only configuration** (§6.8). The user explicitly enables it in the model service settings. There are no keys, endpoints or model declarations; it uses the agent runtime's own sign-in. Enabling means agreeing to send the prompt and reference images to the account and service provider that the agent runtime is signed in with, using the user's own subscription quota.
- **The selection rules are unchanged** (§6.2). It can be specified explicitly or set as the default for `generateImage`; the factory default does not point to it.
- Other capabilities and other Drivers are added the same way, each opened after its own validation.

### 6.10 Usage ledger

Every real call to an online or agent provider records one usage entry, for the usage page in settings and for the API provider details (product design §7.6). It answers how much was used and roughly what it cost; it is not a budget. The authorization and budget ledger is `store/grants.json` (§7.8, §12.5), and admission does not read this ledger.

**File.** `<runtime-home>/store/usage.jsonl`, append-only, mode 0600, one record per line. Bad lines are skipped when reading.

```ts
interface UsageRecord {
  at: string;                         // ISO time, when the call ends
  providerId: string;
  accountId: string | null;           // null for agent providers and for unsaved verification
  capability: ModelServiceCapability | null;   // null when source is 'verify'; required otherwise
  modelId: string | null;                      // same as above
  source: 'job' | 'inline' | 'agent-tool' | 'verify';
  ref?: { jobId?: string; taskId?: string; conversationId?: string };
  units: UsageUnits;
  cost: { kind: 'reported'; amount: string; currency: 'USD' | 'CNY' } | { kind: 'unknown' };   // estimates are not written to the ledger
  durationMs: number;
  status: 'ok' | 'error';
  error?: string;                     // error code and one sentence of explanation, no keys
}
interface UsageUnits { inputTokens?: number; outputTokens?: number; cachedTokens?: number; audioSeconds?: number; chars?: number; images?: number }
```

**Write points.** For online providers, each call writes one record when it ends, whether it succeeds or fails: there is one write point each for transcription, speech synthesis, image generation and text generation. Both entry points of text generation are recorded: a Job from `models.generateText` is `job`, and a call through the in-process `TextGenerator` is `inline`. Calls made directly by an agent tool are `agent-tool`. Chunking inside one call (§6.4) and bounded retries are merged into one record, with their usage added together. Key verification (`verify`) also records one entry: it belongs to no capability or model, so `capability` and `modelId` are null (every other source must give them), `units` is empty, and no cost is charged. When verification fails and the account is not saved, `accountId` is null. Agent providers (`agent:codex`) are recorded too, with `accountId` null and cost `unknown`. Calls to local and LAN nodes are not recorded: they are not billed, and their data never leaves the user's own device.

**Usage** is filled per capability. For text, it is the input, output and cache-hit tokens; for transcription, the audio seconds; for speech synthesis, the character count (in Unicode code points, the same unit as the limit in §6.4); for image generation, the number of images. Where the provider reported a value, the reported value is used. Items not reported are left empty, and usage is not estimated.

**Cost.** The ledger records only the amount the API provider reports (`reported`, in the original currency); when nothing is reported, it is `unknown`. Estimates are not written to the ledger. They are calculated at read time from the price table: `packages/models/src/model-prices.ts` is a constant table that gives, per `providerId/modelId`, the unit prices per million input, output and cached tokens, per audio minute, per thousand characters and per image, with the currency. Each unit price has a comment stating the official pricing page and the date it was checked; anything uncertain is left unfilled. After the price table is updated, past estimates are recalculated with the new table. Estimates are a reference, not a bill.

Amounts come in three kinds: reported by the provider (`reported`), estimated from list prices (`estimated`), and unknown (`unknown`). They must not be merged into one number that hides the difference. A record with a report uses the report. Otherwise, if the price table has this model's unit prices and the needed usage is complete, an estimate is computed. Otherwise the cost is unknown and the call is counted in the unknown-cost calls. Different currencies are totalled separately and are not converted.

**Reading.** `models.usage { period, providerId? }` returns a `UsageReport`. `period` is `today`, `7d`, `30d` or `all`, split by the local date of the machine running the Runtime; `providerId` restricts the report to one API provider.

```ts
interface UsageReport {
  period: { from: string; to: string };
  totals: { calls: number; failed: number; units: UsageUnits; cost: { estimated: Money[]; reported: Money[]; unknownCalls: number } };
  byDay: Array<{ day: string; calls: number; units: UsageUnits; byCapability: Partial<Record<ModelServiceCapability, number>> }>;
  byProvider: UsageRow[]; byCapability: UsageRow[]; byModel: UsageRow[]; byAccount: UsageRow[];
}
interface UsageRow {
  key: string; label: string; providerId?: string;
  calls: number; failed: number; units: UsageUnits;
  cost: Money[];                                      // per currency, one entry each; empty when no amount can be counted
  costKind: 'reported' | 'estimated' | 'mixed' | 'unknown';
}
```

`Money` is `{ amount, currency }`, with the amount as a decimal string (same as §7.8). `costKind` says where a row's amounts come from: all reported, all estimated, both, or none at all. When an account's `label` is empty, a mask is shown; deleted accounts are still listed by `accountId`. Usage does not go into videos or Spaces.

**Balance (P1).** `models.checkBalance { providerId, accountId? }` uses the account's key to query the balance from the API provider, and returns the balance (per currency) and the query time. When `accountId` is not given, the first enabled account that has a key is used. Only providers that expose a balance endpoint are supported: DeepSeek (`/user/balance`), OpenRouter (`/api/v1/credits`) and Moonshot (`/v1/users/me/balance`); others are rejected with `invalid-request`. A balance queried this way is not written to the usage ledger and does not count toward budgets.

---

## 7. Jobs and scheduling

### 7.1 Execution and application are separate

"Artifact ready → waiting for application → applied" is one flow at the product level, but the database does not flatten it into a single status chain shared by all Jobs. One Artifact can be applied to several different videos, and export and analysis Jobs can have no video application.

```text
Job:
created → awaiting-approval → queued → running
   → succeeded | failed | interrupted
   → cancel-requested → cancelled | needs-reconciliation

Artifact:
staging → validated → published (immutable)

Application:
pending → validating → committed
                  → stale-input | rejected | cancelled

Task / Run:
planned → running → needs-review / stopping
        → completed | partially-completed | failed | stopped
```

A Job `succeeded` only means the output contract holds. A Task `completed` is settled against the TaskContract's `deliverables` and blocking issues:

| `requiredStage` | Settled when |
| --- | --- |
| `candidate-ready` | a real, openable candidate exists |
| `committed` | a receipt for the target version exists |
| `published` | a validated and published file exists |

The UI may combine them into "audio generated, not yet applied", but it does not merge the two facts into one misleading status.

Stages of generation Jobs (`synthesizeSpeech`, `generateImage`, `generateMusic`): `queued → starting → generating → validating → publishing → (if a video was given) applying → done`. Progress is counted by outputs received (`unit: 'outputs'`). The music engine does not report steps, so its `generating` stage shows indeterminate progress only and goes to 1/1 on completion. When a video was given, after publishing the Runtime imports all outputs as assets in a single transaction under the identity `system:jobs`. They are only candidates and are not placed on the timeline. The asset source records `origin: 'generated'` with the task, provider, model, input hash, artifact and parameter summary, but not the original text or the prompt. If the video was changed between read and write, a new `commandId` is used to re-read and retry, at most 3 times. If import fails, the task fails with `APPLY_FAILED` (`STALE_JOB_INPUT` when the video has been closed), and the published artifacts are kept in the result. Without a video, publishing completes the task, and the frozen parameters are the generation record (§7.9). A video given at submission must already be open and stays open while the task runs.

The Job states in the implementation are `queued`, `running`, `completed`, `failed`, `cancelled`, `interrupted` and `needs-reconciliation`. `needs-reconciliation` is a terminal state like the last four: nothing more runs, and the user decides via `jobs.reconcile` (§7.5). The Application state set is closed: it is the six states in the diagram above, and no others. Each time transcription or generation writes a result into a video, that is one Application. The task record's `applications` lists them in order, and the task's `state` is the conclusion combining execution and the most recent application:

| Latest application | Task | Artifact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `committed` | `completed`, `result` gains the document or asset ID | applied |
| `stale-input` | `failed` (`STALE_JOB_INPUT`) | kept in `result`, as a candidate |
| `rejected` | `failed` (`APPLY_FAILED`; when the receipt cannot be found, `details.reason` is `RECEIPT_UNKNOWN`) | same as above |
| `cancelled` | `cancelled` (no automatic apply after stopping, §7.4) | same as above |

### 7.2 Job spec and application record

```ts
interface JobSpec {
  id: Id;
  taskId: Id;
  runId: Id;
  runGeneration: string;
  capability: string;
  inputVersionRefs: VersionRef[];
  frozenInputRef: Id;
  parameters: JsonValue;
  providerSelection: ResolvedProviderSelection;
  permissionGrantRef: Id;
  budgetReservationRef?: Id;
  outputContract: string;
}

interface ApplicationRecord {
  applicationId: Id;
  jobId: Id;
  /** Published, immutable artifacts. */
  artifactIds: string[];
  videoId: Id;
  /** One-to-one with `artifactIds`: for transcription it is the asset ID; for generation it is the import operation's ref (`output1`, …). */
  targetRefs: string[];
  /** Video revision read at validation; null until validation has run. */
  baseVideoRevision: Revision | null;
  /** Command used for the latest submission; null until submitted. */
  commandId: Id | null;
  state: ApplicationState;
  /** `recovered`: the receipt was looked up from the engine by `commandId` after a restart or reconciliation. */
  receipt: { transactionId: Id | null; videoRevision: Revision | null; refs: Record<string, Id>; recovered?: true } | null;
  error: JobError | null;
  createdAt: string;
  updatedAt: string;
}
```

`inputVersionRefs`, permissions and budget are frozen by the server. A task cannot switch mid-run to reading the “current video”; changed inputs require a new attempt.

The authority for application records is `<home>/store/applications.jsonl` (the application ledger, separate from the task ledger). The `applications` in the task record is its projection, and when a task is evicted from the ledger, its applications are deleted with it. The `taskId` in `JobSpec` is not implemented. `runId` and `runGeneration` are used only for the engine-side stop barrier (§7.4) and do not go into the task record; the Runtime computes them at submission time. Before Task and Run are implemented, the task itself is its execution: `runId` is the task ID and `runGeneration` is the task's `attempt`. Steps of a fixed workflow use the parent task's.

For a generation task, the input is the text or the prompt itself: `contentHash` is its sha256, and `inputHash` is the sha256 of the canonical JSON of the capability, provider, model and frozen parameters. A matching input digest does not reuse an existing result, because generation is not reproducible; regenerating means asking for a new result. A repeated submission with the same `commandId` returns the same task.

A long call returns `jobId` immediately. Asynchronous completion notifications enter a persistent inbox, deduplicated by event. A follow-up Agent Turn is woken only after confirming that the Task has not been stopped and that authorization and budget still hold.

### 7.3 Recoverable loop from artifact to video

```text
frozen input → execute → staging bytes → validate and atomically publish Artifact
  → create Application → read current video → check targets and inputs
  → submit idempotent Transaction → persist receipt → mark committed
```

- The video is committed but the Node side crashes before recording success: on recovery, look up the receipt from the Engine by `commandId`. Do not insert the asset again.
- The result is published but its target has been deleted: keep the candidate and mark it `stale-input`. Do not guess at a nearby object.
- When identical content can be reused, do not buy it again. When the content has changed, do not force-apply the old audio.

**Ledger write order**:

1. Before an artifact is written to the artifact store, first write the publish intent to the task ledger: the artifact IDs to be written, the result, the application targets and warnings. The artifact store is content-addressed, so the ID is computed before writing.
2. After the artifact is published, first write the result, the budget settlement and a `pending` application into both ledgers (clearing the publish intent at the same time), then start validation.
3. Before each submission, use a new `commandId` (`cmd_<applicationId>_<n>`), record it together with `baseVideoRevision` as `validating`, and submit the transaction only after that has hit disk. The engine treats the same command with a different payload as an idempotency conflict, so an old command is never reused after re-validation.
4. After the transaction commits, record the receipt (`committed`).

**Claiming artifacts already written**: on restart, a task that carries a publish intent means the artifact may already be in the artifact store while the result has not been recorded.

- If all the artifacts in the intent are present and match their sha256: claim them. Record the result. If there is a target, create a new application to finish the work (as in “artifact published, application not finished”). If there is no target, complete the task. Do not re-infer and do not re-request the provider. The budget is settled once, on completion.
- If an artifact is missing or does not match, drop the intent and recover as “running” (§7.5).
- The intent is recorded in the task ledger, not as task metadata attached to the artifact. The artifact store deduplicates by content, so identical bytes are one file; attaching metadata would conflict with that rule, and recovery would have to scan the artifact store. The cost is one extra durable ledger write before each publish.

**Ledger durability**: the task and application ledgers are append-only JSONL (`store/jobs.jsonl`, `store/applications.jsonl`). The first line is the header `{"op":"header","formatVersion":2}`. Each following line is one operation: `{"op":"put", ...record}` writes or replaces one record, and `{"op":"remove","jobId"|"applicationId":…}` deletes one. The ledger remembers how each record was last written to disk, appends only the lines that changed, and fsyncs the file on every append (and the directory too when it is created). The publish-intent ordering depends on this. On replay, tasks keep their original position by creation order, while applications written again move to the end (older ones first). When the line count exceeds max(4 × live record count, 256), or the byte size exceeds 8 MiB and is more than twice the live records, the live records are compacted in full into a new file (temp file, fsync, rename, fsync the directory). Appends and compaction run on the same serial write chain. After a failed write, the next write compacts the whole file. On reading, a trailing partial line and unrecognized lines are skipped and logged, and the next write compacts them away. If the header is unrecognized, the file is renamed `.corrupt-<time>`, kept, and the ledger starts empty. The old whole-file `jobs.json` / `applications.json` are imported at startup and renamed `.migrated`. The authorization ledger still uses durable atomic replacement (write a temp file and fsync, rename, then fsync the directory). Other files (settings, sessions, etc.) use only atomic replacement or appending, with no fsync. Artifact store writes: the temp file (or clone) is fully written, fsynced, renamed, and then the directory is fsynced. Artifacts referenced by the ledger are durable on disk before the ledger records them. (On macOS, Node's fsync is not `F_FULLFSYNC`; this caveat applies here too.) On claim, the sha256 is still checked, to cover artifacts written earlier, the disk's own write cache and external changes.

**Artifact store sweeping** (`StorageGc`, `runtime-core/src/storage-gc.ts`): files in `artifacts/` that are unreferenced, have a modification time more than 1 hour old, and are not earlier than the sweep start point (`sweepSince` in §5.7) are deleted. Leftover `.tmp` files older than 1 hour are deleted too. The reference set is found by scanning text for every `sha256:<hex>`: tasks in any state in the ledger (records, frozen specs, execution parameters, publish intents), the application ledger, and Space artifact records (including references kept only for sweeping, and those in the on-disk `space-artifacts.json.corrupt-*`). The 1-hour grace period exists because artifacts are written to the store before being recorded. Re-publishing existing content refreshes the file's modification time. Timing: after Space backfills its records from the ledger, one round runs. Afterwards, each ledger eviction of a task is merged into one round 30 seconds later. While tasks are queued or running, the whole round is skipped, because the flow holds freshly written artifacts only in the staging directory until its steps complete. The skipped round runs at the next eviction or in the hourly scheduled rerun. A run in which the artifact records were entirely unreadable and it started empty does not sweep.

**Looking up receipts by command**: the engine's receipts are persisted in the video directory and remain queryable after the Engine Host restarts (`receipts.byCommand`).

- On restart, applications still unfinished (`pending`, `validating`) first look up their receipt by the recorded `commandId`. If one is found, record it and do not write again. If none is found, rebuild the same operation from the record and artifacts, re-validate, and submit under a new command.
- When a submission errors and it is unknown whether the transaction landed (connection dropped, engine restarted), also look up the receipt first.
- If the lookup itself fails (engine unavailable), do not rewrite under a new command. Mark the application `rejected` (`RECEIPT_UNKNOWN`) and leave it for `jobs.reconcile apply`. `apply` also looks up the receipt first, and when it cannot, it refuses with `busy`.
- If the video was changed between read and write (`conflict`), retry under a new command, at most 3 times.
- The engine's task protection rejection (`TASK_PROTECTED`, §3.2) and the stop barrier (§7.4) open no transaction: do not look up the receipt, do not retry, and mark the application `rejected`.

**Budget**: at artifact publish, the budget is settled on completion. Afterwards, whether the application succeeds, whether it is a restart resume, or whether `apply` runs during reconciliation, the provider is no longer called and nothing is settled again.

**Video location**: at submission, the video's location is recorded (source directory, relative path, and the owning project or session). When the video is not open after a restart, it is reopened by location as the identity `system:jobs-recovery`, holding a lease that is released when the application finishes. If the directory no longer exists, or contains a different video there, the application is `stale-input`.

**Tests**: the `faults` of `JobManager` (the Runtime's `jobFaults`) simulate a crash or insert an action at these moments:

- `artifact-stored`: the artifact is written to the artifact store, but the result is not recorded (only the publish intent exists);
- `artifact-published`: the artifact is published, with no application yet;
- `application-recorded`: the ledger has recorded the command, but it is not yet submitted;
- `application-committed`: already committed, receipt not yet recorded;
- `application-submitting`: the Node-side checks have passed, but the work has not been handed to the engine. The test cancels the task here to simulate a race between stop and submission.

End-to-end tests use a real engine and restart the Runtime on the same `CUCCUT_HOME`.

### 7.4 Stop barrier and cancellation

The primary button `tasks.stop`:

1. Marks the current Run as `stopping` and raises `runGeneration`. New tool admission and automatic application are rejected immediately.
2. Sends cancellation to the Agent Turn and to child Jobs. A child Job is a Job that the agent in this session has submitted and that is still queued or running (its `submitter` is this session, not limited to Jobs submitted by this Task, §7.9). It takes the same path as `jobs.cancel`. Jobs that have already finished keep their results, and Jobs submitted by other sessions or by the user are not affected. The number of cancellation requests is recorded in one session notice.

Every pending command to apply must validate `runGeneration`. A transaction that has already entered the indivisible commit section is linearized to completion and given a receipt. The stop confirmation lists these completed modifications together.

**The barrier cannot be just a boolean in Node memory.** The Runtime submits a `runGeneration` invalidation control to the Engine Host over a private channel. The Engine checks the generation at the video's serial commit entry. The “local stop in effect” response of `tasks.stop` must wait until the barrier is confirmed. Old generations that are queued but not yet linearized are rejected as automatic applications too. After an Engine restart or a connection generation change, all old authorization handles become invalid and must be re-registered explicitly by the new Runtime. A command's carried generation cannot be used to restore authorization on its own. Re-registration is not implemented (§14): after the Engine Host restarts, only the Node-side barrier exists.

**Accepting a stop does not mean the remote side has been cancelled.** External results that could not be cancelled may be kept as candidates, but they are not applied automatically. The performance target for local stop (acceptance and tests §4) refers to stopping new calls and automatic application. It does not mean the provider has stopped billing. Committed edits do not disappear automatically because of a stop.

**The Node-side barrier** is the task's cancellation request: `jobs.cancel`, and the cancellation that `tasks.stop` sends to child Jobs. The application checks it once before validation and once before submission:

- If it has already stopped, it is not submitted. The application is marked `cancelled` (`APPLICATION_CANCELLED`), the task is `cancelled`, and the artifacts are kept as candidates for the user to apply with `jobs.reconcile apply`.
- A submission that stops only after the Node-side check is rejected by the engine-side barrier. If the engine has already linearized it, it completes as usual and returns a receipt.
- After a restart, a task submitted by an agent is not applied automatically, because its Run ended with the restart. Look up the receipt first. If it was never submitted, mark it `cancelled`.

**The engine-side barrier** covers only the commits that task and fixed-workflow runs make to write video. Ordinary edits by users and agents, and `jobs.reconcile apply`, are not affected by it.

- These commits (`edits.apply`) carry `run { runId, runGeneration }`.
- On cancellation, before any waiting, the Runtime sends `runs.invalidate { videoId, runId, runGeneration }` over the private channel. The Engine Host records the generation to which this execution is invalidated on this video. The record only grows, never shrinks.
- The Engine Host checks at the video's serial commit entry. If the submitted generation is not greater than the invalidated generation and the command has no receipt, it rejects with `TASK_STOPPED`, opens no transaction and writes no receipt. A command that already has a receipt still returns the original receipt, so idempotency is unaffected.
- Node records `TASK_STOPPED` as application `cancelled` (`APPLICATION_CANCELLED`, `details.barrier: 'engine'`). It is not `rejected`: no receipt lookup and no retry.
- The Engine Host handles requests one at a time, in order. So when `runs.invalidate` is acknowledged, every commit handed to the engine earlier already has a result, and any later commit is rejected. `tasks.stop` answers only after the barrier of child Jobs is confirmed, waiting at most 5 seconds. When the engine is stuck, it stops waiting, and later commits have only the Node-side barrier.
- The invalidation records are bounded. They are cleared when the video is closed, each video keeps at most 1024 executions, and the oldest are dropped when exceeded. Nothing is recorded while the video is not open, because a closed video receives no commits.
- When the Engine Host restarts, invalidation records are lost and it falls back to the Node-side barrier. The new Runtime does not re-register invalidations.

**The three facts of cancellation** are recorded in the task record's `cancellation` (`JobCancellation`). The UI and CLI show them separately and do not merge them into one “cancelled”:

- `localStoppedAt`: the moment the system stopped waiting locally.
- `remote`:
  - `not-applicable`: local computation, or the result has already been obtained;
  - `not-submitted`: not yet sent;
  - `cancelled`: the node confirmed it stopped;
  - `cancel-unsupported`: an online provider, where disconnecting the request does not mean the provider has stopped;
  - `unknown`: not known.
- `cost`:
  - `none`;
  - `possible`: may already have been billed;
  - `charged`: the result has been obtained and the cost has been incurred.

Secondary actions:

| Action | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| `agents.interrupt` | Stops only the current reply. Creates no barrier and does not cancel the Job |
| `jobs.cancel` | Cancels one computation |
| Stop and undo | Establishes the barrier first, then compensates item by item with verification; does not roll back the whole old video |
| Pause | Offered only for tasks that truly support safe resumption |

### 7.5 Reconciliation and recovery matrix

| Lost point | Recovery behaviour | Forbidden |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Job not yet claimed | Re-check the Task and permissions before re-queueing | Executing an old Run that has already been stopped |
| Local segmented task has a valid checkpoint | Verify input and state, then continue | Treating partial intermediate files as a complete result |
| Local inference or encoding has no recovery capability | Mark `interrupted`, allow a new attempt | Pretending it can be paused and resumed |
| Model Worker crashes | Mark the current Job `interrupted`; automatic retry bounded per §6.5; above the threshold, disable the model package | Endlessly relaunching the same crashing model; treating partial segment results as a complete transcript |
| Cloud submitted, has a task ID | Query the remote, then resume | Automatically re-purchasing |
| Cloud submission result unknown, and not queryable | `needs-reconciliation`, decided by the user | Treating a timeout as never submitted |
| Remote node lost | Reconnect for 60 seconds; if still failing, the task fails with `REMOTE_NODE_LOST`, offering “Use this machine instead” | Automatically switching to another machine or to the local machine |
| Artifact published, not yet applied | Reuse the artifact and re-verify the Application | Regenerating and billing again |
| Video submitted, receipt response lost | Query the original receipt with the same idempotency key | Adding subtitles or clips again |
| Unclear whether the agent accepted the input | Query the native session, or mark as uncertain | Blindly resending the same instruction |

Service recovery, session recovery and task recovery are measured separately. Cancellation involves three facts — locally no longer waiting, remotely cancelled, cost incurred — which are shown separately (§7.4).

**Implementation**: On startup, read the ledger and handle each task according to where it stopped. Those that must wait until authorization and model services are ready (re-queue, complete the application, query the remote) run after startup.

| Where it stopped | Handling |
| --- | --- |
| Queued, not claimed (transcription and generation submitted by a connection, the Runtime or an external service) | The old reservation is released. Reopen the video at the recorded position, read the material and check its content, re-select the Provider, re-admit authorization and budget, and once passed re-queue with the same attempt. If the input is gone, it is `failed` (`STALE_JOB_INPUT`); if the Provider, authorization or budget no longer holds at this moment, it is `interrupted` |
| Queued, but the reservation was already recorded as started (crash after “started” is written to disk and before `running` is recorded) | Handle as “result unknown” below: `needs-reconciliation`, budget settled conservatively, no re-queue |
| Queued, agent submission | `interrupted` |
| Running locally or on a node | `interrupted` |
| Running on an online or agent Provider, with a queryable remote task ID | Query the remote: `failed` → failed; `not-found` (request did not arrive) → `interrupted`; otherwise `needs-reconciliation`. Only the interface (`RemoteTaskQuery`, §14) |
| Running on an online or agent Provider, result unknown | `needs-reconciliation` (`JOB_NEEDS_RECONCILIATION`). The reservation is settled by the conservative rule: a call that began execution counts once, with the estimate deducted, the same as `conservative` in the authorization ledger (§7.8). Never automatically resent |
| Artifact published, application not finished | Reuse the artifact; first query the receipt by `commandId`, then complete the application (§7.3) |
| Artifact written to the artifact library, result not recorded in the ledger (task carries a publish intent) | Verify the artifact, then claim it, same as the row above; if the artifact is incomplete, drop the intent and handle according to where the task stopped (§7.3) |
| Fixed-flow parent tasks and steps; tasks not run through a model (export, model and tool installation, voice cloning); tasks executed on behalf of a remote node; transcription without a video | `interrupted` |

A normal stop (§2.4) likewise leaves no pending records:

- Queued → `interrupted`; on the next startup it is not re-queued;
- Running local and node tasks → `interrupted`;
- Running online calls → `needs-reconciliation`, because disconnecting the request does not mean the provider did not execute it; those the user cannot retry (last row of the table above) remain `interrupted`, and the budget is still settled according to whether they started.

**`jobs.reconcile`** has exactly three decisions, a closed set. An invalid request is rejected with `conflict` (`RECONCILE_NOT_ALLOWED`; `details` gives `state`, `decision` and `allowed`).

| Decision | Valid states | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `retry` | `needs-reconciliation` or `interrupted` transcription with a video, and generation (not fixed-flow steps, nor node-executed ones) | The same task returns to `queued`, `attempt` plus one, and is re-admitted: this is a new call and a new reservation; the previous settlement stays in `grant.retries`. When there is a video, the video must already be open (`VIDEO_NOT_OPEN`) |
| `discard` | `needs-reconciliation` | `cancelled`; the online call's `cancellation` is `remote: unknown`, `cost: possible`. The conservatively deducted budget is not refunded |
| `apply` | `failed` or `cancelled`, with a video and result, and the most recent application was `stale-input`, `rejected` or `cancelled` | First query the receipt by the most recent `commandId`; only if never submitted, create one new application, re-verify it, then submit. The video must already be open |

There is no “mark as submitted / not submitted”: the user's judgement cannot be verified, so it is not used to refund budget or replay the request.

Reconciliation is decided only by the user. Entry points:

- Agent: `jobs_inspect` shows the state, `applications` and `cancellation`, but there is no reconciliation tool (§3.12);
- MCP server: likewise has no reconciliation tool;
- Web service: exposed per `jobs.*`, rejected when read-only;
- CLI: `cuccut jobs reconcile <jobId> retry|discard|apply`.

### 7.6 Scheduling and resources

Rendering, speech recognition, speech synthesis, image generation and browser sessions share the same `ResourceScheduler`. Admission is controlled by CPU, memory, GPU, disk and download, and external concurrency limits. Mutually independent tasks may run in parallel; video transactions are short and ordered, and do not hold the video lock while waiting on the network.

Foreground preview has priority, but preemption is not assumed to be forceable: only executors that support safe interruption are preempted. Model and browser instances are reused according to compatibility and security domain; reusing an instance that holds private state across users, or across incompatible authorizations, is forbidden. Use bounded frame windows, backpressure, explicit surface release and idle reclamation; do not launch browsers in proportion to CPU core count.

Currently connected to the scheduler are the tasks in JobManager: local transcription and model package checks, finished-video and audio export, model download, and steps in fixed flows (§7.9) that declare requirements (currently file transcoding). The rest (Render Worker's code sessions, browser sessions, image generation) are not yet connected. Rules:

- **Queue concurrency is a constraint of scheduling**. Each Provider or executor queue (same model package, same online Provider, same remote node) keeps its order and does not exceed its concurrency limit; no separate concurrency system is set up. Calls to online Providers and remote nodes are constrained only by this one constraint and do not count local resources.
- **Capacity** uses only the Node standard library: memory `os.totalmem()`, CPU threads `os.availableParallelism()`, free space on the staging volume `fs.statfs` (refreshed in the background at most every 2 seconds; admission reads only the cached value). On Apple chips, the GPU-usable amount is estimated from unified memory (at 32 GiB and below, 2/3 of memory; above that, 3/4), and GPU demand is counted against memory at the same time. On other machines GPU reports unknown and admission does not use it. The preference `resources.capacity` (§5.10) can override memory, GPU memory and thread count, and takes effect immediately on change.
- **Reservations**: the system reserve is used by no one (25% of memory, at least 2 GiB; 1 thread when there are more than 4; 1 GiB of disk). The interactive reserve is not used by background work (1 GiB of memory, 512 MiB of GPU memory, a quarter of the thread count, at most 2).
- **Interactive first**: tasks carry priority `interactive` or `background` (default). Interactive tasks may use the interactive reserve and are placed ahead of all background waiters; they do not preempt running work. Currently only model package checks are interactive.

### 7.7 Peak working-set admission, leases and actual resource release

`ExecutionAdmission` estimates, from the frozen RenderGraph, the code sessions, decoding surfaces, memory and GPU, disk staging and external concurrency that are **simultaneously active at peak**, and returns executable, awaitable, or unsatisfiable together with the reason.

- Nine sequential scenes can be executed segment by segment in a pool of capacity 2, without requiring nine sessions to be leased first.
- When a transition requires two scenes to be active at the same time, admit by the overlapping working set, or choose a verified intermediate-clip approach.
- Do not acquire half the resources and then wait indefinitely for the other half, which would form a deadlock.

**Lease** contains: the owner's instance and worker generation, the resource dimension, the limit, the actual allocation, the TTL, and the renewal state.

- TTL expiry only blocks new allocations and starts cleanup. Browser or GPU resources that have not yet exited still count as occupied / quarantined.
- Only after confirming that the process has exited or the resource handle has actually been released is that portion allowed to be reallocated. Issuing a release request does not mean the resource has been returned.

**Fairness**: preview guarantee, aging of background tasks, cancellable waits, idle cache eviction. A waiting export must not block preview permanently, and preview must not occupy resources indefinitely. Priority does not mean an executing inference can be safely forcibly preempted; tasks that do not support pausing release at a natural boundary.

Disk space is checked before costly generation or export. The budget is only an estimate; during execution it must still be monitored, and failures must be safe.

The creation, queuing, invalidation, exit and held leases of each browser instance are traceable. After a loss of contact, a new Runtime does not reuse unverified old process handles, nor does it claim a process based on PID alone.

Admission is a resource mechanism and must not be used to bypass task budgets and user authorization.

Current implementation (`ResourceScheduler`, in `packages/jobs`):

- **Requirements** are declared across four dimensions: memory, GPU memory, CPU threads, staging disk. Peak estimates for each task type are centralized in `resource-profiles.ts`; they are conservative initial values, pending calibration (§14). CPU thread demand is capped at the number of threads available to background work; it only limits concurrency and is never rejected.
- **Admission**: a task starts only when its queue has a free slot and all dimensions fit, and then obtains a lease. When it does not fit, it queues, and `JobRecord.wait` states what it is waiting for (`concurrency` or `resources`, the missing dimension, and how many are ahead), pushed with `jobs.updated`. Tasks that must queue at submission already carry `wait` in the first `queued` record pushed. When a single requirement exceeds the most this priority can use (capacity minus reserve), it fails immediately with error code `RESOURCE_ADMISSION_UNSATISFIABLE`; `details.dimensions` lists the missing dimensions, and it does not wait indefinitely. Export, model download and model package checks are rejected at submission (`conflict`, with the same error code); transcription fails at admission; fixed-flow steps fail at step admission. The disk limit is computed from the most recent `statfs` plus the amount leased since, so that one's own staging does not cause a false over-limit.
- **No queue-jumping, no half-acquisition**: admission acquires all dimensions at once. Work waiting on some dimension blocks later-arriving work that also needs those dimensions; work that touches none of those dimensions may start first. Within one queue, strict ordering applies.
- **Shared process (holder)**: one model package's Model Worker is used by successive tasks of the same model package, and its amount is counted only once. The task's lease and the process each hold a share. While the process is still alive after a task ends, it still counts; it is returned only when the process actually exits (child process `close`). An idle holder with no task using it is evicted least-recently-used (unloading that model package) when work waiting on a dimension it occupies exists and no waiter needs it.
- **Engine processes not via Worker**: local music generation (§6.1) starts one audio.cpp process per task, not resident, with no holder. Its peak is taken from the estimate registered for the model package (YuE2 Q8 plus F16 VAE, about 10 GiB, `peakEstimated`) and added directly to the task; on Apple Silicon it counts as GPU memory, on other platforms as memory, plus 256 MiB of staging. It is returned after the process exits.
- **Lease release** occurs after execution truly ends: a lease is returned only when the task's execution function returns and the Provider has stopped (on cancel, the Model Worker is killed and exits, and the export's process group is confirmed nonexistent). The Runtime waits for all executions to finish when it stops. For an abnormally exited or cancelled export worker, the whole process group is killed first (also when the worker exits on its own during cancel, since it may leave ffmpeg behind), and it counts as finished only after confirming all processes in the group have exited (waiting at most 5 seconds). Fixed-flow steps are admitted before the step starts and returned after the step ends (including failure and cancellation); the waiting reason is written to both the step and the parent task.
- **Visibility**: `jobs.resources` returns capacity (and its source), reserves, the amount leased out, the available amounts for both priorities, leases, holders and waiting queues. CLI: `cuccut jobs resources`. The agent's `jobs_inspect` summary carries `waiting`, and when waiting on resources it advises not to resubmit or to cancel and start over.
- **Not yet implemented**: aging of background tasks (interactive tasks always stay ahead), TTL and renewal (leases are only within this process, and are returned when execution ends), estimates based on the frozen RenderGraph.

### 7.8 Budget admission

Budgets cover candidates, retries and parallel Jobs alike. Before scheduling, atomically reserve in the ledger this call's verifiable amount or call count; release the balance after settlement. Parallel tasks must not each see the same remaining budget and overspend.

When there is no reliable upper bound on the amount, offer only “批准这一次明确的调用，金额未知” (approve this one explicit call, amount unknown) or block the call. Never claim that a hard amount cap exists.

**Ledger.** Budgets are recorded on the Grant (§12.5). The ledger lives with the Grant in the Runtime Home at `store/grants.json` (0600). There are two measures: call count (`maxCalls`, which may be unlimited) and amount (`budgetCap`, present only on `estimate-cap` Grants). Amounts are estimated only from the prices in the model description (`ModelPrice`: amount, currency and pricing unit); nothing is invented. Only units that allow an upper bound to be computed at submission time (per request, per image, and per thousand characters) can be estimated. Models priced per minute, per token, or with no price cannot be estimated. Amounts are decimal strings. The ledger computes them as integers in millionths, not floating point. Different currencies cannot be estimated.

**Admission.** Online and agent Provider Jobs are admitted through `JobAdmission` in the same step in which `JobManager` creates the record: match a Grant, check quota, and reserve one call plus the estimated amount. The whole process is synchronous with no waits in between, so parallel submissions cannot each see the same balance (test: with a cap of 3 calls, 8 parallel submissions yield exactly 3 successes). Local models and LAN nodes do not go through the ledger. When admission fails, the submission is rejected with an error and the Job is not created:

| Error code | Category | Meaning | Remedy (`details.remedy`) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `GRANT_REQUIRED` | `forbidden` | No Grant covers this outbound transfer | User issues a Grant, or approves this one time in the session |
| `GRANT_REVOKED` | `forbidden` | The covering Grant was revoked, expired, or tightened while queued | User re-authorizes; not restored automatically |
| `BUDGET_EXCEEDED` | `conflict` | Count or amount is insufficient for this call (`details.measure`) | User raises the limit, or waits for in-flight calls to settle |
| `BUDGET_UNVERIFIABLE` | `conflict` | The Grant has an amount cap, but this model's amount cannot be estimated | User approves each call (amount unknown), or switches to a per-request Grant |

`details` also carries the recipient, data kind, video, Grant and usage, plus CLI commands that can be copied as-is. Agent tool results carry `next`, which asks the agent to relay the remedy to the user rather than switch Provider to bypass the check.

**Start and settlement.** Before a Job actually starts execution (when data is handed over), re-check the Grant's generation (§12.5). On mismatch, the Job fails with `GRANT_REVOKED`, the reservation is released, and no data is handed over. After the check passes, the reservation is marked “started” and durably written to the authorization ledger (§7.3) before data is handed over. If the write to disk fails, the Job fails with `INTERNAL`, the reservation is released, and again no data is handed over. On finish, the reservation is settled according to `grant.settled.basis` recorded on the Job record:

- `released`: ended before it started (cancelled while queued, revoked before start, Runtime stopped); not counted;
- `reported`: completed, and the service reported the cost; counted as reported;
- `estimate`: completed, and the service did not report a cost; counted as the reserved estimate (when there is an amount cap);
- `unknown`: completed, amount unknown; counted by calls only;
- `conservative`: already started, then failed, cancelled or interrupted. The data may have been handed over and may have been billed; counted as one call plus the reserved estimate.
