- **Parameters**. Targets are as above in “Tools”: `{ videoId }`, `{ entryId }` or `{ create: { projectId, name?, media } }` (name defaults to the file name). Alternatively, give no target and only `file` (an absolute path to a local media file, or `{ entryId }` of a media entry in Space). In that case optional `outDir` (see “Save location” above) may be given, and `captions` may not. Optional: `assetId`, `language` (an assertion), `hint` (recognition hint, up to 1200 characters; the video's enabled transcription glossary is still merged in), `provider`, `model`, `captions` (default true). When the target video already has a transcript: `destination` (`'new-video' | 'replace'`, default `new-video`; the tool and CLI call it `target`, §3.5), `name` (name of the new video, used only for `new-video`), `translations` (`'carry' | 'discard'`, default `carry`, used only for `replace`), and `acceptEdited` (passes the manual-edit gate, §6.6). These last few are paired only with `{ videoId }` and `{ entryId }` targets. The provider and model are selected and frozen at submission. If transcription is not configured, the request fails with `CAPABILITY_NOT_CONFIGURED`; if `media` is not an absolute path, `invalid-request`; if the file is missing, `INPUT_NOT_FOUND`. None of these creates a task. A link is not its input: go through link import first.
- **Asset**. Without `assetId`, the asset on the root sequence's main track is taken. The main track is the picture track with the smallest `order` that has audio/video clips; if there is none, the audio track. If more than one asset is on the main track, this transcription step fails with `TRANSCRIBE_ASSET_AMBIGUOUS` (`details.assetIds` lists them). If there is none, it fails with `TRANSCRIBE_ASSET_NOT_FOUND`; give `assetId` and run again.
- **Steps**. With a target: `target` → (when creating) `create` → `transcribe` → `captions`. The transcribe Job itself applies the new `speech` document to the video (§7.3), so “apply” is inside transcribe and there is no separate step. The new video's Provenance records `origin: 'file-import'`, the file path and this run. When the target video already has a transcript and `destination` is `new-video`, `create` still runs: a new video is created in the original video's project (or the conversation's source directory), named by default “<original name> · Re-transcribed”. The target asset is imported as a linked file and placed on the timeline, and the following steps operate on the new video. When the target video has no transcript, this is skipped and the transcript is written into it directly. `replace` switching is done in the `transcribe` step: applying the transcribe Job's result is the replace-transcript transaction of §6.6 (new version, switch, carry-over). The manual-edit gate is checked again before applying. The caption layer has already been re-cut within this transaction, so the `captions` step is `skipped` under the existing caption-layer rules; a caption layer that did not exist before is built as usual. File-only: a single `transcribe` step (a file transcription Job). After transcription, in the same step, `<source file name>.txt` and `.srt` are written to the save location, using the same implementation as the `transcribe` step of link import. On a name clash both files get sequence numbers together; a retry recognises existing results and does not overwrite them. The result is a generated record without a video, shown in Space as two entries, a document and subtitles (the artifact record in the file flow has one additional `transcribe`). The UI's “Create video from this” pairs the subtitles with the media via `space.openForEdit`.
- **Landing**. When a video already has a transcript, `destination` decides between creating a new video and switching the transcript; it never adds a second copy to this video. With `replace`, if the transcript's full-text fingerprint does not match `stages.asr` and there is no `acceptEdited`, the request is rejected at submission with `TRANSCRIPT_EDITED` and no task is created. If the transcript is edited after submission but before application, the `transcribe` step fails with it (`acceptEdited` covers only the fingerprint at submission). In the summary, `target` is `new-video`, `replace` or `first` (no transcript before; written directly in). `newVideo { videoId, name }` is the new video. `replaced { documentId, previousVersion, transactionId }` is the transcript version before switching and the undoable transaction. `translations[] { language, kept, keptReviewed, stale, unmatched }`, `captionPins { reanchored, orphaned }` and `dubs[] { language, kept, stale }` are counts from the switch receipt's `impact`. The UI uses them to write the result, and to offer “Refresh stale translations” and undo. The summary's `speakerCount` is the number of speakers distinguished in the new transcript (taken from the document version summary); the transcription tool's result page reports how many speakers were distinguished.
- **Retries do not duplicate**. Transcription reclaims Jobs submitted by this run that are still running, or completed while the document still exists. If the document was deleted, transcription is redone. Each retry submits a transcribe Job with a new `commandId`, so it does not reclaim the one that failed. Creation, import and transcription in link import use the same implementation as new-video-from-file.
- **Caption layer** (product design S01). One edit transaction: it writes one `caption` document (derived from that transcript or translation; `extensions.pipeline` records this run) and places a caption instance on the root sequence, applying to the clips on the timeline that use this asset. When the same transcript (or translation) already has a caption layer, the root sequence having an instance that references a caption document derived from it (those made by the editor and by the flow count alike), this step is `skipped`. If no clip on the timeline uses this asset, nothing is built and `CAPTIONS_NOT_ON_TIMELINE` is warned. If no caption cues can be cut, `CAPTIONS_EMPTY` is warned. The original-language caption layer goes on the first empty, unlocked caption track. When the asset already shows an original caption layer, the new one is placed disabled (see “No overwrite” above). For a translation, as with the editor's “Put on picture”: a new caption track is created in this language. When only the translation is shown, the paired original caption layer is disabled (not deleted); when bilingual, both layers share one caption style and the original is put back on picture. The agent's `captions_create` (§3.5) uses the same algorithm, with these differences: when a caption layer already exists it does not skip, but still creates a new layer (“No overwrite”); when a layer of the same translation is already showing, the new layer is placed disabled and the paired original is left untouched; the caption document does not write `extensions.pipeline`; for a translation without Worker cues, cues are cut by sentence-level alignment of units (`alignment.sourceWordIds`), taking the times of the original sentence's member words and splitting by display width (`translationCues`, a port of the editor function of the same name).
- **New style**. `transcribe` and `translate` may give `captionStyle` (the Studio style document of video format spec §5.6, with `schema` and object `style`), usable only when building a caption layer; it cannot be given for transcription from a file only. The editor freezes the caption properties saved by the client into this parameter at submission. It is applied when creating a new style and saved in the same transaction as the captions. When a translation shares the existing original style, the video style is kept and not overridden by preferences. Without the parameter, the default preset is used.
- **Entry**. The CLI and agents use the `transcribe` tool in the directory (derived per §3.6, `cuccut transcribe`). The editor's “Generate captions” and “Re-transcribe” also go through this flow (`{ videoId, assetId }` with the user's chosen language, provider, model and destination), and report the result by the summary's `captions`. When `created`, undo uses the `transactionId`. Generating captions from a transcript already in the video does not go through it.

---

## 8. Code Runtime

Code composition lets agents and authors make animations with p5.js, Three.js, Canvas, Remotion and similar technologies, and take part in the composition as an instance on the timeline. The code bundle manifest, instance fields and author contract are in the code bundle spec; this chapter describes the runtime mechanism.

### 8.1 Two kinds of composition

- A nested sequence references CucCut's own structured sequence, which can still be edited element by element (not yet included in the instance types; video format spec §3.4).
- Code composition (`composition` instance, sourced from a code bundle) references an author environment and its code bundle. CucCut only understands instance properties, public parameters and the execution contract.

The durable type set does not add one instance type per JavaScript library. Browser code uniformly declares `engine: 'browser'`, and uses `contract` to distinguish the CucCut Web Adapter, HyperFrames and others; `frameworkHints` is only for development hints. Remotion uses a separate `engine`. This way, adding a new graphics library does not require upgrading the instance type set of the whole video.

### 8.2 Authoring workspace and publishing

Code drafts live in a separate authoring workspace. The publishing steps are: build → check → sample verification → compute digest and register as an immutable version → transactionally switch the specified instances from the old version to the new version.

- Saving files in a draft does not directly change the published code bundle being exported.
- Building a draft is not a live Video, and file watching does not automatically overwrite a published code bundle.
- Source publishing, video application and cache invalidation are three separate events.

### 8.3 Adapter interface

```ts
interface CompositionAdapter {
  inspect(bundle: CodeBundleManifest): Promise<VerifiedCapabilities>;
  prepare(input: FrozenCompositionInput, signal: AbortSignal): Promise<Session>;
  sampleVisual(session: Session, request: SampleRequest): Promise<FrameSurface>;
  renderVisualRange?(session: Session, request: RangeRequest): Promise<RenderArtifact>;
  renderAudioRange?(session: Session, request: AudioRangeRequest): Promise<AudioArtifact>;
  dispose(session: Session): Promise<void>;
}
```

- `SampleRequest` contains the exact `localTime`, output size, sampling policy, `quality` and `requestGeneration`. It must not be a bare floating-point seconds value with no context.
- `VerifiedCapabilities` contains at least: the random-access mode, transparency, audio, independently outputtable layers, supported sampling and encoding, the GPU capability actually available, and sandbox status. When the declaration and the measurement disagree, reject or downgrade the capability; do not trust the manifest blindly.
- `FrameSurface` contains size, valid region, PTS, `pixelFormat`, `colorSpace`, `alphaMode`, and a controlled binary handle. A GPU texture is valid only within the negotiated device and process-sharing contract; do not hand a WebGLTexture object directly to Rust. P0 allows CPU RGBA or cached intermediate segments; zero-copy is not a prerequisite.

In the first phase, `packages/code-runtime` (`code-runtime` in §13.1) implements a subset: `VerifiedCapabilities` only covers the contract, random access, transparency, audio, actual width, height, frame rate and duration, the execution environment (Electron off-screen window), and whether network access is actually intercepted. Independently outputtable layers, sampling and encoding, and GPU capability are not yet measured.

### 8.4 FrameTicket, ready handshake and serial execution per page

```ts
interface FrameTicket {
  requestId: Id;
  requestGeneration: string;
  sessionGeneration: string;
  snapshotHash: string;
  itemId: Id;
  bundleRef: VersionRef;
  parametersHash: string;
  timeMapHash: string;
  sampleDomain: 'sequence' | 'export';
  sequenceId: Id;
  editFps: Rate;
  sequenceTime: MediaTime;
  localTime: MediaTime;
  outputSample?: {
    frameIndex: number;
    fps: Rate;
    time: MediaTime;          // relative to export start
    exportRangeStart: MediaTime;
  };
  samplingPolicyHash: string;
  quality: 'draft' | 'interactive' | 'export-exact';
  outputProfileHash: string;
}
```

**Ready handshake.** The Adapter session first waits for dependencies, fonts, textures and initial media; each frame request then waits for the target media to actually decode and present, and for the graphics to actually commit. The resolve of `renderAt` must satisfy the verified presentation contract; it must not merely mean that variables have been updated or that a seek has been issued. The surface receipt carries back the ticket identity, the actual sampled time, complete color and alpha information, and metadata for bytes or handles.

An implementation that relies on "wait two requestAnimationFrame calls" can only be a candidate waiting strategy. Until it passes media and texture readiness tests, Export-Exact must not be opened.

**Serial execution.** Frame fetches on the same page execute serially; parallelism comes from mutually isolated sessions, or from a proven stateless executor. A new interactive seek may retire old requests and old returns that have not yet started; export ranges must not drop frames. A `sequential` source may only advance along legal replay or checkpoint progress. Do not rebuild the browser and the video for every frame.

**End-of-range behaviour** must be chosen explicitly: take the available frame, hold, or report out-of-range. By default, a request beyond the source duration is not automatically treated as a successful hold. The allowed time error comes from the exact video and asset timebase and the chosen sampling policy, not from a fixed tolerance constant; high-frame-rate scenarios must verify that the last frame and its neighbouring frames can be told apart.

**First-phase subset** (`packages/code-runtime`). `FrameTicket` only has `requestId`, `bundleRef`, `compositionId`, `localTime`, `frameIndex`, `fps`, `width`, `height`, `parametersHash` and `quality`. `FrameReceipt` carries back the actual sampled time and the requested seconds, whether it was clamped to the duration, and the sha256 of the frame bytes. Capture waits, beyond the Adapter's two animation frames, for the off-screen window's `paint` event after `invalidate()`, and then takes the frame with `capturePage` (the image carried by the `paint` event may lag by one frame, so it is not used directly). This is still the candidate waiting strategy described above, and the restriction on Export-Exact is unchanged. Frame fetch requests for the same session execute serially; sampled frames are compared by sha256 as evidence of determinism. The end-of-range behaviour of `hyperframes/1` and the seek readback tolerance are in code bundle spec §4.7: the tolerance is half a frame at the sampling frame rate, where the frame rate is the `fps` of the frame ticket (or the bundle's `intrinsic.fps` when there is no ticket), not a fixed constant.

### 8.5 Mixed frame rates and exact local time

`FrameTicket.sequenceTime` and `localTime` are the truth of the request. When `sampleDomain='export'`, `outputSample` is required; when `sampleDomain='sequence'`, no output frame is fabricated. The receiver verifies that the fields are consistent and does not accept multiple mutually contradictory clocks.

- Continuous browser animation and native animation are evaluated at the exact `localTime`.
- Frame-driven code uses `Bundle.intrinsic.fps` to select the local frame; the Adapter returns `actualLocalTime`, the actual source frame or PTS, and `samplingPolicy`.
- The `fixedStep` of `sequential` / `checkpointed` is the simulation step rate and does not change with the output frame rate; output sampling only decides which instants are read.
- Frame-driven third-party interval interfaces convert between half-open and closed interval rules only at the boundary.
- A video frame or output frame must not be used directly as a Remotion composition frame, and the frame rate of a published code bundle must not be rewritten to support a new export frame rate.
- Whether interpolation, hold or nearest is supported must be verified by capability; when it is not supported, reject or explicitly downgrade.

### 8.6 SceneRecipe and BuildLedger

`SceneRecipe` organises the author's intent for a group of scenes: the stable scene ID, target sequence and instances, narrative or voice anchors, author entry points, parameters, shared dependencies, time policy, and resources to be generated. `BuildLedger` stores each build's input hashes, toolchain, output code bundle, verification report and application receipt.

```text
TaskContract / NarrationCueSheet
  → SceneRecipe (author intent)
  → isolated build + smoke / seek / audio checks
  → immutable BundleRevision
  → replaceCodeBundle transaction on the selected instances
  → receipt / preview / evidence
```

- A SceneRecipe is not a writable master timeline. The position of a published instance is determined by Video.
- Replacing a scene by default keeps `itemId`, lineage, external subtitle anchors and the user's pin. "Delete the old clip and batch-create new ones" is not the regular update path; a mapping is established explicitly only when the structure truly changes.
- A change to a shared source, font or theme causes the code bundles that reference it to be rebuilt or republished. When the author asks to change only one scene, update the selected references and leave the other scenes on their existing version.

**Time dependencies must be declared.** Splitting scenes into several independent bundles does not prove that all caches can be reused after an instance moves: scene code may read its own start on the master timeline, or read the global clock. Code bundles therefore declare verified `timeDependencies` (code bundle spec §5). The frozen `CompositionInput` must contain the values and versions of these dependencies. Moving a `local-only` instance reuses the internal frame-fetch results; moving an instance that depends on placement or cue re-evaluates it. When a dependency is undeclared or unverified, invalidate conservatively; do not infer local impact from the code file's hash.

### 8.7 CreativeCatalogue

`CreativeCatalogue` lets the UI and agents retrieve the same batch of verified native templates and code recipes. Entries include: a stable `templateId`, type, tags, parameter schema, language and aspect-ratio variants, required capabilities, dependent assets, licence status and `previewRef`.

- `previewRef` must bind to the exact template or code bundle version and its parameters; an old preview must not be paired with new code.
- The first batch produces only a small number of original or explicitly licensed P0 templates, used to verify the contract for public parameters, landscape and portrait versions, audio and offline dependencies. Template count is not a goal for the first release.
- When licence status is unknown, do not claim it is commercially usable.
- A template change produces a new version; instances already using it are not upgraded automatically.
- When a dependency is missing or a version is incompatible, disable selection or offer a clear substitute.

### 8.8 Scope of adaptation

Third-party interface names in this text are integration references, not guarantees of current availability. The implementation must lock the versions of the framework, browser and renderer, and open features only after obtaining `VerifiedCapabilities`.

- **P0**: import, verification, frame fetch and baking for the controlled Web contract (`cuccut/1`) and the HyperFrames contract (`hyperframes/1`); the execution environment is an Electron off-screen window. p5 / Three / Canvas are integrated according to the supported subset of each. The HyperFrames contract enters the first phase; it is the plan recommendation adopted when the user began the implementation task on 2026-10-07, not an earlier ruling.
- **P1**: import of external Remotion code; richer independently outputtable layers; checkpoints; a Playwright or headless Chromium execution environment. Successful template rendering must not be inflated into support for all existing code.
- **Remotion is recognised, not produced.** CucCut's built-in templates, authoring guidance and CreativeCatalogue do not use Remotion; agents write code against the browser contract by default. Code bundles with `engine: 'remotion'` that the user requests, that an agent writes, or that are imported from outside must be recognised, previewable and renderable. CucCut does not distribute Remotion: the Adapter uses the Remotion that the code bundle itself depends on, and licensing is the responsibility of the code bundle's author (code bundle spec §4.6).

Changes to instances, parameters and source are always kept separate in the UI, in commands and in receipts.

---

## 9. Rendering, Preview and Export

### 9.1 Unified compilation, per-backend execution

```text
Frozen Video + selected language version
              ↓ validate / resolve dependencies
       RenderComposition / RenderGraph
              ├─ native media / text / subtitles / shapes / effects
              ├─ nested sequences
              ├─ code composition → adapter → frame / segment cache
              └─ audio routing → sampled timeline
              ↓
       compositor + mixer
              ↓
       encode / mux → verified artifact
```

The VideoStore format and the RenderGraph are different layers. The RenderGraph is a derived execution plan containing explicit dependencies, active intervals, timeMap, render order and backend selection; users do not maintain it.

**The frame plan** is a snapshot of the RenderGraph at one instant: which layers stack into the picture, how each layer is placed, the moment of its source it samples, and which sounds are audible and at what gain. It has exactly one implementation (`render-graph`); the editor preview calls it via WASM, and export links it directly. The plan's fields are only added, never changed; consumers ignore fields they do not recognise. `layers` itself is always the result of hard cuts: a compositor that does not recognise the new fields draws a picture with "no transition, no effect" without erroring. The compositor consumes transitions, effects and ducking as follows; for the semantics see video format spec §3.5 and §3.9:

- A layer's `transition` indicates that at this moment it is inside a transition: `role` is whether it is on the outgoing or incoming side, `partner` is the complete layer on the other side (its own transform, `sourceRect`, source moment and effects), and `eased` is the progress used for the picture. Compose the two layers at this layer's position according to `kind` and `params`. A one-sided transition has no `partner`; the missing side is transparent. Layers carrying `unsupported` are drawn as hard cuts, and reported.
- A layer's `effects` apply in order; disabled ones are not included. Layers carrying `unsupported` are skipped and reported, not silently. Cropping is already folded into `sourceRect` and placement; the compositor does not handle it separately.
- A sound's `gainDb` already includes crossfade and ducking; use it directly. `unduckedGainDb` is the value before ducking, used by the UI to show how much it was lowered; sounds carrying `transitionId` are currently crossfading.
- Chapters do not enter the frame plan.

Settled: the picture has only one implementation, ported from the v2 render core (`ccut-render` and its element layer, subtitle rendering and the motion library); preview and export share it. The frame plan decides which layers exist at this moment and the moment each one samples its source; each layer is compiled from its element parameters into a DrawOp instruction stream, and the rasteriser replays the instruction stream to obtain the picture. Export links this implementation directly; preview calls the same one via WASM, so no separate drawing path is written. The DrawOp instruction stream has a deterministic binary encoding and fingerprint; the fingerprint of the same frame must be identical for preview and export. v3's own shell is unchanged: freezing, pre-flight, the Worker process and protocol, staging and publishing (§9.11), rational time and the frame plan, and explicit negotiation of hardware encoding (§9.6).

Current state: preview and export are both drawn through `crates/frame-render`. It replaces each layer of the frame plan with an element of the render kernel (drawn through the element pipeline of `subtitle-render`, rasterised in `render-raster`): native export (`export-worker`) links it directly, and preview calls the same one via `bindings/preview-wasm` (`wasm-safe`, with SIMD enabled). The Canvas 2D drawing in `render-core` and in the UI has been deleted.

- What can be drawn: placement and geometry, keyframe binding, the three slots of element animation, masks and tiling, `fx` (the fixed order of video format spec §3.9), text styles and graphics, generative elements (timers, sound waves, progress bars, confetti, hand-drawn, placeholder boxes, template stickers and material stickers, Lottie), whiteboards, template layers, all transitions in spec §3.9, and subtitles (Studio-style and positioning-box-style, including display timing, word-by-word animation and the recipe for designed subtitles). By default, a subtitle block's lines align within the block's own width, and the block is centred on the anchor (the same as the Studio style in v2). When a positioning-box style is converted to a Studio style, `alignWithinWrap` of the root style is carried over: each line instead aligns by `textAlign` within the wrap width (box width minus the padding on both sides), so left-aligned lines stick to the left edge of the box and right-aligned lines to the right edge, and the backing plate follows the lines, the same as text elements. The words of a subtitle (video format spec §3.8) are taken by `frame_render::caption_words` from the transcript that the subtitle document derives from; whether words are kept or dropped follows the effective word stream (`render_graph::text_plan::plan_text`, where the acting instance is the instance that displays this subtitle, `scopeItemIds`). Sentences handed to the kernel keep the sentence IDs from the document; designed-subtitle emphasis (`captionEmphasis`) and per-sentence styles (`cueStyles`) are looked up by these IDs. Colliding sentence IDs within the same group get a suffix, while `sourceItemId` remains the ID in the document. Emphasis written on a multi-character word falls on each character it is split into. The sequence's template layers are handed to the subtitle plan only for avoidance (video format spec §3.17); the template itself is still drawn by the template layer. On export, the engine's `exports.plan` freezes this transcript into `documents` (the subtitle document carries `sourceDocumentId` and `sourceAssetId` in its document header); on preview, the UI sends the same one via `bc_set_speech`, and WASM merges it into the renderer's document. The compiled result of subtitles is cached per asset-using instance and subtitle instance; when the clip changes, it is recompiled.
- What cannot be drawn is reported layer by layer (`frame_render::support`; pre-flight and compositing query the same place; for the reasons see the export section of the command and protocol spec §4.4). It is not drawn as a placeholder box and not left blank: preview skips it and lists it in the preview's status, and export pre-flight rejects it or skips it according to the option. Kernel hints (for example skipped element parameters, or a sound wave with no spectrum) are listed in the same place in preview; on export they are `EXPORT_RENDER_NOTE` warnings.
- Fonts first use the set shipped with the kernel (`render-raster/assets/fonts`, 38 files, with Source Han Sans as fallback): the default export links it into the binary, and the macOS installer reads a shared resource instead. The preview WASM does not embed fonts; the build copies them to the UI, which reads them out in the same order and injects them. When a font family named by a style is not in this set, the machine's installed fonts are searched for that face (`crates/font-files`, ported from v2's local font library; system font directories; tests replace them with a temporary directory via the environment variable `CUCCUT_FONT_DIRS`). While typesetting, the named "family, weight, italic" combinations are recorded, and for the reported-missing families these faces are chosen with the same CSS font matching as the typesetting engine (family name exact first, then case-insensitive, then by PostScript name). Only what the picture actually uses is looked up; the whole font directory is neither listed nor uploaded. The chosen face is extracted as a separate font (a new file header carrying this face's tables only; other faces in the collection are not carried). Extractions larger than 96 MB are not loaded. Preview and export install the same bytes. For export, see "Fonts" in §9.11. For preview, WASM cannot read local fonts, so the UI hands the named faces of reported-missing families to the Runtime's `fonts.resolve` (command and protocol spec §4.1, up to 32 at a time). The engine host gives the file containing the face, its index, and the extraction method. On first use it scans the name tables of the local fonts (about 0.1–0.6 seconds on this machine); the engine host answers `fonts.resolve` on a separate thread, so other requests continue to be handled during the scan, and a late response is paired by `id`. The scan index stays in process and is not cached to disk (v2 does not cache it either). The Runtime issues a read handle; the UI fetches only the face's tables, by range (`Range`; only 206 responses whose length matches are accepted), concatenates them, injects them via `add_font`, and redraws. For a 78 MB PingFang collection of 24 faces, only the one face in use (about 13 MB) is downloaded and injected. Once bytes have been copied into WASM, the UI keeps no copy: after a kernel trap and reload, or when a thumbnail gets its own instance, the same face is requested again (until it is back, a reload does not count as complete). Local fonts that arrive later are also sent to thumbnail instances already split off. Each face is requested once. A family reported missing is recorded on the preview engine (a bold weight typeset later for the same family still requests it). When that family is later downloaded (by the font strip's "Download", a retry, or a download during export), it is requested again, so the picture switches over without reopening the video. While fetching, preview does not report this family as missing. A family found nowhere falls back under the fallback rules, and text elements and subtitles report the same notice in every preview frame and in the export result: "字体 "…" 在当前字体库中不可用，将使用 Noto Sans SC fallback" (Chinese UI copy; gloss: "Font "…" is not available in the current font library; Noto Sans SC fallback will be used").

  In the packaged desktop page under `file://`, fonts are read with XHR using relative addresses (`new URL(filename, import.meta.url)`). Ordinary builds and the Windows package place the fonts next to the renderer output. The macOS package rewrites the URL during staging to point at `Contents/Resources/web/assets`, sharing one set of bytes with the Web build and with the Export Worker when `external-fonts` is on; native file names are mapped to hashed resource names through `bundled-fonts.json` in that directory. Before packaging, the three copies of the font bytes must be verified identical; if any are missing or different, no package is shipped. The whole renderer process depends on Electron granting `file://` pages permission to read files that are also `file://` (fuse `GrantFileProtocolExtraPrivileges`, on by default). If it is switched off, not only the fonts but also the page's module scripts cannot be read, and the UI does not start. So the packaging configuration must not switch this fuse off (the Runtime starts through `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE`, and `RunAsNode` must not be switched off either). To switch it off, the whole renderer process must instead be served by a custom protocol. The production build of the renderer checks that every font is in the output and that `base` is relative, and fails the build otherwise (`checkPreviewFonts` in `apps/desktop/electron.vite.config.ts`). `npm run check:file-fonts` (`apps/desktop/tools/check-file-fonts.mjs`) opens the production build's `index.html` over `file://` in a hidden Electron window, through a self-check entry built alongside the artifact that the app itself does not load (`src/renderer/kernel-check.ts`), following the same load path as preview. It checks that the kernel injected every font in the artifact, and that the text boxes were measured by the kernel. Windows packaging (`apps/desktop/tools/package-desktop.mjs`) sets no fuse and uses Electron's defaults; the font check still targets the ordinary production build. The macOS packaging additionally runs `check-file-fonts.mjs --out=<Resources/app.asar/out>` to check the ASAR page and the shared font paths extracted from the final ZIP. That window uses a development Electron of the same version, which does not replace functional acceptance of the final App.
- Downloadable fonts (Google Fonts): when a family is in the font catalogue but is neither bundled with the kernel nor installed locally, the Runtime downloads it to `fonts/` in the Runtime Home (not in the project directory). From then on, as with local fonts, the engine selects the face, extracts it and reads it through a read handle, and preview and export use the same file. The resolution order is: bundled with the kernel → local → download cache → download. A family installed locally uses only the local copy and is not downloaded; a family bundled with the kernel is never downloaded. The requested weight is first matched against the weights the family actually has in the catalogue (with the same CSS matching the engine uses to choose faces). If the cache already holds that face, it is not downloaded again.
  - **Download.** No API key is needed. A face is requested from the public stylesheet endpoint `<fonts.cssEndpoint>/css2?family=<family>:wght@<weight>` (`ital,wght@` when italic). The request carries only the family name, weight and italic flag, and a fixed User-Agent of `CucCut`. For a User-Agent that does not look like a browser, the endpoint returns one complete TrueType file per face (CJK fonts are also a single file, about 5–20 MB); it does not split by `unicode-range` and does not serve WOFF2, so there is no WOFF2 decoding and no shard merging. A response that is sharded, or whose file address is not under the https base of `fonts.fileEndpoint`, is treated as the font service not providing this face (`FONT_DOWNLOAD_SOURCE`). Redirects are not followed. The stylesheet is capped at 256 KiB and the font file at 64 MiB (both the declared and the actually received size are checked). Connection failures, 5xx, 429 and a read that breaks halfway are retried three times with backoff; 30 seconds without receiving bytes counts as a stall. The download is written to a temporary file in `fonts/.staging/`, then checked by the engine host's `fonts.inspect` using the same resolution as rendering (it must be readable as a font, and the file must contain this family name). Only then is it renamed to `fonts/files/<family>/<family>-<weight>[i]-<first 12 digits of digest>.ttf` and recorded in `fonts/index.json` (family, weight, italic, licence, sha256, size, download time; the address is not recorded). A file that fails the check is deleted (`FONT_DOWNLOAD_INTEGRITY`); a full disk is `FONT_DOWNLOAD_NO_SPACE`. Logs record only the family name, never addresses. Fonts in the download cache are used only for font resolution and never execute anything else.
  - **Jobs and concurrency.** A download is a `fontDownload` job in the JobManager (several faces of one family; progress by bytes over the `jobs` topic; cancel via `jobs.cancel`). The queue `font-download` runs at most two at a time. The same face is downloaded only once at a time; later requests wait for the same download. A download cancelled by `jobs.cancel` ends as `CANCELLED`; in the font-picker list that family is `failed` with `error.code` `CANCELLED` (the UI shows "已取消" (cancelled), which can be retried). A download belongs to the party that started it: choosing a font, opening a video and starting a preview start a `fontDownload` job. Faces downloaded during an export do not create their own job; their progress is recorded on the export job (stage `downloading`), and cancelling the export only stops the downloads that the export itself started. A face another party is already downloading is simply waited for, and cancelling the export does not cancel it. A face whose automatic download failed is not retried automatically for ten minutes; `fonts.download`, the manual download, is the retry. Cancellation (cancelling a download job, cancelling an export, deletion) does not count as a failure, and the next time the face is needed it is automatically downloaded as usual. When preview waits, it calls `fonts.resolve` without `download`, which only waits for downloads already started and does not restart one just cancelled. Before downloading, the settings are checked: when `fonts.autoDownload` is off or `offline.strict` is on, nothing is downloaded automatically.
  - **Preview.** The UI's `fonts.resolve` is sent with `download: true`. A face that is not local but is in the catalogue starts downloading and is marked `downloading`. The UI asks about those faces again after 1.5 seconds, and when a download finishes (succeeded, failed or cancelled), it hands them to the preview engine for injection and redraws (`packages/ui/src/render/font-downloads.ts`; in the meantime the fallback font is used and no missing-font notice is shown). Faces already local, and faces downloaded or finished earlier in the same batch, are injected first (`onPartial`), without waiting for the faces in the batch still downloading. Besides the faces reported missing by preview frames and thumbnails, the trigger also includes the check run when a video is opened (see the next item).
  - **Census and samples.** `fonts.usage` takes a census of the fonts a video uses. The used families are scattered across text elements, animation presets, template-generated content and subtitle styles, so the same frozen snapshot used for export is given to the Render Worker to typeset (`export-worker census`, typesetting only, no drawing). This snapshot carries no assets (`skipAssets` of `exports.plan`), so videos with missing assets, assets that are gone, or assets that changed can still be censused. For each family, it reports the named faces, their current status, and what the kernel currently draws them with (the fallback family, which for the bundled kernel is Source Han Sans, `Noto Sans SC`). With `download: true`, faces not yet downloaded start downloading under the same rules as automatic download (disabled, strict offline, or failed within the last ten minutes means no download; cancellations do not count as failures). The UI calls this once when a video is opened (whether to download is decided by the Runtime according to settings), and once more when the export panel opens (without `download`, so no downloads). `fonts.sample` gives the specimen for the font-picker list: a family in a catalogue, with a subset containing only the characters in the family name (the `text` parameter of the stylesheet endpoint). The request carries only the family name, the regular weight and those characters, and uses the same endpoint settings, host restrictions and limits as downloads. Samples are stored in `fonts/samples/`, and `fonts.clear` does not delete them. Not fetched in strict offline mode.
  - **UI** (product design §5.9; prototypes `panel-font-picker.jsx`, `font-downloads.jsx`, `settings-fonts.jsx`). The font picker (for text, timer and generator element styles, subtitle styles, and the stage toolbar) is a single virtual-scrolled list: fonts used in the video → recently used → all fonts. When searching or filtering (by category or text), it becomes a combined search result. The order is frozen when opened, while status changes live through the `jobs` topic and `fonts.catalogue`: built-in, local, downloaded, downloadable, downloading (with percentage and cancel), failed (with retry), cancelled. The ⓘ icon shows details and licence. Selecting a font that is not yet downloaded takes effect immediately, starts the download, and shows a notice. The font strip above the stage is the only place for this when a video is opened: while in progress (which one, the family name and progress, a "skip this" and "cancel all"), when all have arrived (collapses after 3 seconds), when some could not be fetched (family → fallback font · reason, retryable), and when automatic download is off (Download, Settings). Settings › Fonts: automatic download; two mirror addresses that accept only https (invalid ones are reported on the spot and not saved); downloaded fonts (those used by the export are marked and cannot be deleted); total size and clear all. The export panel is in §9.11. Browser sessions have no `fonts.*`: the Web entry keeps the older font picker that lists only kernel-bundled fonts; no font strip is shown, Settings has no "Fonts" section, and the export panel shows no font notices. No placeholder UI is shown.
  - **Engine.** The engine host's font thread takes `cacheDir` (`<home>/fonts/files`). When a family is not installed locally, it looks in this directory, and the reply carries `source: 'downloaded'`. `fonts.families` gives the names of locally installed families (for the font-picker list), and `fonts.inspect` checks downloaded files (read-only, with limits).
  - **Deletion.** `fonts.remove` and `fonts.clear` delete files in the cache. A file frozen by an export still in progress, or a face being waited on, is not deleted (`FONT_IN_USE`; left behind on clear). It is released when the export finishes. Videos already rendered are unaffected.
  - **Tests.** Tests do not touch the network. The Runtime's `fonts` option injects the font catalogue and fetch (rewriting Google's addresses to a local fake service). When nothing is injected, vitest runs with `CUCCUT_FONT_DOWNLOADS=off` (the catalogue is empty and nothing is downloaded).
- When new text is created, the text box is measured (if `w` is not given, the box width is the measured text width). This also goes through the same WASM module (`bc_measure_text`, `frame_render::text_measure`), using the same typesetting engine and the same font set as drawing text; there is no other measuring method. If the kernel is not yet loaded, creation waits for it to load; if the kernel traps mid-measurement and is reloading (`PLANNER_CRASHED`), the whole batch is re-measured after it is back (up to three times); if the kernel fails to load or reload, a notice is shown and nothing is created (`packages/ui/src/components/editor/text-measure.ts`). The box is computed against the sequence at the moment of creation; if the sequence changes while waiting, it is computed against the changed sequence. The thumbnails of text preset cards are measured by the kernel instance that draws them.
- The editor's thumbnails (element cells, subtitle style cards, text preset cards, and the Lottie stickers in the brand library) are drawn into transparent-background frames by a separate instance of the same WASM module, so they match what lands on the canvas. A subtitle style card loops a sample sentence using the style's own per-word animation: the sample gets a transcript with one word per beat (620 ms), and after the sentence is spoken it pauses one beat before restarting from the beginning. Only cards in view follow the shared clock; once a full cycle of frames has been drawn, the frames are cached and reused. Cards without per-word animation stop after one cycle. Under `prefers-reduced-motion`, they stop on the frame where the second word is 35% spoken.
- SVG images (a material version's `mediaType` is `image/svg+xml`; this covers images, stickers and placeholder boxes) are drawn in the kernel. `FrameRenderer` rasterizes them from the asset bytes at the output's long-edge size (`render_raster::svg`, resvg). When a sticker has `fillOverrides`, the SVG text is first rewritten according to the recolor table (`timeline::svg_fill::apply_overrides`, the same grouping as the sticker panel's color swatches). Results are cached per asset version and recolor table; changing the output size re-rasterizes. Preview passes the SVG asset bytes into WASM (the same path as Lottie) and does not read pixels from the image element. Export reads the bytes from the frozen file, and preflight uses the same function to decide whether the asset can be decoded (`asset-undecodable`). The preview WASM includes only resvg's rasterizer, not its text layout, and the export resvg font database is empty, so `<text>` in an SVG is drawn on neither side. An SVG that is not UTF-8 cannot be recolored; it is drawn in its original colors and a hint is shown.
- GIF images (`mediaType` is `image/gif`, same scope as SVG) are also drawn in the kernel. `frame_render::load_gif` decodes all frames from the asset bytes (`render_raster::source::AnimatedImage`, each frame's display duration taken from the GIF's delay), and picks the frame for “this moment minus the instance's start”. Image instances loop over the GIF's own total duration; sticker assets follow `loop` (`loop` repeats, `once` stops on the last frame, `hold` stops on the first frame). If the frame count exceeds 2048, or the decoded pixels exceed 128 MiB, only the first frame is drawn and a hint is shown. A GIF with a total duration of 0, or with only one frame, is also drawn as its first frame. Preview and export share the same limits (`frame_render::GIF_BUDGET`), and decoded frames are cached per asset version. Preview passes the GIF bytes into WASM (the same path as SVG), export reads the bytes from the frozen file, and preflight uses the same function to decide whether the asset can be decoded (`asset-undecodable`). Bitmaps are not recolored.
- The bytes of a Lottie asset are either bodymovin JSON or a `.lottie` archive, and both sides read them in the kernel (`render_raster::source::lottie`). For an archive, only the zip central directory is read. Only stored and deflate (miniz_oxide) are accepted; encrypted archives, ZIP64, multi-disk archives, and names containing `..` or absolute paths are all rejected. After decompression, each entry must be ≤ 64 MiB, the total must be ≤ 256 MiB, there may be at most 4096 entries, and both size and CRC must match. The `activeAnimationId` in the manifest (`manifest.json`) selects the animation to draw (version 2 uses `initial.animation`). If there is none, the first item in `animations` is used; if that is also missing, the alphabetically first `animations/*.json` or `a/*.json` is used. Image sub-assets that are referenced only by path are looked up in the archive as: the path itself (with the leading `/` removed), `images/<filename>`, `i/<filename>`, and `<filename>`. Image sub-assets are bitmaps (PNG, JPEG, the first frame of a GIF, WebP) or SVG (the data URI may be base64 or percent-encoded text; files inside the archive are the same). SVG is rasterized at the resource's declared `w`×`h` (resvg, `<text>` not drawn). An archive that cannot be read is `lottie-unreadable`; an image that is missing from the archive or cannot be decoded is still `lottie-asset-missing`. On import, the engine uses the same reading logic (`video-engine` depends on `render-raster` without `media`) to detect Lottie and to get its size and duration; see the video format spec §4.1.
- The spectrum for a waveform (BCS1) is computed from the asset's audio. It is computed once per asset version (48 kHz mono, FFT 1024, 60 frames per second; `waveform::dsp`). `frame_render::spectrum` assembles it, according to the audio plan, into one strip for each waveform instance along sequence time, and preview and export use the same assembly. Export has the Render Worker decode the frozen asset file (Symphonia; anything it cannot decode goes to ffmpeg), so the frozen rendition of the finished video includes the assets the waveform listens to. Preview has the UI decode the audio with Web Audio and push it in chunks into the same analysis inside the preview WASM (`bc_spectrum_begin/push/finish`); the result is stored in the asset cache per asset version. Until it is computed, waveforms are drawn in their still state, with no notice. Assets over 512 MB are not analysed in preview; waveforms are drawn still and this is reported. `speaker` and `alwaysShow: false` (video format spec §3.7) are also applied in `frame_render::spectrum`, to the assembled strip. Each speaker's intervals are derived by `render_graph::audio_plan::speaker_activity` from the transcript in the video. Preview has the UI pass the transcript into WASM (`bc_set_speech`). It is sent only when the sequence has a waveform with `speaker` set, or when ducking is triggered by the script. While the transcript text is still being fetched, the speaker hints that read “没有转写” (no transcript) are not reported yet; once it is fetched, it is sent on the next redraw, even while paused. Export carries it through the `video` plan from the engine's `exports.plan` (`speakers`), which is handed to the Render Worker.
- Preview and export are byte-identical. The same input, drawn through the export path (native build), has its frame digest recorded as a fixture (`crates/frame-render/tests/fixtures/parity.json`). The preview WASM must produce the same bytes for the same input, with no tolerance. Browser-decoded pictures (video, bitmaps) and sound are outside this scope: both sides feed the renderer already-decoded pictures and computed spectra. SVG and Lottie are drawn in the kernel from the asset bytes on both sides, so they are in scope.
- Shortcuts for compositing must give exactly the same result as the full-frame composite. An integer translation, and a layer whose pixels are only fully opaque or fully zero (a full video frame), is copied as is (`render_raster::raster::copy_over`; if a single semi-transparent pixel is present, the whole layer goes back to the pipeline and the target is left untouched). Other layers (a line of text, a sticker, a subtitle layer) are composited only within the bounding rectangle of their non-zero pixels (`render_raster::plan::composite_bounded`). For the nine blend modes, the destination is left untouched only when the source is all zero; other blend modes always apply to the full frame. Preview and export take the same path.
- Speed (`tools/preview-bench.ts`, Node, SIMD enabled): video plus bilingual subtitles at 960×540 takes about 11–12 ms per frame, and at 1920×1080 about 45–60 ms. Video plus multiple elements at 960×540 takes about 28–30 ms, and at 1920×1080 about 110–115 ms. The first frame after loading fonts takes about 45 ms (building the text layout engine); after that, the engine is reused when the size changes or the document is swapped.

To do: browser decoding of lossy formats is not sample-identical to Symphonia, so the preview spectrum is close to, but not byte-identical with, the export. The exported waveform listens to the sound of this sequence in the document and does not change with the audio track chosen at export time. The element animation on the other side of a transition is clamped to the time within its own interval.

The path for plain video with native subtitles must be able to run without starting the code-browser Worker. The browser is used only for compositions that truly need it. Do not compile arbitrary CSS / JS into native elements automatically just because an HTML tag is named video or text. Only controlled domain objects, or an explicit subset that has been verified, enter the native path.

### 9.2 Composition boundaries and backdrop dependencies

Code compositions use the complete composition as the boundary by default, not arbitrary DOM nodes. Blend modes, backdrop, filters and nested masks create backdrop dependencies. When splitting a layer would change the picture, the corresponding dependency subgraph must be kept.

A code adapter that needs a background input must declare and accept an explicit backdrop frame. When that cannot be expressed, use a verified same-backend composition approach, or reject the effect. Do not claim that moving an arbitrary native effect into the browser is necessarily equivalent.

### 9.3 Color and alpha

The first version of strict export is limited to SDR for now. The source's color information, the working space, the browser's output color, and the encoding target are each declared. Decided: the rules for color, blending and alpha follow the contract of the v2 render core and its reference images. Preview and export use the same implementation (§9.1), and their agreement is guaranteed by the same code, not by two implementations kept aligned by hand. Rules of the render core (`render-raster`; the files below are all under its `src/`):

- Pixel buffers are always premultiplied RGBA8 with sRGB-encoded values (tiny-skia's format). Blending does not switch to linear space. The frame plan's surface has only one kind, `srgb-premultiplied` u8 (`SurfacePlan::canvas` in `plan/surface.rs`; contract `ColorContract::SRGB_U8` in `motion`). Linear premultiplied exists only in the vocabulary; no executor uses it.
- Colors in DrawOp and gradient color stops are non-premultiplied 0..1 RGBA. Components outside that range are clamped to 0..1, and tiny-skia premultiplies them at draw time. The bitmap side table is already-premultiplied RGBA8 (`drawop.rs`, `raster.rs`). Non-premultiplied inputs (images, animated images, decoded video frames, sbix / CBDT color glyphs) are always converted on entry with tiny-skia's `ColorU8::premultiply`; there is only one rounding rule. COLR color glyph output is already premultiplied and is not multiplied again (`source/animated_image.rs`, `media.rs`, `fonts.rs`).
- Layers are drawn on a fully transparent offscreen canvas and composited back according to the layer's opacity and blend mode. Blend modes map one-to-one to tiny-skia (normal is source-over; the others are multiply, screen, overlay, darken, lighten, difference, exclusion, plus; `effects/composite/blend.rs`). Clipping applies to every draw inside the layer; the composite back does not clip again. A mask takes the mask layer's alpha or luminance (Rec.709 integer weights) as a 0..255 fixed-point factor, and multiplies it into the four premultiplied components as `(c × f + 127) / 255` (`raster.rs`).
- Color filters un-premultiply to f64, operate in the 0..255 domain, clamp, then multiply back by alpha with rounding. Alpha is unchanged, and the luminance weights are Rec.709 (`effects/filters/color_adjust.rs`). Crossfades and transitions that composite by per-pixel coverage (wipes, circle, ink) interpolate integers on premultiplied values, `(f × (255 − a) + t × a + 127) / 255`. Pixels with coverage 0 or 1 copy the input byte for byte (`effects/transitions/mod.rs`). Supersampling and motion blur average in the premultiplied domain (`imgcmp.rs`, `motion_blur.rs`).
- When handing off to a consumer that accepts only non-premultiplied pixels, un-premultiply using tiny-skia's rounding (`c / (a / 255) + 0.5`, `effects/pixel_format.rs`).

Status: preview and export both draw through `render-raster` via `frame-render` (§9.1), and the rules above are the same on both sides. Transitions also blend the two sides' rendered layers in sRGB-encoded values (`transition.rs` in `frame-render`). Transparent frames given to the UI (thumbnails) are un-premultiplied with the rounding above. Following ffmpeg's default, assets are converted to RGB, and the exported video is encoded as BT.709-tagged yuv420p (`colorspace`, `color_primaries` and `color_trc` are all written as bt709). Browser pixels are converted as tagged inputs; you cannot just change the BT.709 label without converting the pixels.

Alpha must be declared as straight or premultiplied. Format conversion is handled uniformly by the receiving end of `FrameSurface`. Transparent edges, text shadows and semi-transparent transitions need dedicated reference images. When HDR input cannot be fully preserved, an explicit tone-map must be shown or the input rejected. It must not be silently treated as ordinary SDR.

### 9.4 Preview tiers

| Tier | Purpose | Rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Draft | Quick look at direction | May use low-resolution proxies or low-quality code pre-rendering |
| Interactive | Responsiveness while editing | Keep interaction smooth; frames may be dropped |
| Export-Exact | Matches export | Uses the frozen version and the export evaluation rules; slower is allowed |

The UI must not label a proxy preview as the exact final result.

Current state (editor preview, `packages/ui/src/components/editor/preview-engine.ts`):

- When stopped (paused, or after a seek), it is Export-Exact: the frame the kernel draws at canvas pixels (display size × device pixel ratio, not exceeding the sequence canvas) is the exact frame export would produce. When stopped, the stopped frame is redrawn at original size.
- During playback it is Interactive: it draws the same plan and the same content, with output reduced to within the pixel budget (`PLAYBACK_PIXELS`, 960×540, `packages/ui/src/render/playback-quality.ts`), and scaled up by the canvas CSS size. The only permissible differences from export are resolution and timing. The content must not differ.
- During playback a video frame is drawn only once. When the display refresh rate is higher than the video frame rate, it is not redrawn. If the document, assets, fonts, spectrum or size changes, the same frame is redrawn.
- While stopped and seeking (dragging the playhead, clicking the timeline, frame stepping), it does not wait for the video seek to land. A video still seeking is first drawn immediately with the last image it read, and consecutive seeks are drawn at the playback pixel budget. This is a difference in timing only. Once 200 ms (`SCRUB_SETTLE_MS`) pass with no further seeks and all media is aligned, the exact frame is redrawn at original size. The engine's `exact` is true only when the canvas shows this frame.
- The preview WASM keeps two renderers per output size (one for playback, one for stopped). Switching between them does not rebuild text layout, subtitle compilation or the asset cache. Fonts, documents, transcripts and spectra passed in apply to each renderer. The spectrum track assembled from waveforms (which is independent of size) carries over on switch and is not re-assembled. `parity.test.ts` checks, after drawing at several sizes back and forth, that the stopped frame is still byte-identical to export.

To do: 1080p 60 fps asset playback only reaches 37–48 fps during playback (30 fps assets reach 30 fps). The kernel allocates, clears and copies a full-frame layer for every element (the overlay entry of `subtitle-render`), which dominates when there are many elements. Reading each video frame back through Canvas 2D every frame (`drawImage` plus `getImageData`) costs about 8–9 ms; avoiding it requires letting the video bypass the kernel and be composited underneath by the GPU (only valid when the video is the bottom layer and everything above is kernel-drawn). The first exact frame after opening is at worst about 1.5 seconds (waiting for the video to load). During playback, the p95 audio/video offset is about 30–50 ms.

Record `requestedFrame`, `renderedFrame`, `displayedFrame` and `requestGeneration`. Once a new seek arrives, old asynchronous decode or capture results may only be discarded and must not overwrite the new picture. Interaction may drop frames; export must not drop frames.

Shared Rust semantics and reusable render modules reduce divergence, but they do not guarantee pixel-level agreement between DOM preview, WebGPU, native WGPU and different GPUs. Strict acceptance is tested against a fixed environment and declared tolerances, not by a verbal promise of absolute WYSIWYG.

### 9.5 Cache keys and invalidation

```text
cacheKey = hash(
  bundle/source revision + all transitive dependency assets + font bytes + dependency lock
  + adapter / runtime / browser version + parameters + seed + local time range
  + size + editFps / outputFps / bundleFps + sampling and frame-count policy
  + color / alpha / quality settings
  + required backdrop dependencies + simulation checkpoints or state version
)
```

- Cache keys for ordinary assets must also include the asset version, timeMap and effect state; they must not consist of frameIndex alone.
- Frame rate is not a vague field: explicitly distinguish the edit frame rate, the output frame rate, the code package's intrinsic frame rate, the source timebase, the actual sample times and quantization, and the frame-count policy. Build keys after normalizing equivalent rational time specifications. Actual samples may be reused; encoded artifacts are keyed separately according to the output contract.
- Do not infer "only two seconds changed" from "one source file changed". P0 conservatively invalidates the entire local composition for a changed code package. P1 enables local invalidation only when the author has declared the dependency scope and verification has passed, or when the structured scene is sufficient to derive it. A user's manual local render range is a constrained operation, not a compiler proof.
- Simulation with `sequential` invalidates the entire dependency tail after the modified time point; only valid checkpoints or independent sub-scenes can narrow the scope.
- Changes to global fonts, theme parameters or shared functions may affect all scenes.

### 9.6 Resource and performance strategy

Optimization priorities:

1. avoid repeated computation;
2. reuse already generated animation;
3. narrow the range that must be handled by the browser;
4. reduce cross-process copying;
5. run with reasonable concurrency;
6. optimize encoding.

Constraints:

- Browser and Remotion sessions are reused per code package and runtime, subject to a memory budget.
- Use bounded queues and backpressure; GPU surfaces must be released explicitly.
- A single 1080p RGBA frame is 1920 × 1080 × 4 = 8,294,400 bytes. Memory is bounded by the window and pool limits and does not grow linearly with the total frame count of the video; keeping raw frames for the whole film resident is not an acceptable caching strategy.
- When the actually available GPU memory is unknown, report it as unknown rather than fabricating an accurate remaining VRAM figure.
- Hardware encoding, cross-process GPU sharing, local caching and proxy playback must all be negotiated and reported; a preview's quality reduction must not be silently carried into export. Hardware encoding is a negotiable fast path; on failure it must not silently change the codec or quality contract.
- GPU zero-copy is a later performance project, not an assumption for first-version correctness.

### 9.7 Shared truth for subtitles and playback

The Stage's local Clock in the UI is only responsible for presentation. Conversions among exact source time, sequence time and local time come from the same semantics in Rust and WASM. Export uses a frozen RenderGraph.

Audio is owned by a single mix path: audio inside an Adapter and external narration must not play twice.

Changing the text or subtitles of the whole film should not unconditionally clear all internal caches of code compositions. However, the premise that "unchanged code need not re-render frames" holds only if its transitive dependencies, runtime environment and backdrop inputs have truly not changed; code compositions with a backdrop must be invalidated by dependency, and must not reuse the wrong picture merely to make the call count zero.

### 9.8 Inspection service and evidence

`InspectionService` produces immutable `InspectionArtifact`s. An inspection request must be explicit: whether it is source media or the final composite (`source-media` / `final-composite`), `snapshotRef`, sequence and version, frame or range, `quality`, and purpose.

**The inspection of a final composite must go through the same composition path as the target RenderGraph.** Capturing only an HTML sub-scene, or returning only a thumbnail of the source video, does not count.

```ts
interface EvidenceReceipt {
  snapshotHash: string;
  videoRevision: Revision;
  sampleId: Id;
  requestedTime: MediaTime;
  actualTime: MediaTime;
  visibleItemIds: Id[];
  samplingPolicy: string;
  frame: { width: number; height: number; colorSpace: string; alphaMode: string };
  contentHash: string;
  byteLength: number;
  renderPath: string;               // render path actually executed
  covered: RangeRef[];
  uncovered: RangeRef[];
}
```

- When binary evidence is received, validate size, format, byte limit and digest; do not trust only the metadata declared by the worker.
- After the task continues editing, evidence from an older version may be kept as history or as a candidate, but must not be labelled "currently inspected". Immutable snapshots are used to identify old evidence and make its ownership explicit; when current state is needed, request evidence again.
- Keyframe inspection, short-clip playback and audio audition are different methods. A single frame cannot prove motion rhythm; checking text cannot prove real pronunciation. Quality records only acknowledge the inspection coverage actually achieved.
- Inspection does not change the video, and must not fabricate a successful artifact for a failed screenshot; it is subject to the same resource admission, privacy egress and logging policies.

### 9.9 RenderExecutionPlan and same-frame switching

`RenderExecutionPlan` records, for each time range: required capabilities, the chosen backend, the evidence version, the input snapshot, the proxy or exact level, the fallback reason and the resource estimate. It is a derived execution plan, kept separate from the stored video.

**Interactive preview** may use the verified media direct-display or DOM overlay fast path. When it meets complex subtitle animation, masks, backdrops, blending, nesting or 3D transforms, it switches to the full composition path. Do not judge the two paths equivalent merely by looking at the instance type. When switching, keep the same `FrameTicket` and audio master clock; until the new surface is ready, do not mark a mismatched old picture as a new frame. On failure, the old preview may be kept with an explicit status, or an error may be shown.

**Export-Exact and final composite inspection** use the frozen plan and the verified baseline path. When a fast path fails, it may fall back only to a backend that satisfies the same output contract. Changing codec, quality, audio track or transparency must be reconfirmed or rejected, and must not be silently downgraded. For a video containing only native subtitles, the number of code-browser launches should be 0 (the desktop UI's own use of Chromium is not counted here).

**Packet remux** must separately prove its eligibility: there is no visual change that must be redrawn, audio routing can pass through, the cut boundaries are compatible with the container and codec, and timestamp handling satisfies the output contract. Otherwise, encode. Do not guarantee stream copy just because "it's only two cuts".

### 9.10 Output sampling chain

Export evaluates the following chain in one direction:

```text
outputFrame → outputTime → sequenceTime → composition / sourceTime → actual sample
```

Sequences and nested layers must not be rounded repeatedly in between. Continuous native curves may be sampled at half a video frame; discrete sources select the actual frame only at the Adapter or decoding boundary. The preview Clock may produce approximate host moments, but Export-Exact is governed by the frozen Rate and exact indices.

When a range cannot be evenly divided by the output frame duration, `exact-grid` rejects it, and `cover-range` writes the tail difference into the preflight and the artifact. Do not secretly change the audio speed, and do not request out-of-bounds frames from the source. The cache and `EvidenceReceipt` both record the video moment and the output frame identity; do not compare results at different frame rates using frameIndex alone.

**Benchmark example**: in a 30 fps video, a linear native animation from x=0 to x=100 lasting 1 second. When exporting at 60 fps, the sampling moment of frame 1 is t=1/60, x=5/3; it is not first rounded back to video frame 0 to get x=0. The source video may still repeat frames per its declared policy, but one must not conclude from this that continuous animation should also repeat.

For numeric rules, see §2 of the video format specification.

### 9.11 Frozen export and publishing

**Freezing**. When export starts, freeze: the video version, the selected variant, the versions of all documents / assets / code packages, fonts, the render configuration, the output range and the adapter version. `ExportSnapshot` also contains `editFpsBySequence`, `outputFps`, the exact `rangeStart` and `rangeDuration`, `frameCountPolicy`, the version of the sampling and quantization contract, and the audio output policy. During export the user may keep editing; the export Job does not read new state, and the user changing the frame rate does not affect it.

**Preflight** must check: missing media or fonts, invalidated translations or dubbing, orphaned anchors, out-of-range clips, unauthorized network access, unimplemented effects and incompatible outputs.

**Publishing**. The export writes to a staging path. After validating frame count, duration, audio track, decodability and the necessary sampled screenshots, it publishes atomically. Insufficient disk space, a Worker crash or an encoding failure must never overwrite an existing successful file. Overwriting an existing output still requires explicit permission.

Publish validation records at least: the logical range, `frameCount`, the first and last PTS, `encodedVideoDuration`, `audioDuration`, the sample rate and the tail difference.

**Fast path**. A pure remux that meets the conditions may proceed without re-encoding. Exact non-keyframe cuts, scaling, burned-in subtitles and complex transitions must not generically promise stream copy. The chosen fast path and its fallback must be written into the report. Exporting only the subtitle file requires no video rendering at all.

**Quick fix**. After a final cut has been exported, when only a few segments have changed, only the affected ranges may be re-rendered, with the rest reusing the encoding result of the previous export (ported from v2). It depends on keeping the baseline artifact from the previous export; where the baseline is kept and when it is cleaned up is pending (§14). Its priority is below porting the render core.

**Tail policy**. An exact 10-second range exported with `cover-range` at 30000/1001 fps yields 300 frames, a 10.010-second visual tail. This is a declared policy and must not claim that all tracks of the container are strictly 10.000 seconds. `exact-grid` rejects it before starting. Changing the frame rate is not authorization for frame interpolation, deduplication, speed change or automatically stretching the audio.

**Implementation in this version** (subtitles, scripts and audio; commands and protocol specification §4.4). Export is the `export` task in JobManager. It shares the ledger, cancellation and post-restart query with model tasks, and does not go through the model or the Worker:

- Freezing: `exports.create` uses one engine request (`exports.plan`) to compute the plan for all ranges on the same video version. Timeline conversion (cuts, speed changes, acting instances) is done in Rust; the Runtime only writes files according to the plan and assembles the ffmpeg filter graph. The plan, the versions of documents and assets, the location and digest of linked assets, and the target directory and file names are stored as an `ExportSnapshot` artifact, which the task record references. Audio and the sound source of the final cut (§9.13) are first converted into the instances' selections `audioItems` (which instances are disabled, and whose mute is cancelled). They are passed with the same request to the engine and act only on this one audio plan; the picture plan of the final cut and the sequence frozen for the Worker are unaffected. `audioItems` accepts only these two kinds. What else is frozen for the final cut is described in "Implementation of the final cut" below.
- Preflight completes at submission time; if it fails, no task is created: missing assets; a linked asset whose content digest does not match the registered one (`ASSET_CHANGED`, re-checked before execution if the file's length or modification time has changed); an empty range; no exportable text; a target directory that is not writable or a specified file that already exists; missing ffmpeg.
- Generation happens in the task's staging. Each file is verified individually: subtitles and scripts are re-parsed and their entry count and times checked; audio is decoded by ffprobe with duration within tolerance (WAV 0.01 seconds, lossy 0.1 seconds). Only files that pass verification are published. Publishing first copies into a hidden temporary file in the target directory, then hard-links to the final name. If the name already exists the link fails, so someone else's file is not overwritten and no half-written file appears. On volumes that do not support hard links, it falls back to "confirm it does not exist, then rename" (§14). Once publishing has started, cancellation is no longer honored; the published files are the result. A cancellation before that cleans up staging and leaves no files. A hidden temporary file left behind by a force-killed process is deleted by residual cleanup at the next startup (§5.8).
- Audio mixing shares the same sound semantics as preview: gain, volume envelope, mute, fade in and out, constant speed change (`atempo`, which changes speed without changing pitch, consistent with the preview's playback rate), and transition sound cross-fades and ducking. `audio_plan` in `render-graph` writes out the per-frame plan's sound by range: a cross-fade attaches an equal-power curve to each side and fills in the segment taken from handles beyond the instance range. The volume envelope is evaluated by `render-graph::envelope` (replacing `volume`; eased segments are baked into a polyline at 0.02 seconds per v2 `volume_curve`). The per-frame plan takes the value at that instant; the range plan maps the same polyline onto the range's clock and writes it into the segment's `envelope`, with the segment's `gainDb` recorded as 0. Ducking uses the same envelope from `ducking.rs`, expanded into breakpoints; for ducking triggered by the script, the effective word stream is produced by `audio_plan::speech_activity`. Export (`exports.plan`) and preview (the per-frame plan with the transcription fed into WASM) receive the same batch of transcriptions. The Runtime multiplies the envelope, fades, cross-fade and ducking into one per-sample amplitude expression (`aeval`, with the polyline's segment selected by nested binary `if`s), so the gain at any instant equals the frame plan's `gainDb` (the `render-graph` tests compare the two plans instant by instant). The mix filter graph is written to a file in staging and handed to ffmpeg (from 7.0 it is `-/filter_complex <file>`; earlier versions use `-filter_complex_script`, chosen per the `ffmpeg -h long` description), not placed on the command line: densely baked envelopes and ducking could exceed the length limit of a single argument or the whole command line. Preview changes volume on each refresh tick while export applies it per sample; this difference is in sampling and is not reported. Places where it is inconsistent with preview are written into the task's warnings: a gain or envelope peak above 0 dB (preview peaks at 0 dB), a freeze frame that is silent, and a cross-fade needing handles beyond the asset (the missing part is exported as silence). Loudness normalization is described below.

**Implementation of the final cut** (`video` kind; commands and protocol specification §4.4). Freezing, preflight and publishing are the same as above. The picture is drawn by a separate Render Worker process (`export-worker`):

- Freezing: `exports.plan` for the `video` kind additionally provides the video portion needed for frame-by-frame planning (this sequence and the asset records it references), the picture overview and sound plan for each segment, and the frozen text of the documents that subtitles need to read (subtitle documents and style documents). If the sequence has waveforms with `speaker` set and the video has transcription, it also provides the intervals in which each speaker talks on the sequence (`speakers`). It also provides the output size and the picture's position within it (`output`, `render_graph::video_plan::output_geometry`; rules in commands and protocol specification §4.4). With `skipAssets`, assets are not frozen (`assets` is empty, and no check is made whether files exist or have changed); they are provided only for enumerating fonts (§9.1). Export does not use it. The Worker calls the same frame plan of `render-graph` frame by frame, with `t_k = start + k / output frame rate` (when the output frame rate differs from the edit frame rate, samples are taken at the exact moment of the output frame, §9.10). The Worker reads the frozen input files that the Runtime wrote into staging; edits made during export do not affect it (AT-25).
- Preflight is run once by the Worker at submission time (`preflight`); if it fails, no task is created. Content that cannot be drawn is listed item by item (`EXPORT_UNSUPPORTED_CONTENT`; each entry in `details.items` gives the instance, the granularity, the layer kind, the reason and a description). Assets are checked with ffprobe to confirm their picture can be decoded. When ffmpeg encoding is needed but the encoder is missing, `EXPORT_TOOL_MISSING` is raised (not checked when the native writer is used; see "Picture" below). With `onUnsupported: 'skip'`, these items become task warnings (`EXPORT_CONTENT_SKIPPED`): the whole layer is not drawn, and the effect or transition is skipped and treated as a hard cut.
- When the output's aspect ratio differs from the canvas (both width and height given), the Worker draws at the size of the picture's region (like changing only the output height, it is a whole-image scale without re-laying out) and copies it line by line into an opaque black-background output frame before handing it to encoding. The long side of SVG rasterization also follows that region. The output frame is the buffer for the encoding queue: the encoder writer runs on a separate thread, and the compositing thread hands frames into a bounded queue (2 frames) and then draws the next frame.
