Buffers rotate from a fixed pool; the black borders are laid down only once when the pool is built, and when the proportions match the canvas, the whole frame is copied once into the buffer.
- Picture: **platform-native first, ffmpeg as fallback** (`media-native`: macOS uses AVFoundation / VideoToolbox, Windows uses Media Foundation). Each video instance has one sequential decode stream. A separate thread reads two frames ahead of compositing, taking the last frame whose time is no later than the target moment (consistent with the preview's media element), and reopens the stream when it goes backwards or jumps far forward. The native stream is scaled to the picture size directly inside the decoder (on macOS, portrait material swaps the requested width and height for the rotation, then is straightened on the CPU; Windows' source reader does not scale and scales on the CPU). Frame moments use the same source-media timeline as ffmpeg `-copyts` (including the PTS origin). Swapping native BGRA to RGBA is done only inside `media-native`; the export loop sees only RGBA. The ffmpeg stream is raw RGBA read through a pipe, with frame moments coming from `showinfo`. Images are decoded once (via ffmpeg). SVG and GIF are drawn by the frame rasterizer from the asset bytes (SVG rasterized at the output's long side; GIF takes frames by moment; §9.1, same as preview; if it cannot be decoded, it is `asset-undecodable`). The compositing result is handed to the encoding queue. MP4 is H.264 (default) or HEVC plus AAC; WebM is VP9 plus Opus. Quality is CRF or target bitrate. When H.264 goes through the native writer, CRF is replaced by an average bitrate (the `video_bitrate` curve is used as CRF 18; each +6 CRF halves the bitrate; clamped to 60 Mb/s). If a target bitrate is given, it is used as is. The AAC bitrate is taken from the output settings. The picture carries BT.709 primaries / transfer / matrix tags. Sound uses the audio export's mix, padded with silence or trimmed to the picture's length, and is multiplexed with the picture. Source codecs are not filtered by the output encoder's allowed values: SDR VP8, VP9 and AV1 assets that the local FFmpeg can decode (including MP4, WebM, and Opus / Vorbis audio tracks) can be exported with the default MP4 configuration without first rewriting the source file (those the native path cannot decode fall back to ffmpeg). A whole film without cuts also goes through the same decoding and compositing chain.

  The fallback is decided per asset and per output. Its reason is written into the Worker's `done` event (`video: { decoder, encoder, fallbacks: [{ scope, assetId?, reason, message }] }`; `decoder` is the backend name, `mixed`, or `null` when there is no video), and the Runtime records it in the log:

  | Side | Cases falling back to ffmpeg (`reason`) |
  | --- | --- |
  | Decode | No native backend on the platform (`native-unavailable`); the asset may have transparency (`alpha-unsupported`; native flattens alpha to 255); native cannot open it (`open-failed`, e.g. MKV, unsupported codec), fails midway through reading (`read-failed`), or cannot deliver a single frame (`no-frames`). Decoding continues with ffmpeg from the current frame and is not counted in `decoderRestarts`. |
  | Encode | Not H.264 (`codec`: HEVC, VP9); not MP4 (`container`); odd width or height (`odd-size`); frame rate out of range (`frame-rate`); the mix is not 32-bit float or 16 / 24 / 32-bit integer WAV at 48 kHz stereo (`audio-format`); the native writer cannot open it (`open-failed`) |
  | Both | Environment variable `CUCCUT_EXPORT_NATIVE=0` (`native-disabled`, for troubleshooting and testing) |

  Therefore ffprobe (asset probing, publish validation) and ffmpeg (images, mixing, and fallback decoding and encoding) remain required dependencies for final cut export. Native encoding only exempts the ffmpeg encoder preflight when it would actually be used: when going native, a missing `libx264` / `aac` does not block; if native cannot open at execution time and the encoder needed by the fallback is missing, it fails with `EXPORT_TOOL_MISSING`. At export start the mix has not yet been written, so the estimate is whether native is feasible; it is re-decided at execution from the actual WAV. The Windows native path (decoding, writing, and whether the BT.709 tag reaches the bitstream) has only been type-checked through cross-compilation, not tested on Windows.
- Worker location: environment variable `CUCCUT_EXPORT_WORKER`. If unset, use `export-worker` next to `engine-host` (same directory). If still not found, search the cargo output directory the same way as `engine-host` (`CARGO_TARGET_DIR`, `build.target-dir` in `.cargo/config*.toml`, `target/` in the repo, with `release` before `debug`). It is searched at each export. If not found, the final cut export and audio export with loudness normalization enabled are rejected with `EXPORT_TOOL_MISSING`; other exports are unaffected. If the Worker disappears after submission, when it is started within the task, the task fails with `EXPORT_TOOL_MISSING`, with `missing` set to `export-worker`, and the remedy says to build or specify the Worker (according to which tool failed to start; do not generically tell people to install ffmpeg). `npm run build:engine` builds both.
- Progress is counted in frames (`progress.unit: 'frames'`). Cancellation is signalled to the Worker via stdin (or by stdin closing, which also happens when the Runtime exits). The Worker stops its decoding and encoding processes and then exits, and staging is deleted entirely. A Worker crash fails only this task (`EXPORT_RENDER_FAILED`) and does not affect the Runtime. Native encoding and decoding run in the Worker process; on abandonment, the write is cancelled and a half-written file is deleted. On POSIX the Worker forms its own process group, and the ffmpeg processes it starts (the fallback path) are all in that group. When the Worker does not end normally (crash, force-kill, cancellation timeout), the Runtime first kills the whole group and then deletes staging, so ffmpeg is not left orphaned and does not keep writing after staging has been deleted.
- Before publishing, validate with ffprobe: both streams are present; the frame count is within one frame of expected; dimensions, frame rate, codec and picture duration match (tolerance one frame); and the audio duration equals the picture length (plus 50 ms of encoder padding).
- The picture is drawn by `frame-render`, the same implementation as preview (§9.1's status lists what can be drawn and what is pending). Content that cannot be drawn is listed item by item in preflight, with the same reasons preview reports. The kernel's hint is an `EXPORT_RENDER_NOTE` warning, with `detail` recording the instance ID and the hint.
- **Fonts.** First use the fonts shipped with the render kernel (`render-raster/assets/fonts`, the same set as preview). If the family named by the style is not in that set, use a local font, freezing it by face and recording its location and digest like assets. At preflight, the Render Worker lays out text with the kernel's fonts for every picture layer in the range (each instance as it first appears; subtitles lay out all sentences; video, images, Lottie and waveforms are not drawn), and reports each "family name, weight, italic" that is named but absent from the kernel's fonts. The Runtime uses the engine's `fonts.resolve` to pick the face locally (§9.1, same resolution and extraction as preview), and records in the frozen snapshot (`ExportSnapshot.fonts`) the file it lives in, its index within the file, and the `sha256` digest of the whole file and its byte count. When several faces share one font collection, the digest is computed only once. Faces that cannot be found are recorded as `fallback`. Fonts downloaded on demand (§9.1): those already in the download cache are still recorded as files (`source: 'downloaded'`). Those present in the font directory but not yet downloaded are recorded as `download`, and are downloaded inside the export task before it starts drawing (stage `downloading`, progress by bytes, cancellable along with the export). Once downloaded, they are frozen into this render in the same way and pinned until the task ends. When `fonts.autoDownload` is off, under strict offline mode, or on download failure, the font is recorded as `fallback: 'not-downloaded'` and drawn with the fallback font, with warning `FONT_NOT_DOWNLOADED` explaining the reason (`font` carries the family, weight, italic, fallback family and reason). The export is not rejected, and the user is not asked to choose a substitute font first (already decided in product design §12). Before export, the export panel prompts per `fonts.usage` about fonts still downloading (export waits first), download failures (fallback font is used instead; retry is available), and families not yet downloaded (when auto-download is on, export starts by downloading first; when off, export uses the fallback font, and the user can download now). During stage `downloading` it reads aloud "download fonts · family percentage"; after completion it lists `FONT_NOT_DOWNLOADED` as family → fallback font · reason. At execution, before drawing the first frame, the Worker verifies those files by digest, extracts only the recorded faces into the renderer, and no longer searches the local machine. If a file is missing or has changed, the whole task fails with `FONT_MISSING` ("font cannot be reproduced"), and the final cut does not silently swap to a different font. A family not found at freeze time is drawn by the fallback rules, and the result contains a hint. When the engine host is open and a font is newly installed, if a face is not found and the font directory has changed since the last scan, it rescans before answering. When nothing is found anywhere, it draws by the kernel's fallback rule (missing characters are replaced as a whole run by the same fallback font, with Source Han Sans as the final fallback). Text elements and subtitles both report the missing-font hint. Shaping, line breaking and bidirectional text are all handled in the kernel (cosmic-text), the same as preview.

The only difference from the preview is where the frame pixels come from. Preview video frames are read from the media element (scaled down to the full output size before being fed into WASM, with slight drift allowed during playback). Export decodes each frame at the exact timestamp of the output frame. SVG previews are decoded by the browser, while export rasterizes them with `render-raster`. Everything else (typesetting, transitions, effects, subtitles, unrecognised styles) is the same code in both.

**Evidence**. The export row on the video card (product design §3.2.2) references `EvidenceReceipt` and the export Artifact that passed publish validation. They show, respectively, the video version, the check method, the sampled range and the rendering route. A file's media check and a sampled visual check are different coverage and do not stand in for each other. Shared source code, render entry points with the same name, or a few similar screenshots are not proof of pixel-identical output across devices.

### 9.12 Observability and performance

Record separately the time spent in queue / setup / decode / seek / draw / capture / transfer / compose / audio / encode / mux, plus cache hits, peak RSS, available GPU memory information and fallback reasons. Use the data to locate bottlenecks, and do not attribute all slowness to the browser.

Verify in a frozen environment: frame time, independent random seek versus sequential rendering, single Worker versus multiple Workers, transparent edges, and audio/video duration. Across environments, only defined tolerance levels can be given; byte-for-byte identity is not assumed by default.

### 9.13 Export kinds

Freezing, preflight and publishing (§9.11) apply to all export kinds. Kinds differ only in what they output.

| Kind | Output | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Video file | One video file | Frames, the selected subtitle track and burned-in elements. Track toggles at export time apply only to this export and do not change the video. Implemented: MP4 (H.264 / HEVC + AAC) and WebM (VP9 + Opus). Output width and height (black bars are added when the aspect ratio differs from the canvas), frame rate, CRF or bitrate, range, and whether subtitles are burned in are parameters. Content that cannot be rendered is rejected by default and listed item by item (§9.11, “Implementation of the finished video”) |
| Audio | WAV, MP3 or M4A | Goes only through the sound rendering path and does not render the picture. You can choose the finished mix, only the original sound, or only one dub group (`source`: `mix`, `original`, `{ dubGroupId }`). All are implemented; sample rate, channels, bitrate and loudness normalisation are parameters. The source changes only this export's sound plan, not the video. “Original only” disables instances marked `cuccut.dub` (dubbing and separated background and vocal tracks), and cancels the muting of `mutedItemIds` that each dub plan recorded on this sequence. Ducking added by dubbing becomes invalid together with the instances that trigger it. “Only one dub group” keeps only that group's dub instances and disables all other instances; if the sequence has no such group, it is rejected with `DUB_GROUP_NOT_FOUND`. Video file export accepts the same `source` and only swaps the finished film's sound; the picture stays as is |
| Subtitles and transcript | SRT, VTT, ASS, JSON with word-level timing; Markdown or plain text | Does not render video (fast path in §9.11). Bilingual merging, chapter headings and timecodes are export parameters. Implemented: times are timeline times, and cut words do not appear. A line's width counts full-width characters as 2; each cue has at most two lines and at most 7 seconds, and long sentences are split between words. Bilingual output pairs the translation with each sentence (the translation `cuccut.translation/2` is matched to its word members by `sourceSentenceId`, and when transcription did not store sentences, sentences are split by these members; `/1` from older imported projects is matched by sentence ID; sentences that were only partly kept and stale units are not paired, see video format spec §5.3), or pairs subtitles in another language by time. ASS does basic style mapping; styles it cannot express are written into warnings. Word-level timing is written only when all of it is trustworthy (AT-05). Transcripts (Markdown, plain text) are written by paragraph, using the same rules as the transcript panel in the editor (`TRANSCRIPT_PARAGRAPH`: a break at a marked paragraph start, at a speaker change, or at a pause of 2 seconds or more; also at a sentence end when the paragraph has reached 240 characters, or after a pause of 0.6 seconds or more when it has 60 characters or more). When speakers are on, every paragraph starts with the speaker label (also when there is only one speaker; the colon is always a half-width colon). Options include a paragraph-end timecode, chapter headings written from the chapter markers on the sequence, Markdown front matter, and keeping cut-out parts instead of skipping them (original text before the cut, in source-media time). The format details are in the command and protocol spec §4.4 |
| Project | Project files for other editing software | See below. Implemented: Final Cut Pro 7 XML (`xmeml`), one sequence |
| Portable package | One `.cuccut` file | Implemented, §5.8 |

- **Text preview for subtitles and transcripts**. `exports.renderText` writes no file and creates no task. It produces the body text of subtitles or the transcript using the same freezing, preflight and formatting as `exports.create` with the same settings, and it is byte-for-byte identical to the file exported with those settings. The transcript page of the export panel in the interface uses it to show a preview and copy the full text.
- **Scope**. The whole film, several chapters, several clips, or custom in/out points. Non-adjacent selections can be joined into one file or exported as separate files. The latter gives one export job several artifacts, each validated and published separately, and partial failures are reported honestly.
- **Purpose**. The `ExportSettings` of every export kind accept `purpose: 'deliverable' | 'preview'`, defaulting to `deliverable` when omitted. `preview` is only for internal checks by the agent. It does not enter the session video card, the session artifact list or its count, or the Space (§5.7); background tasks and export files are kept. When no directory is given, output goes to `.cuccut-out/exports/` under the source directory (deliverables go to `exports/`); directories starting with a dot are not scanned. The purpose is frozen with the settings and stored in the snapshot and the Job record, and it does not change rendering, validation or publishing. Previews, short clips and segments that the user asks for are deliverables and are not automatically turned into internal checks based on file name, range or duration. Unmarked historical records keep deliverable semantics.
- **Multilingual audio**. When there are several dub groups, video export can mix in only one selected language, or write one audio track per language, each labelled with its language. Each track is mixed separately and its duration is validated separately.
- **Loudness normalisation** is part of the audio output policy and is frozen in `ExportSnapshot`: a target integrated loudness (−70 to 0 LUFS, default −16) and a true-peak ceiling (−20 to 0 dBTP, default −1.2). It runs after the final mix and belongs to export rather than to a modification of the video. It is off by default; when it is off, the video's mix is exported as is. Audio export and the finished film's sound use the same chain, the v2 master (`audio-dsp`). BS.1770 integrated-loudness normalisation (left unchanged when silent or shorter than 400 ms) and true-peak limiting (the ceiling is the target minus 0.1 dB) alternate for three rounds. Finally, a static fallback based on 4× oversampled true peak brings the signal below the ceiling and clamps it to [−1, 1]. It is deterministic: the Render Worker's `master` subcommand processes the signal as a streaming 4-second window (intermediate results are stored in the task's staging), and the result is bit-for-bit identical to processing the whole signal at once. Transcendental functions use only libm, so the same mix yields bit-identical samples on every platform. Loudness is measured at the output sample rate and channel count; mono is measured as one channel (the same as ffmpeg `ebur128`). The integrated loudness before and after mastering, the true peak after mastering, and the sum of the static gains of each round are written into the publish validation. When loudness cannot be measured (for example, complete silence), only the true-peak fallback is applied, with a warning (`LOUDNESS_NOT_MEASURABLE`).
- **Which machine exports**. Export runs on this machine by default. Handing export to a LAN node requires sending the frozen inputs in full, which goes beyond the node's boundary of “sharing capability, not video” (§6.7). Whether this will be offered is undecided (§14).

**Project export** replaces the video's timeline with a project that other editing software can open (for example JianYing drafts, and Final Cut and Premiere XML). It is a one-way, lossy exchange, not rendering:

- Only objects the target format can express are exported: clip in/out points and positions, audio tracks, subtitle text and timing, and basic transforms. Objects the target format cannot express (code frames, templates, word-level motion effects, and so on) are either rendered to a media file first and referenced as that asset, or omitted. How each item is handled is written to the export report and is never dropped silently.
- Assets are written either by reference or by copy, as the user chooses. Paths in the project must be resolvable on the target machine.
- An exported project cannot be imported back into CucCut to become the same video.

The implemented form is the most basic one: `{ kind: 'project', format: 'xmeml', sequenceId? }`, which writes one sequence (the main sequence by default) as Final Cut Pro 7 XML (xmeml v5). Premiere Pro and DaVinci Resolve can import it. The file name is “<video name>.xmeml.xml”.

- Written out: the sequence's frame rate and aspect ratio; the positions and in/out points of video and image instances on the picture tracks (ordered by `order` from bottom to top), with compositions that have a pre-rendered stand-in written as that stand-in; audio instances on sound tracks, and the sound carried by video instances (on separate sound tracks); disabled and muted instances and tracks are still marked.
- Assets are written by reference only. A linked asset writes its local path; an asset collected into the video writes its path under `blobs/` (`file://localhost/` plus the absolute path, encoded segment by segment). The project file therefore opens directly only on this machine, while the assets are still in place.
- Anything that cannot be expressed is written, item by item, as the task warning `PROJECT_ITEM_OMITTED` (text, graphics, subtitles, compositions without a stand-in, transitions, effects, cropping, transforms that do not fill the canvas, opacity, gain and fades, speed changes and freeze frames, unreadable assets). The warning includes the instance's ID and name. If the sequence has no clips that can be written, the export is rejected with `EXPORT_NOTHING_TO_EXPORT`; if the given sequence does not exist, it is rejected with `EXPORT_SOURCE_NOT_FOUND`.
- Copy-style export, FCPXML and JianYing drafts, and render-to-media-then-reference are deferred (§14). In Space, project files and portable packages both belong to the single kind “package” (`package`).

---

## 10. Dependency updates and minimal changes

Every derived artifact records a fingerprint of its inputs. “Moved but content unchanged” and “content changed but ID unchanged” are judged separately. A single coarse `videoDirty` flag is not enough.

### 10.1 Change impact table

| Change | Update immediately | Mark stale or revalidate | Keep reusing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Subtitle colour, font size | Subtitle layout or style | Reading and safe-area check of the new layout | Speech recognition, natural translation, speech synthesis, unchanged code animation |
| Typo in source text | SpeechDocument version, fingerprint of valid sentences | Corresponding translated sentences, alignment, dubbing | Unaffected sentences and source video bytes |
| Source subtitle line break | User pin, source Cue and Line | Display check | Natural translation, acoustic alignment of the target language |
| Natural translated sentence | TranslationUnit version | Target block, display rewrite, linked dubbing | Original transcription, unlinked languages |
| Dubbing script changed only | DubbingUnit version | That unit's audio, alignment, and motion that responds to the sound | Unchanged natural translated sentences and visual elements |
| Shift a complete spoken sentence | Clip position, projection of subtitles and semantic anchors | Rhythm between sentences and overlays | Text and translation content of the complete sentence |
| Delete words within a sentence | Edited speech view, clips and lineage | Translation, dubbing, semantic blocks, sentence anchors | Original complete transcription |
| Shift a code instance | `fromFrame`, external composition | Packages that depend on master time or external cues need checking | Rendered images that depend only on localTime |
| Code parameters | Instance parameters | Code cache tied to the parameters | Other instances, original audio |
| Shared code functions or fonts | New code package version | Conservatively invalidate all local scope | Native content that does not reference the package |
| Source media bytes change | New AssetRevision | Alignment, proxies, transcription, all dependants | History and frozen tasks bound to the old version |
| Transition | Transition object | Frames of the two instances on either side within the window; the mix within the window when `audioCrossfade` | Frames outside the window, other instances |
| Effect stack or crop | Instance's effect stack or `crop` | Frames where this instance appears, including transition windows with it on one side | Source media and decode cache, other instances |
| Ducking rule, or moving or trimming the instance that triggers it | Rule, instance position | The mix of the target group within the old and new trigger intervals (with attack and release added before and after) | All picture, the trigger group's own sound |
| Chapters | Chapter markers | Exported chapter info | All picture and sound |

### 10.2 Two kinds of impact must not be mixed

`ImpactReport` expresses `semanticChanges`, `retimeOnly`, `staleDependencies`, `renderInvalidation` and `preservedRefs` together.

- Changing one spot may cause a whole segment of code to re-render, but that never justifies redesigning the whole piece.
- Conversely, when the ID is unchanged but the content fingerprint changes, semantic dependencies must still be marked stale.
- The scope of user intent and the scope of render invalidation are always kept apart: invalidating a whole segment's cache for render correctness does not amount to authorization to rewrite the whole segment of creative content.

Every TranslationUnit, DubbingUnit and chart anchor is bound to a valid word view or to an explicit source. Invalidation does not delete old results; it gives an entry point to review, redo, or keep as a candidate. Features whose automatic repair is not yet open must still show `stale` / `orphaned` / `needs-fit`; blocking items found by strict export checks must not be ignored.

### 10.3 Protect the user's manual edits

Manual pins, manual framing, confirmed terminology, the chosen voice and locked ranges are video facts. Automatic layout, generated repairs and source-content replacement must not quietly restore the model's old values.

The impact of replacing a material is computed for the selected instances; other occurrences of the same media stay unchanged unless the user explicitly asks for a batch update.

Once the user accepts a warning, the acceptance record is bound to the input fingerprint and video version at that time. After the relevant dependencies change, it is checked again; a consent given earlier in history is not a permanent exemption. The manual-edit gate for switching transcripts to an edited script (§6.6, `acceptEdited`) is one example of this.

### 10.4 Dependency rules for scenes and sound

| Change | Must update or invalidate | Precondition for reuse |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Move a `local-only` scene | The position and time of the external composition | Internal parameters, material, environment and background inputs are all unchanged |
| Move a scene that depends on placement | The frozen placementContext and the internal frame-seek cache | Only the independent parts that do not depend on that context may be reused |
| Update an external cue table | Animation and sound compilation that read that `cueRef` | The other scenes that are explicitly independent stay unchanged |
| Replace the source of a selected scene | The new code package reference, parameter compatibility and the related cache | `itemId`, lineage and untouched user attributes are kept |
| Manually drag one sound effect | The corresponding AudioItem and pin / override | Identical material bytes and the other cue instances are kept |
| Recompile a SoundCueSheet | Unpinned related instances, orphaned-anchor check | Confirmed manual edits are not overwritten wholesale |

### 10.5 Invalidation scope for edit frame rate and output frame rate

| Change | Must update or check | Should be kept |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Change only `ExportSettings.fps` | Output sampling plan, frame count and tail, encoding cache, quality evidence | Video version, source media, transcript and translation, speech synthesis results, sound speed |
| `changeSequenceFrameRate`, `preserve=time` | FrameSpan, visual keyframes and transitions, the coarse frame remainder of audio, projection and quantization impact | Exact audio and word timing, source AssetRevision, unchanged code packages |
| Request the same actual local moment | Verify all transitive dependencies and the sampling contract | Source-code frame cache with the same dependencies may be reused |
| Change the author frame rate of a code package | New code package version, compatibility of parameters and duration, instance version switch and cache | Other instances still referencing the old code package, and old exports |

Setting only the export frame rate does not require re-translation or re-dubbing. If code truly depends on the number of steps of the output sampling, that dependency must be explicitly frozen, and the cache must not be reused in error.

---

## 11. Client architecture

### 11.1 Responsibilities of the Renderer

The Renderer implements the interface with React, and the only authoritative state it holds is interface state: dragging, selection, playhead, drafts and panel layout. Video data is a read-only projection (Replica), and all changes are submitted to the Runtime through `@cuccut/client`.

Interface components use React Spectrum 2 (`@react-spectrum/s2`). Its `style` macro generates styles at build time; the product's own layout and colors use the same set of design tokens.

Client state uses Zustand, split into several stores by responsibility:

| Store | Contents | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| connection | Connection state, Runtime information, Driver capability snapshot (the most recent probe result of each Driver and the Driver used in the first probe) | Client connection events, the snapshot of the `agents` topic and `agents.updated` (the same view as `agents.list`) |
| directory | List projection of projects and sessions | Snapshot and increments of the `directory` topic |
| timeline | Session content, stored by session ID | Snapshot and increments of the `conversation:<id>` topic |
| shell | Navigation, sidebar width and expanded state, drafts saved per session, access mode and feature-area tabs | Local machine only; persisted to localStorage except for the current page |

- A snapshot pushed by the Runtime replaces the mirror as a whole; increments are reduced by the pure functions of `@cuccut/client`, and unchanged entries keep the same reference.
- A store does not send requests directly. Requests, subscriptions and reconnection are handled by one `RuntimeSession`: it holds the `CucCutClient`, writes topic events into the stores, and components obtain it through React Context to submit commands.
- The main process does not relay business requests. The Renderer obtains the Runtime's address and token through the preload and connects directly over WebSocket. The preload additionally provides only native capabilities such as choosing a directory and showing a file in its folder. The unified attachment picker of the input box returns only the files or directories the system dialog has just selected: directories are not read recursively, and ordinary files carry only their local path. When the Agent supports image attachments, the main process reads the image bytes according to the format, size and count limits of message attachments; the Renderer then hands them to the Runtime through `attachments.prepare` and a one-time upload address. Non-image references are saved in the session draft, and on sending the paths are appended to the body, one per line, in quotes. If sending fails, the draft is kept; on success, the references just sent are cleared.

The pure semantic modules written in Rust are compiled to WASM and used in the Renderer for interaction prediction (for example, snapping while dragging and previews of quantization results). Once the receipt arrives, the receipt takes precedence over the prediction.

### 11.2 Shell: Rail, Home and Space

```text
App Shell
  ├─ Rail (primary: Home / Space / Tools; secondary: Services / Background tasks; bottom: Settings; ownership principle in the product design §2.1)
  ├─ Home Surface
  │    ├─ Sidebar: list projection of conversations + projects
  │    ├─ Conversation: card projection of ConversationProjector + Composer
  │    └─ Panel: editor component + artifact viewer + task details
  └─ Space Surface
       ├─ Catalog: list projection of SpaceEntry
       ├─ Viewers: viewers by kind
       └─ Editor: session on the left + editor component (same as the Panel in Home)
```

- Home and Space are two interfaces within the same application shell. They share one connection to the Runtime and the same set of client stores.
- **The video Replica is shared by `videoId`.** When the same video appears both in Home's feature area and in Space's editor, both read the same projection, subscribe to the same video event stream, and a change submitted in one place is updated in the other through events.
- **There is only one editor component, and only one layout.** Both the Home feature area and opening a video in Space show the session on the left and the full editor on the right. Selection, playhead position and uncommitted drafts are saved by `videoId` and kept when switching between the two entry points.
- Each interface saves its own navigation state and layout. Switching interfaces does not cancel subscriptions to running tasks.
- Each window has its own Renderer and interface state, and all windows connect to the same Runtime.

**Tabs in the feature area.** Agents produce files in many formats and users also need to view web pages, so the feature area opens four kinds of content as tabs: video (editor), file viewer, project file browsing, and web pages. The set and order of tabs are saved per session in the interface state.

- **Project file browsing** reads `projects.files.list` and `projects.files.read`. It is read-only; paths are limited to the directory of the project the session belongs to, and after resolving symbolic links the path must still be inside that directory, otherwise it is refused. A session that belongs to no project is limited to its own working directory. File contents are obtained through a controlled handle of the media channel (§5.6), and the viewer is chosen by the actual content: text with an unknown suffix can still be read, and unsupported binary files show basic information and “Show in folder”; non-existence and read failures are reported separately.
- **Creating a new web page** (the tool card on the new-tab start page) calls `projects.files.create`. The root directory is the same as for project file browsing (it can also be specified directly by `projectId` for the project directory). After resolving `dir` through symbolic links it must still be inside the root directory, and it must not be in hidden, dependency or video directories. The file name takes only the last segment; empty names, `.`, `..` and names starting with `.` are refused. It is created exclusively and never overwrites an existing file; on a name clash it is renamed in turn to “name 2.ext” and “name 3.ext” (名字 2.扩展名, 名字 3.扩展名). When the file belongs to a project, the response returns a locator by project, and it is the same tab as the one opened from project files.
- The security requirements for **web tabs** are in §12.10.

For the interface design, see the product design §2–§5.

### 11.3 Session projection

`ConversationProjector` projects Driver events into thread entries (messages, reasoning, tool steps, approvals). Task entries are written by the Harness when messages are sent and received; video receipts (`video-created`, `video-change`) and slide and design receipts (`slides-created`, `design-created`) are written into the session by tools. Job records are not projected into entries: the cards to display are derived by the client from the records according to the rules below (product design §6.5).

- Card state comes from events and receipts, not from the model's natural language.
- A card stores stable references (`taskId`, `jobId`, `artifactRef`, `receiptId`, `candidateRef`); on click, the feature area opens the corresponding view by reference.
- Streaming text may merge small chunks; terminal states and permission events must not be lost. Before emitting `item.append`, the Runtime merges text deltas within a window of 60 ms per entry and field (the default): the first chunk at the start of the window is emitted immediately, and chunks arriving later are gathered until the window ends and then emitted as one. Before emitting any other event for the same session, replying to `conversations.get`, and writing to disk, the buffered deltas are emitted first, so that the `seq` in the record and the text are at the same watermark.
- Video cards and download cards in the thread (product design §3.2.2) are not projected by the server. The client derives them from the Job records submitted by the agent (`submitter.kind === 'agent'`; `submitter.taskId` links the Job to the message in the session that references it; one card per video, attached after the last reference; if the Job has not yet ended, it is attached by `endedAt` to a later turn when that turn started later; the steps of a fixed pipeline are child records whose `parentJobId` points to the parent task and whose `submitter.kind === 'pipeline'`), together with the `video-created` / `video-change` receipts in the session. `video-created` records the video ID, the name, the position where the editor opens (written the same way as the `target` of a change card) and the version at creation, and records only videos newly created by the agent through `videos_create`. Videos created by a fixed pipeline (importing from a link, and `target.create` for transcription) are not recorded separately; instead, look at the `videoId` in the parent task record: as soon as the video is created (before importing media), it is written into the parent task and saved to disk, and pushed to the client through the `jobs` topic (§7.9).
- `slides-created` records one slide deck (the `target` of the deck directory, written the same way as `video-created`), its title and page count. It is written when the agent's `slides_check` finds no problems; there is only one entry per deck, and later checks update the title, page count and `taskId` (changed to the task of that check, so the card attaches under the reply of that round), while the position stays the same. The thread does not draw it as a tool-step row; the slide card is derived by the client from this receipt (slide design §7.3).
- `design-created` records one design (the `target` of the design directory), its title and artboard count. It is written when `design_check` finds no problems; the other rules are the same as for `slides-created`.
- The status badge in the sidebar is derived by summarizing the states of the session's active Tasks.

### 11.4 Indexed Replica

The renderer builds rebuildable indexes over the authoritative projection: `itemsByTrackId`, time-range indexes, occurrence indexes for words and sentences, and selection lookup tables. They are read-only derived structures and add no video write permission.

Components subscribe by the IDs and fields they need. A change to one subtitle should not force the whole timeline and the full transcript to repaint.

In Zustand, this means: the store keys data by ID (`byConversationId`, and later `byVideoId`), and components use selectors to pick only the one item or the few fields they need. Derivations such as indexes, groupings and sidebar summaries are pure functions whose result is unchanged when their inputs are unchanged; they are computed in the component on demand, cached, and not written back to the store.

What is verified is update granularity, event consistency and interaction budgets. Zustand is only a tool for enforcing these constraints; replacing it should not change the semantics of the Replica.

### 11.5 Visible Window

The timeline, subtitle transcript, waveforms and thumbnails use virtualization with a “visible window + reserved zone”. Zooming, horizontal scrolling and background events are processed in batches within a frame budget. Budgets and window sizes are determined by measurement on each device.

- Resource loading may prefetch ahead.
- Virtualization must not lose keyboard focus, IME state, accessibility semantics, or an ongoing gesture.
- A selected object that scrolls off-screen still has a logical identity; when it returns to the visible area, edits are not resubmitted.
- **Unmounted instances also take part in export, conflict and dependency calculations.** The DOM is not all of the video data.

Implementation constraints (subtitle list, translation list, transcript panel and timeline):

- Vertical lists (subtitles, translations, transcript) mount only the rows one screen above and below the viewport; the rest are collapsed into blank spacer segments that occupy the height. Rows stay in the normal document flow, so DOM order equals row order, and Tab order and screen-reader order are unchanged. Row wrappers carry no outer margin, and rows carry `aria-setsize` and `aria-posinset`. Timeline tracks are few, so they are not vertically virtualized; only clips, dub blocks, cut bands and ticks are horizontally clipped to the visible time window (with one extra screen on each side).
- Stateful rows or items stay mounted whether or not they are in the window: the one being edited, search hits, the one with focus (for vertical lists, together with one row before and after it), the one with an open menu or inline input, the ends of the selection, and the one being dragged or holding pointer capture. Unmounting them would lose uncommitted text, IME composition, focus or gestures. Selection state lives in the store and does not need pinning.
- Behaviours that depend on the DOM (playback follow, search jump, keyboard positioning) must first compute the index or time from the model, scroll there, and align within the row only after the target is mounted. Do not look up elements in the DOM that may not be mounted.
- Row heights are cached by row key from the heights actually measured; unmeasured rows use a content-based estimate. When the container's content width or display view changes, the old heights of unmounted rows are invalidated. The browser's built-in scroll anchoring is disabled; when a row above the viewport measures a height different from its estimate, `scrollTop` is compensated manually.
- Scrolling written by the list itself (anchor compensation, corrections after scrolling to a row) must not be treated as user scrolling. Playback follow only treats the wheel, touch drags and presses on the scrollbar as the user scrolling away.
- Correctness does not depend on `requestAnimationFrame` (it does not fire while the window is in the background). Measurement uses ResizeObserver callbacks and synchronous reads in the layout phase, and the visible range is recomputed immediately on scroll and size changes.
- Projections derived from the document (the paragraph line table, subtitle positions on the sequence, reading speed) are cached outside the component by document version, so they are not recomputed when the panel is remounted.

### 11.6 Separating High-Frequency State from Low-Frequency Document

The playhead, hover and drag transient layers are independent of low-frequency document version updates. Playback does not trigger document-level re-rendering; a document version change does not reset playback state.

Space's list is virtualized too. Thumbnails (`space.thumbnail`) are requested only for visible cards and rows, in-flight requests are capped, and requests for items scrolled out are no longer awaited. Previews are loaded on demand through the media channel.

### 11.7 Time Display

The seconds and timecodes used for display, and the video frames used for fine trimming, read the same authoritative position. Truncation and zero-padding on screen only affect presentation; copying and committing use exact values. For interface behaviour, see product design §5.8.

---

## 12. Security and Isolation

### 12.1 Desktop Shell

The Electron renderer disables Node integration and enables context isolation and the sandbox. The preload exposes only the minimum capabilities the desktop needs.

### 12.2 Local Connections

Local WebSocket connections still require authentication, Origin checks and scope checks. Media channel handles are bound to the video, asset version and consumer (§5.6).

**Reusing the connection in desktop development mode.** Vite's origin may change with the port, and a Runtime started first by the CLI may not have the development origin in its allowlist. The Electron main process, only for registered application windows, for requests whose origin is the current Vite address, and for WebSocket / HTTP requests whose target is the exact port `127.0.0.1` of the discovered Runtime, maps an existing `Origin` to `null`, which the gateway already supports. In HTTP responses, the corresponding `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: null` is mapped back to the application origin. Token, media and attachment handle validation is unchanged; the shared Runtime is not restarted and its allowlist is not widened. External web pages, other ports and unknown frameworks must not go through this mapping; the packaged `file://` and Web service connections keep their existing rules.

### 12.3 Video Write Permission

The Engine Host accepts only the Runtime's private authorization channel. Agent processes, model processes and code Workers do not get VideoStore paths or write permission.

### 12.4 Untrusted Execution Surface

Building code, installing dependencies and rendering code are untrusted execution surfaces. One must not claim safety merely because the code draws inside an iframe.

- The manifest of a published code package declares `permissions.network: 'deny'` and the asset IDs it may access; the runtime environment must actually enforce these restrictions.
- Subtitles, web pages, metadata, code comments and READMEs in files are task material, not instructions, and do not expand permissions.
- A trusted development mode that has the same broad shell permissions as the user carries bypass risk; tool allowlists and prompts cannot provide strong file isolation.
- **When the system cannot meet sandbox requirements, refuse untrusted execution, or explicitly switch to trusted mode and stop claiming strong isolation.** Do not silently degrade to full permissions and then keep calling it safe.

Process isolation is a stability boundary and does not automatically constitute a security sandbox.

### 12.5 Grant

```ts
interface Grant {
  grantId: Id;
  dataKinds: Array<'transcript' | 'frames' | 'audio' | 'video' | 'document' | 'context'>;
  recipient: string;                  // recipient: Provider (openai, custom:<name>, agent:codex…)
  scope: { videoId: Id | null };      // null means all videos
  purpose: string;
  budgetMode: 'estimate-cap' | 'per-call-unknown-cost' | 'free-local';
  budgetCap: { amount: string; currency: string } | null;
  maxCalls: number | null;
  expiresAt: string | null;
  generation: number;
  taskId: Id | null;
  once: boolean;                      // “only this once”
  origin: 'user' | 'approval' | 'provider-enable';
  approvalId: Id | null;
  state: 'active' | 'expired' | 'revoked' | 'exhausted';   // computed on read
  createdAt: string; updatedAt: string; revokedAt: string | null;
  usage: { calls: number; reservedCalls: number; amount: string | null; reservedAmount: string | null; unknownCostCalls: number };
}
```

- Approving a thumbnail does not equal approving the original video. Grants are bound item by item to data kind, recipient, scope and purpose.
- Grants required by the same task may be presented together.
- After the user revokes a Grant, new calls and automatic application are forbidden; a raised `generation` causes calls from the old generation to be rejected. Parts already sent or already billed cannot be erased by local revocation alone, and the interface must state this honestly.
- The conversation data flow of an agent and the data flow of model capabilities are granted separately (product design §7.3).
- The recipient is the Provider, not its account (§6.8). When an API provider switches which account it uses, or adds or removes accounts, the Grant matching does not change.

**Storage.** Grants and the budget ledger live in `store/grants.json` under the Runtime Home (0600, written via temp file plus rename), and are read and written only through the Runtime's `GrantService`. Grants that have ended (revoked, expired, used up) keep only the newest 200 entries; those with reservations are not cleared. They do not enter the video or the Space.

**Who needs a Grant.** Calls to online and agent Providers need one, matched by the kind of data sent out. Sentence-by-sentence synthesis for dubbing sends out the translated text, which is `transcript`; voice-clone upload of a reference recording is `audio` (§5.9, §7.9). The default Grant created when a Provider is enabled does not cover these: the default Grant for speech synthesis contains only `document`, not the transcript. Decided: no loosening (§14). Dubbing sends out the user's transcript, and minimising outbound data requires the user to explicitly issue the Grant. So the first dubbing is rejected at submission with `GRANT_REQUIRED`, and the rejection states which Grant to issue (`remedy.commands`) and that the job is re-run after issuing (§7.9). Local models (`free-local`, data never leaves this machine) and paired LAN nodes (the user's own devices, pairing is authorization, §6.7) do not need it, and their behaviour is unchanged. `context` (the agent conversation's context) is retained only within the model in this version and is not yet connected to the Driver.

**Matching.** For a call, the kinds of data sent out, the recipient, the video and the task are matched against the Grant together: the recipient is the same; each piece of data in the call is in the Grant's `dataKinds`; the Grant's video is empty (all videos) or equal to the call's video; the Grant's task is empty or equal to the call's task; and it is neither revoked nor expired. A “only this once” Grant matches only the single call that names it. When several Grants cover a call, they are preferred in this order: the one belonging only to this task, then the one belonging only to this video, then the all-videos one; within the same level the newer one wins. A Grant whose quota is used up does not count as covering, and the next one is used. Which data a call sends out is determined by the capability (transcription is audio; synthesis, image generation and text generation are text). The caller states it more specifically when it knows more (the translation flow sends the transcript).

**Issuing.** Four sources:

- The user issues it in settings or the CLI (`grants.create`, `cuccut grants create`);
- Issued together with approval (§3.12): by default “only this once”, amount unknown. If the user chooses to continue, a persistent Grant is also issued, whose call limit is taken only from the user's choice and not inherited from the task's requested count. When a persistent Grant has an amount cap but the model cannot estimate the amount, that call uses a separate “only this once” Grant;
- Merged requests: the agent lists in one go, with `grants_request`, the outbound items this task needs (each item is a capability, a Provider, a video, a count and a purpose), and the user approves them together in one `high` approval. Each item issues one Grant that belongs only to this task, with a call limit equal to the requested count (for the persistent choice, as in the previous item). Items already covered or not needing authorization do not enter the approval;
- The default Grant created when an online or agent Provider is enabled (migration rules, §6.8).

A “only this once” Grant issued at approval time, if the subsequent submission is rejected (wrong parameters, model unavailable, quota taken by concurrency), means the call did not happen. The Grant is revoked immediately and does not remain in the list.

**Revocation, tightening and generation.** `grants.revoke` forbids new calls after revocation. `grants.update` narrowing the data kinds or scope, lowering limits, or expiring early all count as tightening. Both increment `generation` by one. Jobs admitted under the old generation that are still queued fail with `GRANT_REVOKED` when they start executing, their reservations are released, and no data is handed out. A job already running finishes and settles as usual, because the data has already been handed out. The revocation result honestly lists calls already counted, amounts, the number of calls with unknown amounts (`alreadySent`) and the Jobs in progress, and explains that these cannot be erased by local revocation. After a default Grant is revoked it is not automatically reissued; it is issued again only when the Provider is re-enabled.

**Records.** Job records carry `grant` (`JobGrantUse`): which generation of which Grant was used, the kinds of data sent out, and the reservation and settlement (§7.8). `grants.usage` lists a Grant's usage and the Jobs that used it. The `grants` topic pushes changes to Grants. Grant management is exposed only to the desktop interface and the CLI: the Web service's browser allowlist does not include `grants.*` or the `grants` topic, the MCP service's tool catalogue does not contain them, and agents can only request through `grants_request`.

**Grant and task budget.** A Grant answers “may this data be handed to this recipient”. The task budget (§3.2, §7.8) answers “how many calls and how much money this task may spend in total”. The `taskId` on a Grant only limits which calls can match it (Grants issued by `grants_request` and by approval belong to one task). The task budget is recorded separately under the task contract's `budgetPolicyRef` and totals across all Grants used by this task. The task budget can only tighten, not authorize: calls not covered by a Grant still need authorization. The task budget is set and changed by the user in the task contract (both the desktop interface and non-read-only Web may do so, and it can only tighten within the Grant). In Web read-only mode it can only be viewed; the MCP service has no contract, and agents can only read it.

Budget admission is described in §7.8.

### 12.6 Logs and diagnostics

By default, no raw video, audio, full subtitles, prompts or keys are collected. Product analytics is limited to necessary event and performance summaries; non-essential collection can be turned off. Before exporting diagnostics, the content can be previewed, redacted, and its scope shown. Logs do not record sensitive local paths or credentials. Keys and tokens appear only in the auth headers sent to providers or nodes, and on the stdin and stdout of the credential helper (§6.8): they do not go into command-line arguments, environment variables, logs, events, Job records or error details.

Crash reports are first stored in a local send queue, and the user decides item by item whether to send them; the content can be viewed before sending. "Auto-send" is a preference that is off by default. Usage statistics contain only counts of feature use, with no media, text or paths, and can be turned off. Clearing derived caches is an explicit command that deletes only data that can be rebuilt (§5.1).

### 12.7 LAN nodes

The node service is the only listening surface allowed to bind a non-loopback address, and it exists only when the user has turned on sharing. Its source gate, pairing and token rules are in §6.7. The node service does not expose video, sessions, Spaces or the local file system; it accepts only capability tasks. The gateway, MCP and media channels always listen on loopback only.

### 12.8 External services

The external services in §4.8 add to the Runtime's attack surface, and all follow these rules:

- **Off by default, loopback by default.** Apart from the node service (§12.7), all external services listen only on the loopback address, and there is no switch to make them listen on other addresses.
- **Authentication.** Each service uses its own token, unrelated to the local gateway token, which is handed to the user only through the CucCut UI or CLI. The MCP service and model API service issue tokens per client (the two services' tokens are not interchangeable): one per external app, carried as `Authorization: Bearer`. The plaintext is shown only once at creation; the Runtime stores only a salted hash, the token stays valid across restarts, it can be revoked individually, and revocation takes effect immediately. A request with no token or a wrong token gets 401. Requests carrying a browser `Origin` are rejected by default (the Web service accepts only its own origin), to prevent web pages from calling through loopback; requests whose `Host` is not a loopback address are also rejected, to prevent DNS rebinding. A tokenless local connection, if offered, must be an explicit option with a stated risk; the first version does not provide it (§14).
- **Tokens written into host configs.** `cuccut mcp install` (§4.8) is the only exit for a token from the CucCut UI and terminal: where the host's own environment-variable section can hold it, the token goes there and the config holds only a reference (Claude Code); where it cannot, the token is written in plaintext to the host's user-level config file (Codex, Cursor, Gemini CLI), and the command's `--help` and result both state this, recommend the `ask` level, and give the revocation command. Config writes always read, modify and atomically replace: only the `cuccut` entry is touched, other keys are kept as they are, an unreadable config produces an error and is not overwritten; newly created files are readable and writable only by the owner, existing files keep their permissions; existing entries are not overwritten unless `--yes` is given. JSON configs keep the original file's indentation and line endings. If writing the Claude Code entry fails, the original token from `settings.json` is put back. Each install is a new client; when an entry is replaced, the old client is identified from the old token and revoked, and if it cannot be identified, the result lists same-named clients for the user to revoke.
- **Browser for the Web service.** The session cookie is `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Strict`, and is obtained by exchanging a one-time, two-minute, hash-only access code (§4.8). The access code appears in the link's fragment and in the terminal that started the command: this is the exception to "tokens only in auth headers", constrained by being one-time, short-lived, kept out of logs, URL query strings and process arguments (`--launch` has the browser open a login page without the code, and the user pastes the code from the terminal). Cookies on loopback are not isolated by port (other services on the machine's other ports also receive them), so WebSocket upgrades and login must carry an `Origin` equal to the service's own origin (`http://127.0.0.1:<port>` or `http://localhost:<port>`), and `Host` must be one of those two; requests the browser marks as initiated by another site (`Sec-Fetch-Site` not `same-origin` or `none`) cannot obtain content that requires a session. Every response carries `Content-Security-Policy` (the client page allows only its own origin; scripts allow no inline code or `eval`, only compiled WebAssembly; `frame-ancestors 'none'`), `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` and `X-Frame-Options: DENY`. What a browser session can do is the same as the desktop UI (including letting agents run and answering approvals), minus the management surface outside the allowlist; the remaining risk is a program on this machine that can control a browser, which is on a par with one that can control the desktop UI.
- **Minimal surface.** External services offer no settings, credentials, management of the external services themselves, file access outside the project directory, or arbitrary command execution. The available methods are an allowlist, not the full set of gateway methods.
- **Files the MCP service touches are only within the project.** The MCP service opens tools that read and write local files (list in §4.8); paths are always judged by their real path, and anything that reaches outside via `..` or a symbolic link counts as outside:
  - **Read**: asset imports (`importAsset` in `edits_apply`, and `assets_import`) accept only files inside the project directory of the video, and anything outside is refused with `PATH_OUTSIDE_PROJECT`.
  - **Write**: writes go only to fixed subdirectories within the project the video belongs to. Export (`export`) writes to `exports/` and the subdirectories already present within it; frame grabbing (`videos_frames`) writes to `exports/.cuccut-out/frames/<videoId>/`; downloads from links (`download`, and `transcribe` given a `url`) go to `downloads/` of the owning project. None go into the video directory (including `video.db`) or `.ccut`. Tools that are not tied to a video and write elsewhere (`artifacts_save`, `downloads_save`) are not exposed.
  - **Local path arguments**: parameters such as `file`, `files` and `outDir` are always refused by the service with `INVALID_ARGUMENTS`; files can enter only via in-scope videos, project assets, or links. The `transcode` tool, which accepts only local files, is not exposed.
  - **New videos**: `videos_create` must be given a registered project; when the access policy is a video allowlist, a new video is added to the allowlist, and this does not widen the allowlist to other videos.

  Rationale: an MCP client is another program that comes in with one token, and the text it reads may carry prompt injection. Confining the files it can reach to the project directories the user has already handed to CucCut means that if the token leaks or the client is manipulated, the loss does not leave the project and CucCut cannot be used to read or overwrite the user's other files; outputs land in fixed project subdirectories, so the user knows where to look. New videos must be written into the allowlist because videos outside it do not exist for the service; without that, the client could not see the videos it created. yt-dlp's installation and consent do not go through MCP (§12.9).
- **Content from outside is material.** Text and files in external requests are handled per §12.4: they are not instructions and do not expand permissions.
- **Size and concurrency have limits.** Uploads to the model API service are capped at 256 MiB, JSON request bodies at 8 MiB; beyond that the service answers 413 (if `Content-Length` already exceeds the limit, not a single byte is read), and closes the connection. Uploads are written as they are read to `staging/model-api/` in the Runtime Home, deleted when the request ends, and anything left by the last run is cleared when the service starts. Each client has a cap on generation requests in flight at once; requests over the cap get 429. The service accepts no file path parameters: input comes only from the request body.
- **Auditable.** Every external request records the caller, tool or endpoint, target and result, but not the body, media content or tokens. The user can see recent requests and current connections in the UI and can stop the service at any time. Stopping the service immediately disconnects existing connections and cancels pending service approvals; Jobs it started that are still running finish as usual, and the user decides in the task center whether to cancel them.

### 12.9 Managed external tools and install actions

CucCut depends on some external programs that are not shipped with the app, and it also installs things on the system on the user's behalf. These actions are not model inference, nor an execution surface for code packages; they are constrained separately:

**Managed external tools** (for example ffmpeg, video download tools):

- Tools not distributed with the app are never downloaded by CucCut in the background. On first need, the tool's name, purpose, source (download address), version, size and license are shown; a download happens only after the user consents, and consent applies only to that tool.
- **Registry.** The only downloadable tool is yt-dlp (importing from links, §7.9); the audio.cpp engine for music generation is not in the registry, as it is a component of the music model package and downloads with the model package (§6.3); ffmpeg is not downloaded by CucCut, still resolved via `CUCCUT_FFMPEG` and PATH, and is listed only in the status. Each tool has a manifest: purpose, probe command, minimum version, and whether user consent is required; downloadable tools also have releases: version, per-platform file names, size and sha256, official address, license and homepage.
- **Status.** `externalTools.list` reports each tool's status: `installed` (path, version and source: `env` environment variable, `bundled` for the copy shipped with the app (a reserved value; no tool currently uses it), `user` user-specified, `managed` a copy CucCut downloaded, `system` PATH), `missing`, `outdated` (below the minimum version), `unavailable` (cannot run), plus the reason, remedy, consent and download notes (`offer`). `externalTools.detect` re-probes. Probing only runs `--version` locally and does not touch the network. Resolution order: environment variable (`CUCCUT_FFMPEG` for ffmpeg) → user-specified path (`externalTools.setPath`) → managed copy → PATH of the Runtime's tool environment (on Windows, extensions are added per `PATHEXT` as on the command line; if what is found is a script such as `.cmd` or `.bat`, it is reported as unavailable, and a specified path of that kind is not accepted either — CucCut does not run tools through the command interpreter). A user-specified path that cannot run is reported as unavailable and is not quietly swapped for another. Specifying a path does not imply consent.
- **Consent.** Tools that require consent (download tools) record when and how consent was given (`app`, `cli`, `agent-approval`), and it can be withdrawn (`externalTools.consent`); after withdrawal, flows that use the tool are refused at submission, before each step, and on retry (`TOOL_CONSENT_REQUIRED`). Consent records, user-specified paths and managed-copy registrations are kept in `store/external-tools.json` in the Runtime Home.
- **Download** (`externalTools.install`). Without an explicit `consent: true`, it is refused with `TOOL_CONSENT_REQUIRED` and returns `offer`, and nothing is downloaded; with it, consent is recorded and a task of `kind: 'toolInstall'` is submitted. If the manifest's sha256 is unknown, it is refused with `TOOL_MANIFEST_INCOMPLETE`: digests are not made up, and it is better not to download (§14). The task downloads the file into `tools/.staging/`, reports progress by actual bytes, checks size and sha256 (on mismatch the bad file is deleted, `TOOL_DOWNLOAD_INTEGRITY`), sets the executable bit (0755), atomically renames it to `tools/<tool>/<version>/`, and then runs `--version` once more to confirm it runs. On cancel, a partially downloaded file is kept and resumed next time. Refused in strict offline mode (`OFFLINE_STRICT`). The first version does not verify upstream signatures (§14).
- **Download source.** The default is the official release address in the manifest; setting `tools.downloadEndpoint` or the environment variable `CUCCUT_TOOLS_ENDPOINT` (which takes precedence) can point to a mirror, with the address in the form `<base>/<tool>/<version>/<filename>`; the base must be `http(s)://` without credentials, query parameters or fragments. Files downloaded from a mirror are checked against the manifest in the same way. The audio.cpp engine component of the music model package (§6.3) also downloads from this mirror, at `<base>/audiocpp/<version>/<filename>`.
- **Updating the copy on the system** (`externalTools.update`, belongs to the "install actions" below). On probing, the way the executable was installed is determined from its real location (following symbolic links), and the `update` field of the status gives the method and the full command: Homebrew (real location under `<prefix>/Cellar/<formula>/<version>/`, uses `brew upgrade` with the same prefix, and adds `--fetch-HEAD` for development builds installed from source), pipx (under `pipx/venvs/<package>/`, `pipx upgrade`), pip (entry script, uses the interpreter in its shebang with `-m pip install -U`, adding `--user` for user-directory installs; on Windows the entry is the `.exe` launcher in the Scripts directory, the interpreter is taken from the `#!` line in the launcher, treated as pipx if it points to a pipx venv, and `--user` is added if the entry is not beside the interpreter), Windows package managers (the entry is a copied `.exe` shim or a symbolic link, recognized before a standalone program is identified: winget's real location is under `WinGet\Packages\<package ID>_<source>\`, upgraded non-interactively with the winget found on PATH (or the app execution alias under `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Microsoft\WindowsApps`), upgrading only this one package and querying only the winget source; machine-wide scope requires administrator rights; Scoop's entry is under `<Scoop dir>\shims\` with a same-named `.shim` beside it, or under `apps\<app>\<version>\`, executed as Scoop's own `scoop.cmd` does, running `scoop.ps1 update <app>` via System32's powershell.exe, and a global install gets only `scoop update <app> --global`; Chocolatey's is under `bin\` or `lib\<package>\`, always requires administrator rights, and only `choco upgrade <package>` is offered), and official standalone programs (a binary or zipapp, running its built-in `-U`); where the method cannot be determined (self-written wrapper scripts, tools installed by uv — its environment has no pip) and for copies CucCut downloaded, there is no option, and the UI lists common practices. Confirmation has two steps: if `command` is not sent back, or differs from the method as it now stands, the request is refused with `TOOL_UPDATE_CONFIRM_REQUIRED` and the method is handed back; only when the user has seen the full command and sends back the same one (in the UI, this is the run button next to the displayed command) is a task of `kind: 'toolUpdate'` submitted; the command is not reassembled and caller-supplied arguments are not accepted. The full command is written for the Runtime host's platform so it can be pasted directly into that host's terminal: on Windows, PowerShell quoting rules apply (with `&` placed before the first word when it is quoted); other systems use POSIX shell. The command is not run through a shell, runs in its own process group, has stdin closed, carries environment variables that turn off colour and prompts, and has a 15-minute timeout; on stop the whole group is terminated (Windows has no process groups, so `taskkill /T /F` ends the whole process tree, with no first step asking it to exit). Output has terminal colour stripped, lines rewritten with `\r` keep only the last version, and the last ~32 KB is pushed to the UI with the task record (`JobRecord.command`); the full output and the before/after versions are written as task artifacts and logged. After it ends, the tool is re-probed regardless of outcome; a non-zero exit code makes the task fail with `TOOL_UPDATE_FAILED` (winget exiting with 0x8A15002B when there is no upgradable version counts as success; negative exit codes or those above 0xFFFF are shown to the user in hexadecimal). The original copy is the responsibility of the tool that installed it, and CucCut does not touch it. If the current user cannot write to the location being changed, it is not executed on the user's behalf (`TOOL_UPDATE_MANUAL`): for standalone programs, a command with `sudo` is given (on Windows, the instruction is to open a terminal as administrator and run it), and for everything else only the original command is given with the reason explained; on Windows access control lists cannot be inspected, so the location decides, and anything under Program Files, ProgramData and the Windows directory counts as requiring administrator rights. While an update is in progress, flows that use the tool refuse to start (`TOOL_UPDATING`), and in strict offline mode it is refused (`OFFLINE_STRICT`).
- A copy CucCut downloads itself does not modify an existing install on the system and is not added to the user's `PATH`. `externalTools.remove` deletes only managed copies, and is refused while a task is installing, updating or using it (`TOOL_IN_USE`).
- Tools run with a minimal argument set: arguments are arrays, no shell is used, and input and output are confined to the task's directory; yt-dlp always runs with `--ignore-config` and `--no-plugin-dirs`, runs in its own process group, and on cancel the whole group is terminated (on Windows, `taskkill /T /F` ends the process tree). Its output is data, not instructions. The tool's name, version and source are recorded on the Job that used it.
- **Who can use it.** Only the desktop UI and the CLI. The Web service does not expose `externalTools.*` and `pipelines.*` (§4.8), and the handlers refuse them again for browser sessions and external services; the MCP service does not offer tool installation or consent. Its `download` is available only after the user has already installed yt-dlp in CucCut and consented, and its destination is limited to existing videos within scope or registered projects (`newVideo` requires `project`, and a new video is registered within the service's scope, §4.8, §14). Agents use `download` in a session under §3.12: a download is `command`, and is `high` when yt-dlp must first be downloaded or consent added; the approval summary carries the source, version, size and license. Agents have no tool to update tools.
- CLI: `cuccut external-tools [list]`, `external-tools detect [name]`, `external-tools install <name> [--yes]`, `external-tools update <name> [--yes]`, `external-tools path <name> <file>` (`--clear` to clear), `external-tools remove <name>`, `external-tools consent <name> [--revoke]`. `install` first prints the source, version, size and license; `update` first prints the install method and full command; both ask once in the terminal, and are refused when not in a terminal without `--yes`. When `update` runs, it prints output line by line, and at the end says which version it was updated to. Importing from a link is the derived command `cuccut download <link> [--project …] [--video …] [--transcribe] [--subs en,zh-Hans] [--audio-only]`.
- **Local browsers** (`externalTools.cookieBrowsers`). For "website login" in video downloads, lists the browsers on this machine that have a cookie store (§7.9), checking only whether the file exists and its modification time, without reading cookies or running the download tool; the result carries the platform of the Runtime's host, and the UI prompts for system authorization accordingly (macOS keychain and full disk access permission); on Windows it prompts to fully quit Chromium-based browsers first, as Chrome, Edge and Brave use app-bound encryption and cookies cannot be read otherwise. As with `externalTools.*`, it is not exposed to the Web service.
- When a dependency is missing, the feature that needs it is reported as unavailable with a remedy (in the manner of §6.2), and other features continue to work as normal.

**Install actions** (installing or upgrading the agent runtime, installing the system's package-manager dependencies, installing built-in Skills into other agents' directories):

- Each one is executed only after the user confirms it each time. The confirmation shows the full command to be run or the path to be written; the output of execution is shown verbatim and recorded in the log.
- Actions that need administrator rights are not escalated within CucCut: they are handed to the system terminal for the user to run, and CucCut only re-probes afterwards.
- **Skill installation** places the Skills that ship with the app into other agents' Skills directories (global or for a given project), as a link or a copy. Skills are versioned and declare the CLI and protocol versions they need; a copy behind the app is reported as stale. Installation writes only to the Skill's own directory, and refuses to overwrite when the target location has other content. Sessions inside the app do not depend on installation: the Harness provides the same guidance directly at the start of each session (§3.8).
- These actions are executed by a process that holds write permission on the user's directories, initiated through the Runtime's commands and leaving a record; the Renderer does not write to user directories directly (§12.1).

**Adding and importing Agent skills** (the opposite direction from "Skill installation" above: putting skills from elsewhere into CucCut's own user-level directory, §3.8):

- **Who can use it.** `skills.add` (local folder), `skills.importGithub` (GitHub) and `skills.remove` are open only to the desktop UI and the CLI; they are not in the Web service's allowlist (`WEB_METHOD_NOT_ALLOWED`), and agents have no matching tool. They only copy files and never execute content, so the user's own request is the confirmation and no further prompt appears; the UI shows the source before starting, and both results and errors carry the target directory (`path`) and the source (`source` or `url`).
- **Writes only to its own directory.** The target is `<Runtime Home>/skills/<id>/`; `id` defaults to the folder name (for import, the subdirectory name or repository name) and can be given otherwise. If the target already exists, or a skill with the same id exists (including built-in ones), it is refused with `SKILL_EXISTS` and not overwritten; this check happens before copying or going online. Content is assembled first in `skills/.staging/`, `SKILL.md` is validated, the source record is written, and then it is atomically renamed to the target; if any step fails, the staging directory is deleted, leaving no half-finished skill.
- **Limits.** A skill has at most 200 files and 8 MiB in total, `SKILL.md` at most 64 KiB, and relative paths at most 300 characters; beyond these, `SKILL_TOO_LARGE`. `skills.readFile` and `skills_read` read at most 256 KiB of text at a time. Symbolic links, special files, or a missing `SKILL.md` give `SKILL_INVALID`; local adding cannot point to the user-level directory itself or to a folder within it.
- **GitHub.** Accepts `owner/repo`, repository URLs, and `…/tree/<ref>/<subdirectory>` (`ref` is the segment after `tree/`; branch names containing `/` are replaced by a commit sha or tag). Uses only public endpoints, anonymously: without `ref`, the default branch is used; `ref` is first resolved to a commit, and everything afterwards is fetched at that commit. It walks down the subdirectory to the target directory and recursively lists its entries; before downloading any file, it refuses if the entries contain symbolic links, lack `SKILL.md`, exceed the limits, or if the listing is truncated. Submodules and dot-prefixed entries are not fetched. Each request has a 30-second timeout, and the whole import has 2 minutes; redirects are followed only to GitHub's own hosts. The source record contains the address, `ref`, commit and subdirectory. When rate-limited, `SKILL_GITHUB_RATE_LIMITED` (with `retryAt`); network problems `SKILL_GITHUB_NETWORK`; not found or private `SKILL_SOURCE_NOT_FOUND`; no automatic retry.
