Refused in strict offline mode (`OFFLINE_STRICT`).
- **Removal** deletes only that skill's directory in the user layer; built-in skills cannot be removed (`SKILL_BUILTIN_NOT_REMOVABLE`), but can be turned off.
- CLI: `cuccut skills [list]`, `skills show <id>`, `skills add <folder> [--id <id>]`, `skills import <address> [--id <id>]`, `skills enable|disable <id>`, `skills remove <id>`; `cuccut chat --skill <id>` picks a skill to send.

### 12.10 In-app web pages

The web tabs in the function bar show untrusted external content, strictly separated from the app's own UI:

- Each web tab is an independent, sandboxed browsing context that uses a session partition separate from the app UI: no Node capabilities, no preload, no access to Runtime endpoints or tokens, and no access to `file:` or the app's own origin.
- Only `http` and `https` are allowed. Account and password embedded in the address are stripped. Navigation to loopback addresses is denied by default, so a page cannot use the app to reach the local Runtime and external services (§12.8). The user's own dev servers must be allowed origin by origin.
- New windows, downloads and permission requests (camera, microphone, location, notifications) are denied by default. Downloads are handed to the system browser. "在浏览器中打开" (Open in browser) is always available.
- Web content is material, not instructions (§12.4). Agents read web pages with their own tools, not through this tab. When the user hands web content to a session, that is an explicit reference.
- Login state of a web tab stays in its own partition and can be cleared in settings. It is not shared with the system browser.
- The desktop host handles in-page find, ignore-cache reload and device viewport emulation. Each tab keeps its state, and find results accept only the latest request ID. Shortcuts pressed while a web page has focus are routed back to the owning window. Device emulation is enabled or revoked only after the native view is ready. The view is hidden while menus and popovers are open, so it does not cover the UI. Popups do not launch external apps. Denied permission requests and handed-off downloads show a dismissible notice.
- The file viewer does not read files through a web tab. It uses the restricted handle from `media.resolve`. The PDF.js Worker, CMap, standard fonts and image-decoding WASM are bundled with the client, use a local Blob Worker and embedded binary resources, and follow the same path in Electron's `file://` and on the Web. It only parses and renders; PDF actions are not executed. HTML is statically sanitized and displayed in a `srcdoc` sandbox with no permissions. The CSP blocks network access, scripts and forms, and link navigation attributes are removed. Relative-path images in Markdown resolve against the directory of the article, into the same kind of locator: adjacent paths in the session or project; for Space entries, joined against the source directory, where deliverables outside the source directory first look for a registered entry in the same directory and, on desktop, fall back to `{ localPath }`; local files use `{ localPath }` in the same directory. They are likewise obtained as restricted handles via `media.resolve` and displayed. Out-of-bounds or unobtainable images show alternative text. Addresses with a protocol are still handled by react-markdown's default filtering. Absolute paths and `file:` are not resolved, and an article's references do not widen the readable scope.

---

## 13. Code organization and version contract

### 13.1 Target directory layout

```text
apps/
  desktop/          Electron Main / preload / UI entry
  runtime/          Supervisor / Node Worker entry
  cli/              Thin client
  web/              Browser client: static build of the same UI package as desktop, served by the Web service (§4.8)
packages/
  ui/               React UI: shell, Home, Space, editor components (React Spectrum 2 + Zustand)
  client/           Command / Query / Subscribe SDK
  protocol/         Wire protocol: envelope, methods, topic events, domain types and runtime validation
  runtime-core/     Assembly, gateway, identity, service lifecycle
  harness/          Conversation / Task / Run / AgentSession / Context
  agent-drivers/    Agent machine protocol adapters
  video/            VideoService facade, Review, RenderService
  models/           Capability contracts, Provider interface and registry, model pack catalog, model service configuration
  providers/        Adapters for online providers: one directory per vendor (§6.4); agent Providers (`agent/`, §6.9) execute through the Driver
  nodes/            LAN capability sharing (§6.7): node service (pairing, source gate, HTTP surface for capability tasks) and initiator side (node client, remote node Provider, paired nodes, discovery)
  services/         External services (§4.8): ServiceManager, MCP service, model interface service, listening surface and access policy of the Web service
  jobs/             Job / Application / budget and resource scheduling, fixed workflows (§7.9)
  tools/            Transport-independent tool catalog
  code-runtime/     Browser / Remotion, isolated builds
  runtime-storage/  Interfaces and two backends for sessions, tasks, approvals, event ledger, Space directory, user library (§5.9), preferences (§5.10), content index (§5.11), credential storage (§6.8)
  process-host/     Processes, leases, private executor channel (JSON-line Worker client), lookup of native programs shipped with the app (`CUCCUT_BIN_DIR`, cargo artifacts in development), per-platform install hints when an external program is missing
  editor-wasm/      Synchronous loading of `bindings/editor-wasm`: transcribed sentences and source fingerprints, stage boxes, engine value ranges, element style catalog. Node reads from file; the browser inlines via Vite. Shared by jobs, Runtime and UI (§7.9, §13.2)
  contracts-generated/   Generated Rust DTOs / Schemas
crates/
  editor-semantics/ Time, cropping, anchors, selection
  video-model/      Video exchange DTOs: shared by engine, render plan and WASM preview
  video-engine/     Video transactions, re-validation of permission scope, receipts
  video-store/      SQLite / revision / outbox / Blob references
  speech-doc/       Core of subtitles and translation (ported from v2): words / sentence / block / cue / line; translation and alignment engines; subtitle export and checks (§7.9). `speech-worker` and `bindings/editor-wasm` link through `speech-doc-bridge`; the `translate` workflow uses it through the Worker (§7.9)
  speech-doc-bridge/ Lossless mapping between v2 TranscriptDoc and v3 speech / translation bodies; transcribed sentence and source fingerprints; pure functions (§7.9; video format spec §5.2, §5.3)
  speech-worker/    Worker process: subtitle steps that call models (currently translation: brief, paged translation, quality gate, alignment and time projection of translated subtitles). Model calls are handed to the Runtime (§7.9; protocol in command protocol spec §4.5)
  render-graph/     Compilation and dependencies of frozen inputs
  frame-render/     Frame rasterization: draws each layer of the frame plan as a render-kernel element into one RGBA frame (`fx` order, transitions, subtitle groups, template layers, judgment of what cannot be drawn). Native export links it directly; preview uses the same code via `bindings/preview-wasm` (§9.1)
  render-raster/    CPU render core: DrawOp instruction IR and fingerprints, tiny-skia rasterization replay, text engine (cosmic-text), effects / filters / masks / transitions, procedural sources (Lottie, confetti, progress, visualization, hand-drawn), CPU executor for frame plans, BCF layout and recording, image comparison. Built-in fonts live in the crate's `assets/fonts/` (ported from v2 `ccut-render`). Wired through `frame-render` (§9.1). The `media` feature is on by default (resource loading, frame supply, mixing and packaging, built-in fonts compiled into the binary). The WASM build turns it off. The `program` asset and GPU feature groups have source but cannot be enabled (§13.6)
  timeline-render/  Drawing layer for timeline elements: shapes, stickers, visualizations, progress, confetti, hand-drawn and other elements compiled into DrawOp instruction streams and same-source live scene nodes; texture nodes for external media (`external-media-scene`) and the BCS2 scene wire format (`scene-wire`). Pure functions shared by export and preview (ported from v2 `ccut-timeline-render`). Wired through the element pipeline of `subtitle-render` (§9.1)
  subtitle-render/  Subtitle overlay render kernel: typesetting and style projection, per-word animation, Designed Caption recipes, subtitle transitions, template layers, timeline element composition, DrawOp fingerprints and CPU rasterization, ASS style header. Built-in subtitle styles and sprites live in the crate's `assets/`; fonts are read from `render-raster`'s built-in fonts (ported from v2 `ccut-subtitle-render`). Wired through `frame-render` (§9.1). The `host` feature (project reading, media decoding, system fonts) is on by default; the WASM build swaps it for `wasm-safe`
  motion/           Shared animation kernel: easing and springs, keyframe sampling, animation / effect / text presets and shapes, registries for stickers, visualizations, progress and confetti (built-in recipes live in the crate's `presets/`, embedded at compile time), flow compilation and per-word motion. Pure functions; transcendental functions go only through libm (ported from v2 `ccut-motion`). Wired through `timeline` and the render kernels
  scene-primitives/ Deterministic primitives of the render core: easing, color, SVG paths and stroke styles, time expressions, channel sampling, text typesetting and glyph grids, SVG animation, mixing constants, content fingerprints, static layout, and the BCF parsing (resolve → `Ir`) and checks that the v2 render entry consumes (ported from v2 `ccut-core`). Wired into the render kernel; the BCF part has no callers
  timeline/         Timeline semantics and element model: source-level cuts, segment layout and time mapping, semantic follow and word anchors, detection and criteria for speech cuts, keyframes, voice-based ducking, element schema and validation, geometry, conversion of effects and transitions, template layers, on-screen text. Pure functions; algorithm internals use floating-point seconds (ported from v2 `ccut-timeline`). VideoEngine's cuts and keyframes, `render-graph`'s ducking, and `frame-render`'s geometry, keyframes, effects and template layers call it (§14)
  audio-dsp/        Offline audio DSP primitives: filtering, synthesis, reverb, ducking, BS.1770 loudness and true-peak limiting, mastering (ported from v2 `ccut-audio-dsp`). Loudness, true peak and limiting are used by the Render Worker's loudness mastering (§9.13); the rest is not yet wired (§14)
  waveform/         Pure-function format layer for audio-derived data: BCW1 peak envelope, BCS1 spectrum, beat detection (ported from v2 `ccut-waveform`; STFT and beats are behind the `dsp` feature, which read-only BCS1 consumers turn off for WASM). The render kernel reads BCS1 spectra; no producer exists yet (§9.1)
  media-core/       PTS, decoding, audio, proxies; decoding, encoding and probing via ffmpeg / ffprobe subprocesses
  media-probe/      Pure-Rust media probing: container, geometry, duration, alpha and edit list, with optional ffprobe fallback (ported from v2 `ccut-media-probe`). Ported, not yet wired (§14)
  media-native/     Platform-native codecs, without starting ffmpeg: single-frame extraction, sequential frame streams, H.264 + AAC MP4 writing. macOS uses AVFoundation / VideoToolbox, Windows uses Media Foundation (plus D3D11 playback frames). Other platforms report unavailable, and the caller falls back to ffmpeg (ported from v2 `ccut-media-native`). Wired: the Render Worker's export decoding and H.264 MP4 encoding prefer native and fall back to ffmpeg (§9.11); `render-raster`'s `media` also gets frames and encodes through it
  export-worker/    Worker process: Render Worker (export preflight and frame-by-frame compositing, loudness mastering for export)
  model-runtime/    Local inference: backend abstraction and pipeline (library) for decode feeding, VAD, ASR, alignment and speaker identification; ported from v2 `ccut-speech-core` / `ccut-speech`, completed in batches (§14 “本地模型的移植” (porting local models))
  engine-host/      Worker process: Engine Host
  model-worker/     Worker process: Model Worker (§6.5)
  credential-helper/ Credential helper: reads and writes the system secure store via stdin / stdout (§6.8); answers only unsupported outside macOS
  face-detect/      Face detection process: YuNet via pure-Rust tract inference, stdio JSON lines, adds face boxes to `videos_frames` frames (§3.5)
bindings/           WASM / Engine IPC / Worker contracts: `preview-wasm` (editor preview: frame plan and frame rasterization, same code as export), `editor-wasm` (editing semantics shared by UI and Node: sentence and source fingerprints, stage boxes, value ranges, style catalog)
tools/              Build and code generation scripts
fixtures/           Time, editing, language, rendering, security, recovery
```

This is a logical division of responsibilities. It does not require splitting out every package mechanically. For directory placement and naming rules, see [repository conventions](../repo-conventions.md).

Dependency direction:

- UI / CLI → Client → Protocol;
- Harness → Tools → Video / Models / Jobs;
- Video → private Engine Client;
- Rust does not depend on Electron, chat rendering or any commercial Provider.

### 13.2 Single source of contracts

Video and semantic types generate TypeScript DTOs and JSON Schema from Rust. The Runtime's Task / Job / Driver protocol generates its runtime validators from a single TypeScript source. A common envelope wraps these two kinds of domain payload. No second hand-written `TimeMap` that differs slightly is allowed.

Rust is the single source of semantics for `MediaTime` / `Rate` / `TimeDomain`, second parsing, frame quantization, audio sampling and time mapping. TypeScript uses only the generated DTOs and bindings.

The editing rules, constants and built-in catalogues that the interface and Node need are also defined only in Rust, and are called synchronously through `bindings/editor-wasm`. TypeScript keeps no separate copy of any number or table: transcript sentences and their source-text fingerprints; the stage box and the swap of `place`, and the default placement of kinds (the same implementation as the frame-plan layer, `render_graph::item_box`); the value ranges and defaults for effects, ducking, volume, keyframes, animation and confetti; the style catalogues for waveform, progress bar and confetti (the built-in recipes of `motion`). What stays in TypeScript is only the interface's own design: copy (names of models, palettes and shapes), the common ranges of sliders, step sizes and readout multipliers, gestures and snapping. Copies not yet moved over are listed in §14 “Pending items in editing semantics”.

### 13.3 Version contract

The following versions evolve independently of one another:

| Version | What it identifies |
| --- | --- |
| `videoSchemaVersion` | Video format (video format spec) |
| `bundleSchemaVersion` | Code bundle manifest (code bundle spec) |
| `adapterContractVersion` | Execution contract of the code-composition Adapter |
| `eventProtocolVersion` | Business protocol and events (command and protocol spec) |
| `timeContractVersion` | Syntax of decimal seconds, rounding rules at each boundary, and sampling policy |
| `runtimeVersion` | Node Runtime |
| `engineVersion` | Rust VideoEngine |

Rules:

- The Worker negotiates capabilities at handshake; a frozen task record holds all versions relevant to execution.
- On an unknown required capability: refuse to run, or open read-only. Never ignore it and write back afterwards.
- Optional vendor extensions are kept in their own namespace; when an unrecognized optional extension is read, write-back must not drop it.
- Every version upgrade needs an explicit compatibility check. Upgrading `timeContractVersion` must not change the meaning of any committed position in an existing video.

### 13.4 Importing legacy projects

New videos use the `cuccut.video` format. The core design has no long-term compatibility layer for any legacy format. The only exception is the old layout left over from a rename before release: a video directory that has only `movie.db` and is marked with the format identifier `cuccut.movie` is upgraded in place once when opened (files, tables and keys are renamed, content unchanged). After the upgrade it is no different from a newly created video, and the Space list also recognizes such directories. Likewise, a project directory from before the upgrade has no `.ccut/project.json`, and the video does not record its owning project: on first open, the project keeps its original identifier by the registered path and writes the marker; when a video is first claimed, it adopts the project it sits in, and `videoId` does not change (§5.1).

Importing a legacy project is an independent, one-way conversion task: back up the old source → freeze the conversion rules → verify the target → pass regression on opening, previewing and key edits → deliver the new video. An old writer and the new Engine must never write the same video at the same time.

- Conversion does not silently infer exact time semantics from JSON numbers. If old data has only a visual `durationFrames`, the known playback length is derived from the old frame rate and the source precision of this value is marked; unknown acoustic precision is not invented.
- Whether an import is complete is judged by capability and behaviour tests, not by “the JSON parses”.
- An unsupported contract is refused on write-back.

**The element model carries over from v2.** The kinds and parameters of on-screen elements (text, graphics, stickers, generated elements, filters, masks, transitions, keyframes and element animation, per-word motion, caption styles) adopt the v2 element model directly as formal fields of the video format. Only the container changes (sequences, tracks, instances, documents and rational time). v3 has not been released, so this change keeps no compatibility for existing v3 videos, and v2 element data is no longer stored as `cuccut.legacy-*` data or placed in `extensions`. The conversion from a v3 video to the render core is therefore a thin adapter layer that maps type by type, and legacy project import writes to the same set of fields.

### 13.5 Ownership of logical components

`CapabilityRegistry`, `GuidanceRegistry`, `ContextBarrier`, `InspectionService`, `ExecutionAdmission`, `ResourceResolver`, `CreativeCatalogue` and `SpaceCatalog` are logical services inside the Runtime, and none of them becomes a separate daemon. The Video authoring layer contains `SceneRecipe` / `BuildLedger` and the SoundCueSheet compiler.

- `FrameTicket`, `EvidenceReceipt` and `CapabilitySnapshot` belong to their own versioned contracts.
- Video, time and audio instances are still defined by the Rust single source.
- The authorization decision of `ResourceResolver` is in Node; reference and version relationships are in VideoStore.
- Applying a SoundCueSheet still goes through VideoEngine.
- The browser page's frame-fetch queue and capture handshake sit inside the Adapter and do not enter the video format.

### 13.6 Porting from v2

v3 is an architectural rework of v2 (`cuccut-app`). Mature domain implementations are ported from v2 as they are, together with their tests, fixtures and built-in data, not rewritten. v3's architecture is adapted only at the boundaries. Before writing any domain module, check v2's `core/crates/` first.

**Adaptation at the boundaries**, applying to every ported module:

- **Time.** The v2 algorithms use floating-point seconds internally, and the port does not change this. At the points where data enters and leaves the crate, it is converted to v3's rational time and frame grid. VideoEngine quantizes it uniformly and issues a quantization receipt (video format spec §2).
- **Network and online services.** Rust does not go online and does not know any commercial Provider (§1.4). The online adapters that v2 wrote in Rust (cloud transcription, synthesis, text-to-image, asset libraries) are not ported. Only their endpoints, quotas and retry policies are referenced, and they are implemented in TypeScript in `packages/providers`. What can be carried into Rust is only the rules, for example the licence decision for assets and the shape of source records.
- **Model inference** goes into the Model Worker (§6.5): one model per process, reading only the files listed in the read-only manifest. Steps that need to call a language model hand the call back to the Runtime (§7.9).
- **Writes.** The v2 code that reads and writes the project directory directly is not ported. Writes go only through VideoEngine (§1.3). What is carried over is the rules inside that code.
- **Pure semantics that the interface needs** are compiled to WASM for the interface to call (§13.2), and are not written again by hand in TypeScript. Copies already hand-written in the interface following v2 or the prototype (caption cut points, preset data, visualization, stage posture, export range, glossary and so on) are switched to calling the same Rust code once the corresponding module is ported. Purely interface geometry and state stay in TypeScript.
  - Packaging of `editor.wasm`: the interface (renderer process and web version) inlines it with Vite via `?inline`. After the Runtime is bundled into `out/main/runtime.js`, the desktop build places it beside the bundle (`out/main/generated/editor.wasm`). On loading, it looks there first, and if not found, walks up the directory tree to the repository's `packages/editor-wasm/src/generated/`. Without a build, calls that use it report `EDITOR_WASM_UNAVAILABLE`.
  - Built-in recipes parse only the categories the interface needs (`motion::preset_registry::parse_builtin_catalogue`); the whole registry is not linked into `editor.wasm`.
- **Element model** carries over from v2 (§13.4), and **the picture** has only one implementation (§9.1).

**Inventory.** “As-is” means the whole crate is brought over; “split” means only the domain part is brought over, and the host part is replaced by modules v3 already has; “not ported” means v3's architecture already has a replacement, or it is something specific to v2.

| v2 crate | Treatment | v3 landing point |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `ccut-flow-core` | As-is | `speech-doc` (ported) |
| `ccut-speech-core`, `ccut-speech`, `ccut-transcribe` | As-is (the existing recognition in `model-runtime` is a trimmed port of these; it adds alignment, speaker separation, Qwen3 1.7B, Whisper, MOSS) | `model-runtime`, `model-worker` |
| `ccut-tts`, `ccut-tts-core` | As-is (local engines; domain modules are handled with dubbing) | Synthesis backend of `model-runtime` |
| `ccut-separate`, `ccut-image-local` | As-is | Separation and text-to-image backends of `model-runtime` |
| `ccut-models` | Split: the fixed file list and hashes are brought over; download and storage are handled by `packages/models` | `packages/models` |
| `ccut-lang` | As-is | With the crate that uses it |
| `ccut-cloud-stt`, `ccut-image-cloud`, `ccut-audio-gen`, `ccut-image`, `ccut-qwen-image` | Not ported, reference only | `packages/providers` |
| `ccut-motion` | As-is; the built-in recipes (`core/presets/builtin`) come with the crate | `motion` (ported) |
| `ccut-render` | As-is. The patched resvg (`core/vendor/ccut-resvg`) comes with the crate in `vendor/`, and as in v2 is a workspace member. The default enables `media` (resource loading, frame supply, native mixing and muxing via ffmpeg, built-in fonts compiled into the binary), the same as v2. WASM builds always use `--no-default-features --features wasm-safe`; the registry in the root `Cargo.toml` is `default-features = false`, so a dependent that does not explicitly ask for `media` does not get it. The small part of `ccut-exec` that `media` uses (command construction, ffmpeg / ffprobe lookup, process-local paths) is moved into the crate's `exec` module. The lookup rules follow v3: first `CUCCUT_FFMPEG` / `CUCCUT_FFPROBE` (if an explicitly given path is unavailable, no other source is substituted), then `PATH`, with no guessed directories added. User-specified paths exist only in the Runtime, and the caller passes them in at wiring time. In v2, `ccut-compile` was part of `media` and served only `program` assets; here it is split into a placeholder `program` feature: when not enabled, loading a `program` asset reports an error, and when enabled, compilation fails. GPU features wait for the GPU-composition batch that introduces wgpu | `render-raster` (ported, wired through `frame-render`, replacing `render-core` and the interface's Canvas drawing, §9.1) |
| `ccut-timeline-render` | As-is, feature table unchanged (`media`, `wasm-safe`, `external-media-scene`, `scene-wire`). WASM builds use `wasm-safe`; GPU and caption-compositor outputs add `scene-wire` | `timeline-render` (ported, wired through the element pipeline of `subtitle-render`, §9.1) |
| `ccut-subtitle-render` | As-is, feature table unchanged (default `host`, plus `wasm-safe` and `external-fonts`). The registry in the root `Cargo.toml` is `default-features = false`. Built-in data is carried in the crate's `assets/`: caption style recipes (`core/assets/captions/styles`), the six textures used by the recipes with their licence notes, and `subtitle-designs.json`. Fonts are not carried separately; they are read by relative path from `render-raster/assets/fonts/`. `subtitle-gallery.json`, which this crate does not read, is not carried. The two parts that depend on it from outside the v2 kernel also land here: the ASS style header (`ass_style_sheet` in `ccut-kernel`) and the check `place_out_of_canvas` (`ccut-engine`). `speech-doc` does not depend back on the renderer | `subtitle-render` (ported, wired through `frame-render`, §9.1) |
| `ccut-compositor` | As-is | Goes with the batch “GPU composition and preview WASM”; the preview WASM is already wired with the CPU core (§9.1) |
| `ccut-core` | As-is. The entry of `ccut-render` and its audio and frame plan both consume the `Ir` produced by BCF parsing, and parsing is tied to composition, the edit path, asset requirements and programmatic sources, so it cannot be split. The BCF-specific modules (`resolve`, `lint`, `events`, `migrate`, `composition`, `editpath`, `assets`, `program_path`) come only because of the render entry. They are removed after the render entry switches to consuming the v3 frame plan (the element and adapter layer batch). `ccut-protocol` uses only two BCF version constants, which are moved into the crate | `scene-primitives` (ported) |
| `ccut-media-native`, `ccut-media-probe` | As-is | `media-native` (ported; the export loop batch becomes the native codec path of `media-core`), `media-probe` (ported) |
| `ccut-wasm`, `ccut-wasm-gpu`, `ccut-wasm-subtitle`, `ccut-wasm-editor` | Not ported; keep their export methods | `bindings/` |
| `ccut-timeline` | As-is. The template layer, brand types and version constants used by `ccut-protocol` are moved into the crate. The data model is adapted to sequences, tracks and instances at wiring time | `timeline` (ported). It was first a standalone crate; at wiring time, the timeline semantics (caption-based cuts, semantic follow, keyframes, speech-based ducking) are called from or merged into `editor-semantics` and VideoEngine. The element model becomes fields of the video format, and validation and conversion are provided to the render-adapter layer and the editor |
| `ccut-editor-core` | Split: the timeline, cuts, short films, animation, stage, elements, export options, caption layout and style library, glossary and chapters are brought over; interface shell, agents and remote computation are not ported | `bindings/editor-wasm` and the corresponding domain crates |
| `ccut-audio-dsp` | As-is | `audio-dsp` (ported) |
| `ccut-waveform` | As-is (waveform, spectrum, beat) | `waveform` (ported) |
| `ccut-score` | As-is | `score` (score synthesis and stem splitting) |
| `ccut-keyframe` | As-is (shot cut points, frame selection) | `media-analysis` |
| `ccut-reframe` | Split: the plan and tracking are brought over; the inference of the detection model goes into the Model Worker | `reframe` |
| `ccut-editable` | As-is, with input changed to the v3 video | `project-export` (CapCut drafts, FCPXML, Premiere, Resolve, MLT, Kdenlive) |
| `ccut-videotool-core` | Split: planning for compression, merging and failure attribution is brought over | `media-tools` |
| `ccut-dubbing` | Split: dubbing planning, placement and quality checks are brought over; inference goes into the Model Worker. v2 has not finished it itself, so it is scheduled last | `dubbing` |
| `ccut-engine` | Split: the prompts and response validation of each process are brought over; model execution is not ported | `speech-worker` |
| `ccut-workspace` | Split: word-level editing and the review model for spoken-text cleanup are brought over; the write transaction is not ported | `speech-doc`, VideoEngine |
| `ccut-kernel` | Split: parsing of time notation, the numeric parameter table, the command impact table, short-film snapping and candidates, covers and frame selection, asset licensing, score track placement, pronunciation annotation, on-screen text scanning and translation, and the rules for upgrading legacy projects are brought over; the command line, services, remote and integrations are not ported | Their respective domain crates |
| `ccut-protocol`, `ccut-config`, `ccut-project` | Split: template validation, parameter units, brand rules and display size with rotation are brought over | Their respective domain crates |
| `ccut-executor` | Not ported; its standalone FLAC encoder can be brought over | — |
| `ccut-serve`, `ccut-agent`, `ccut-jobs`, `ccut-runtime-client`, `ccut-client`, `ccut-secrets`, `ccut-exec`, `ccut-proc`, `ccut-videotool`, `ccut-skill-link` | Not ported; the small part of `ccut-exec` used by the render core is moved into `render-raster` (see above) | Runtime, Harness, Jobs, `process-host`, credential helper |
| `ccut-compile`, `ccut-bcf-host` | Not ported: BCF is v2's code-picture format, and v3 uses code bundles (§8); therefore the `program` feature of `render-raster` is only a placeholder | — |

Built-in data and fixtures come with the crates that use them: `core/presets/builtin` (presets for animation, filters and masks, transitions, text, graphics, progress and visualization), `core/assets` (fonts, elements, caption styles), `core/fixtures`. Fixtures sit under each crate's `tests/fixtures/`. Third-party assets come with their licence files and attributions. v2's design documents are copied to `docs/design/` alongside the corresponding modules, and the data model names in those documents refer to v2.

**Batching.** Dependencies come first; mutually independent items run in parallel. Each batch carries tests, and wiring is a batch of its own.

| Track | Order |
| --- | --- |
| Captions and translation | Crate port; mapping to the v3 document; wiring of translation, caption layer and checks; post-processing of transcription (§14 “Wiring of the caption and translation core”) |
| Local models | Restore the removed interfaces and adapt the manifests; alignment and speaker separation; more recognition models; vocal separation; speech synthesis; text-to-image |
| Rendering and export | Base libraries (audio processing, waveform, media probing); motion library and deterministic primitives of `ccut-core`; element model into the video format; CPU render core and fonts; element layer and adapter layer; caption rendering; native codec and export loop; GPU composition and preview WASM; loudness mastering; project export; quick fixes |
| Editing semantics | `ccut-timeline`: crate port; comparison with the v3 element model; wiring of caption-based cuts, semantic follow, keyframes and speech-based ducking (§14 “Porting the timeline semantics”); the domain part of `ccut-editor-core` reaches the interface via WASM, replacing the copies hand-written in the interface |
| Analysis and tools | Shot cut points and frame selection; smart reframing; compression and merging tools; score; short films; deep rules for the glossary; dubbing planning and quality checks |

---

## 14. Items pending review

The items in the table below are carried out under the "assumptions of the current document"; where an assumption says undecided, decide it when implementation reaches that point.

| Item | What needs to be decided | Current document's assumption |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Two price records (§6.10, §7.8) | Whether the usage-estimate price table (`model-prices.ts`) and the model description `ModelPrice` that budget admission reads should be merged into one | Kept separate for now: the price table is used only for after-the-fact estimates; `ModelPrice` is given only to units whose upper bound can be computed at submission time, and no built-in model has one at present. When merged, the price in the model description is derived from the price table |
| Boundary between Rust persistence and the Engine Host (D01, D02) | Whether to accept the IPC cost of "Node as the only entry point + Rust as the single writer"; whether small native capabilities are embedded bindings | A separate Engine Host process; embedded bindings need separate measurement |
| Disk layout (§5.1) | Handling of linked assets in the task snapshot: on volumes that do not support copy-on-write, whether to offer a "copy first, then export" option | Decided: assets stay in place by default and are copied into the video directory only when the user chooses so; the video directory is not self-contained. On volumes without copy-on-write, verify by digest and do not copy |
| Project registration (§1.5, §3.10) | Where the project and video identifiers live; how to recognise a directory that was moved, copied, or has lost its marker; when a copy gets a new identifier, whether the Space user marks and generation records follow the copy; where the outputs of a session without a project go | Decided: the identifier is in `.ccut/project.json` in the project directory, written only when a project is created or opened; the registry is only a rebuildable index. Moving keeps the identifier; a copied directory gets a new identifier; without a marker, it falls back to the registered path (upgrade path). `video.db` records the owning `projectId`; a video in a copy gets a new `videoId` and records `previousVideoIds`; when two old videos are copies of each other they cannot be told apart (§5.1). A copy does not inherit sessions or user marks. Outputs of a session without a project: the first time a video is created, the session binds to the new project, and the video and the files in the working directory move into the project; `scratch/` exists only while the session has no video (§3.10) |
| First Driver (D08) | The concrete interface of the Codex machine protocol; when the other Drivers are connected | Decided: Codex and Claude Code have passed integration tests. GitHub Copilot, Gemini CLI, Cursor Agent, Grok, Kimi Code, and agents added by users are connected through the same ACP implementation; the shared implementation has run a full real-device session through the claude-code-acp adapter, and all of them can start sessions. Agents whose own behaviour was not personally run end to end are marked "not tested in CucCut". Pi is connected through `pi --mode rpc`; the protocol path is verified and enabled, but it has not been tested with a real model account. OpenCode is connected through `opencode serve`; on a real device it is verified with a free model (§3.1, §3.11) |
| Waiting policy of ContextBarrier (§4.6) | Maximum wait time; default handling of IME composition text and gestures in progress | Prompt the user to choose; time limit undecided |
| Retention policy for inspection evidence (§9.8) | How long `InspectionArtifact` is kept; whether it counts toward Space | Does not enter Space; retention period undecided |
| Lease fairness and resource estimation (§7.7) | Aging parameters; the quota reserved for preview; handling of estimation bias | The mechanism is decided; parameters await benchmarks. Implementation simplification: interactive tasks always rank ahead of background tasks, with no aging; the interactive reservation is a fixed amount (§7.6); actual usage is not monitored during execution, and estimation bias is not corrected. The Model Worker that recognises model packs estimates by weight (§6.5); the coefficients apply only to the resident amount after the two Qwen3-ASR model packs are loaded (0.6B about 0.71 GB, 1.7B about 2.46 GB). Undecided: synthetic model packs still use fixed requirements; estimating per model pack requires measuring the resident amount after each engine loads and its inference peak first |
| Peak estimates for each kind of task (§7.7) | Memory, GPU memory, threads, and staging amounts for the Model Worker, export, transcoding, and model download | Conservative initial estimates, concentrated in `packages/jobs/src/resource-profiles.ts`; not benchmarked; to be calibrated on real machines. If too low, `resources.capacity` can be used to reduce the capacity |
| Verification method for `timeDependencies` (§8.6) | How to verify that the time dependencies a code package declares are true | Unverified means conservatively invalid |
| Contract for SoundCueSheet and pin | Default behaviour when a pin conflicts during recompilation | Keep the pin and prompt about the conflict |
| Visibility markers for Space directories (§5.7) | Enumeration of purpose markers; when candidates disappear from Space | Deliverable outputs enter; discarded candidates move to the trash |
| Retention periods for Space and video deletion | How long after entering the trash items are physically deleted; the GC grace period | Decided: preference `space.trashRetentionDays`, default 30 days; at startup and every 6 hours, expired entries are cleaned up by checking references through `space.purge`; entries that are referenced are kept (§5.7). Outputs in the Artifact Store with no references are deleted by background sweeping under the rules of §7.3 |
| Retention policy for asset versions and undo history (§5.3, §5.5) | When the bytes of deleted assets may be reclaimed; how long undo records are kept | Undecided. `asset_versions` and `undo_records` are never deleted; blob GC on video close reclaims only crash leftovers and expired staging; the bytes of deleted assets stay with their version records |
| Identifiers and references for Space entries (§5.7) | Whether a video entry's `id` uses videoId or a position; whether an output's reference is `artifactRef: VersionRef` or an artifactId | Implementation simplification: a video entry's `id` is a digest of its source and relative path (`sp_…`), and videoId is in `ref.videoId` (before a copy changes its identity on open, a copy has the same videoId as the original; videos are still listed when the engine is unavailable; user marks follow the position). An output entry's `id` and `ref.artifactId` are the artifactId, not a `VersionRef` |
| Source of Space directories (§5.7) | How "index only registered files, do not scan the whole project directory" coexists with the file lists in the interface | Implementation simplification: the project directory and the working directory of sessions without a project are still scanned (with depth and count limits); the interface and session file lists depend on this. Registrations from `space.import` are recorded separately in the Runtime Store; if a file is gone, it shows `missing`. Files outside the project are copied into the project's `imports/` |
| Precision of Space derived state (§5.7) | Which fingerprint `source-changed` uses; what is authoritative for `candidate` / `applied`; where costs and derivation relations come from | Implementation simplification: `source-changed` only compares the `videoRevision` frozen at export with the video's current version; any commit to the video counts as a change. Application records are used only for tasks whose results were not applied: outputs whose latest application had no commit (`stale-input`, `rejected`, `cancelled`) are listed as `candidate`, with `statusDetail` giving the reason. `applied` checks whether the timeline (any sequence) uses an asset imported from the result, and is updated only after the content index rereads the video. `origin` has no `cost`, `derivedFrom`, or `derivedFromVideo`. Outputs of fixed workflows (§7.9) are not listed as separate entries; once published into the source directory, they appear through scanning |
| Retention of Space and the Job Ledger (§5.7) | After the Ledger prunes old records, how outputs that exist only in the Artifact Store stay in the directory | Decided: Space keeps its own output records (`store/space-artifacts.json`, holding only the facts needed for derivation), merged with the Ledger during derivation; records are removed once all outputs are deleted. Pruned tasks cannot be found through `jobs.inspect`, and failure placeholders disappear with the Ledger; for external services to see an output, the submitter must be in the Ledger, and outputs that appear only in output records are invisible to them. Tasks pruned before this version cannot be recovered |
| Reference checks for physical deletion in Space (§5.5, §5.7) | Which references the reference graph covers; the command that deletes a video | Implementation simplification: only checks the asset files linked by videos (facts from the content index) and the outputs used by in-progress tasks; if a video cannot be read or the index is behind, it is treated as referenced. History, frozen snapshots, templates, and the user library are not checked. Output files are deleted directly from the Artifact Store directory (the Artifact Store has no delete interface). Decided: deleting a video is `videos.delete` (moves it into the source directory's trash); physically deleting a deleted video also checks other videos' links, its in-progress tasks and tasks awaiting reconciliation, and files in the directory not managed by the video; only files the video manages are deleted, and the original files of linked assets are not touched (§5.5, §5.7). While confirming the lock, the engine briefly opens the video in write mode |
| Matching and updating in cross-video search (§5.11) | Tokenization, ranking, and the inverted index; how unopened videos discover changes | Implementation simplification: substring matching after NFKC and lowercasing; multiple words combined with AND; no tokenization; no relevance ranking. Words of three or more code points get candidates from the SQLite FTS5 trigram index; shorter words are filtered by substring segment by segment, then verified with the substring rule. Unopened videos discover changes through the modification times of `video.db` and WAL, and then their versions are compared |
| `space.continueInConversation` (§5.7) | Which context is carried when continuing a conversation from an entry, and into which session | Decided: when a session is given, the entry must be visible in it; entries that belong to a project start a new session in the project, while entries that do not belong to a project return to the session they are in or that produced them (a new session if that one no longer exists). Only the entry's identifier and metadata are carried, recorded on the session and sent with the next message (§5.7). An entry that belongs to no project and has no source session (for example, one generated by an external service), once placed in a new session, is not visible to the agent through `space_list` |
| Process granularity of the Model Worker (§6.5) | Whether one process per model pack is too wasteful on machines with tight GPU memory; whether one process may host several models | One process per pack; shared processes are only a later optimisation |
| Crash threshold and deadlines of the Model Worker (§6.5) | Concrete values for N crashes in M minutes; number of automatic retries; deadline for the cancel ack | One retry; ack in 5 seconds; threshold undecided |
| Resuming from segment checkpoints (§6.6) | The JSONL checkpoint format and validity rules for P1 | P0 reruns the whole Job |
| Inference backend matrix on non-macOS (§6.5) | Backend and device choices for ASR, VAD, and alignment on Windows / Linux; memory pressure signals | Decided: on non-Apple Silicon, Qwen3-ASR 0.6B / 1.7B and MOSS run on candle, sharing the same model repositories as MLX and dequantized at load time; devices are chosen in the order CUDA (`cuda` feature, NVIDIA only), then CPU; `CUCCUT_GPU` turns the GPU off; Silero is fixed to CPU. Whisper (GGML) on NVIDIA runs on ggml's CUDA (`whisper-ggml-cuda`); AMD / Intel GPUs serve Whisper only (GGML, Vulkan); installers are split by GPU (§6.5). Resource estimates are by parameter count: on CPU, 4 bytes per parameter counted as system memory; on CUDA, 2 bytes per parameter counted as GPU memory. Current status: the candle batch is complete; on this machine it was run with real weights and cross-checked against MLX (requires `--release`: an MLX build in the dev profile differs by one token from the release build on the fixture's brand name, and v2 is the same; candle matches in both profiles), plus end-to-end and Worker CPU transcription tests; weights with `scales` accept only 4- or 8-bit, as with MLX; `cargo check` passes for `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu` with `--no-default-features --features backend-candle` (C build scripts such as `onig_sys` need a MinGW cross compiler). Not verified: building and running `cuda`, and real Windows / Linux machines. The Model Worker's default features remain MLX + Core ML; `npm run build:engine` on platforms other than Apple Silicon switches to `backend-candle,whisper-ggml` (`CUCCUT_WORKER_FEATURES=cuda,whisper-ggml-cuda` makes candle and Whisper use CUDA; `whisper-ggml-vulkan` makes Whisper use Vulkan). The Whisper (GGML) batch is also complete: v2's whisper.cpp backend (`whisper-rs` 0.16) and its tests were ported unchanged to `backend/ggml/`, with backend `ggml` and device `cuda`, `vulkan`, or `cpu` (reported by the ggml backend name of the selected GPU; when both are compiled in, CUDA takes precedence over Vulkan). Registered `whisper-large-v3@ggml` (`ggml-large-v3-q5_0.bin`) and `whisper-large-v3-turbo@ggml` (`ggml-large-v3-turbo-q8_0.bin`); both files are in the same revision of `ggerganov/whisper.cpp`; the large-v3 manifest is registered in the sibling directory `ggerganov/whisper.cpp-large-v3`, with downloads still taken from upstream (the manifest's `sourceRepo`); the two model packs are installed and removed separately (as in v2). The sha256 values are taken from v2; sizes are requested from the source at install-plan time. Unit tests, the Worker handshake and load check (`--features whisper-ggml`), and `cargo check` for `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu` with `--no-default-features --features backend-candle,whisper-ggml` have been verified (whisper.cpp's bindgen needs `BINDGEN_EXTRA_CLANG_ARGS_x86_64_pc_windows_gnu` pointing to the MinGW sysroot, otherwise it falls back to a mismatched built-in binding). Device selection and labels (CUDA first, `vulkan` when only Vulkan is present, `cpu` when `CUCCUT_GPU=off`) have unit tests; `whisper-ggml-cuda` has only checked feature resolution. Not verified: transcription with real weights (the weights were not downloaded); building and running `whisper-ggml-cuda` and `whisper-ggml-vulkan` (no nvcc or Vulkan SDK on this machine); real Windows / Linux machines. The candle variant of text-to-image (Qwen-Image) (`qwen-image-2.1@candle`, same repository, dequantized at load time) is listed on platforms other than Apple Silicon; peak and timing are in §6.5: on CPU it can produce images, but 1024² takes several hours, so practical use needs CUDA (not verified on this machine). The ten speech synthesis model packs each also have a candle variant (`…@candle`, same repository), listed on platforms other than Apple Silicon; resident amounts and timing are in §6.5: on CPU it can synthesize, but one sentence takes several minutes, so practical use needs CUDA (not verified on this machine). The candle variant of vocal separation (HTDemucs-FT) (`htdemucs-ft@candle`, same repository, weights upcast to f32) is listed on platforms other than Apple Silicon; CPU peak and timing are in §6.5; `cuda` is not verified on this machine. Windows packaging is described below under "Windows installer package"; Linux has no installer flow yet; memory pressure signals are described below under "Model Worker supervision and priority on Windows" |
| Single GPU installer choosing its backend at runtime (§6.5) | Whether Windows can ship one GPU installer that picks CUDA or Vulkan according to the user's GPU | Not possible now; separate CUDA and Vulkan installers (§6.5, "Windows installer package"). Reason: candle's `cuda` links CUDA's import libraries through cudarc's `dynamic-linking`, so model-worker.exe depends directly on the driver's `nvcuda.dll` and on cudart / cublas; on a machine without an NVIDIA driver the process does not start at all, so runtime selection never gets a chance. whisper-rs-sys statically links ggml and its backends into the exe, and a statically linked ggml-cuda likewise depends directly on `nvcuda.dll`, cudart, and cublas (so enabling CUDA only for Whisper is the same); ggml's dynamic backends (`GGML_BACKEND_DL`, one `ggml-*.dll` per backend, loaded at runtime according to availability) do not work on this build chain. Future path: whisper.cpp switches to ggml's dynamic backend DLLs (`load_sidecar_backends()` in `backend/ggml` is already reserved and will load the `ggml-*.dll` next to the exe); candle / cudarc switch to `dynamic-loading` (the driver is found at runtime with `LoadLibrary`; if it is not found, CUDA is reported as unavailable instead of the process failing to start). Once both are done, one package can carry both CUDA and Vulkan, with ggml choosing CUDA first in registration order. None of this has been tested on hardware |
| Manifest and resource estimate for the music model pack (§6.3, §7.7) | The sha256 and size of YuE2 GGUF are taken from the LFS records of the download source at a fixed version; the config files are streamed at that version and hashed; this repository has not installed or run inference for it on this machine; the engine peak of 10 GiB is estimated from upstream's measurement on a 32 GB GPU (about 8.9 GB) with headroom | To be checked after installing and running once on real hardware; until then `peakEstimated` marks it as an estimate, and sizes are as given in the manifest |
| Hashes and sizes in the built-in model manifest (§6.3) | The sha256 values of the earliest registered transcription model packs (Qwen3-ASR 0.6B MLX 4bit and Silero VAD) are taken from values the earlier CucCut pinned for the same version; this repository has not re-verified them online; there is no offline, trustworthy source for file sizes | The old values are used and will be re-verified against upstream during review (not treated as verified until then). Sizes are obtained with a HEAD request to the source at planning time; if unavailable, they are reported as unknown and confirmed against the estimates (about 680 MiB and 1.2 MiB). A model pack whose manifest lacks a hash is refused for installation; it is not downloaded and hashed afterwards |
| Local model download and offline use (§6.3) | Whether strict offline mode may download from a LAN mirror; how long partial staging is kept before cleanup; whether several model packs may download in parallel | Strict offline refuses all downloads and repairs (including mirrors); paused staging is kept until it is installed again, discarded (`discard`), or the model pack is deleted; installs are serial, one at a time |
| Entry point for install management (§6.3, §4.8) | Whether the browser and external services may install or delete local models | Decided: neither the MCP external service nor the web service provides it; agents may only download through `models_install` in a session and check through `models_test`, both confirmed by `command`; deleting and repairing are not allowed |
| First batch of online providers (§6.4) | Which providers to connect first for each capability; the default model choice for each | Transcription: OpenAI, Google; synthesis: OpenAI, ElevenLabs; text-to-image: OpenAI, Google; text: OpenAI, Google; plus OpenAI-compatible endpoints |
| Credential storage (§6.8) | Which secure storage to use on Windows and Linux | Decided: release builds use the OS secure storage (through `credential-helper`); development builds store to a local file; the build type decides; when unavailable, do not fall back to a file. The macOS Keychain is implemented; Windows Credential Manager and read-only migration of v2 credentials are implemented and cross-compiled for the Windows MSVC target, with native runs still to be verified. Linux Secret Service is not implemented; the helper returns `unsupported` and does not fall back to a file |
| Windows installer (§13.1) | Signing; when to release (updates are in the next row) | A packaging script exists (`npm run package:win`, NSIS per-user install and zip, x64) plus a manually triggered `desktop-windows` workflow, with one package each for the standard, CUDA, and Vulkan versions, chosen by GPU (§6.5). Unsigned, so SmartScreen will block it. All three versions include whisper.cpp's Whisper (`whisper-ggml`). The CUDA version is NVIDIA-only: the Model Worker enables `cuda` (candle's CUDA backend) and `whisper-ggml-cuda` (Whisper's ggml-cuda, statically linked; the packaging script passes `CMAKE_CUDA_ARCHITECTURES=80-virtual;86-real;89-real;120-real`, the same convention as candle's `CUDA_COMPUTE_CAP=80`; checked against the build scripts of whisper-rs-sys and ggml; the cudart, cublas, and cublasLt used by ggml-cuda are already in the bundled runtime libraries; the driver's `nvcuda.dll` is not bundled). The Vulkan version targets AMD / Intel GPUs and NVIDIA GPUs that do not support CUDA 13: it uses `whisper-ggml-vulkan` (the build installs the LunarG Vulkan SDK pinned by v2; at runtime it only needs the GPU driver's `vulkan-1.dll`), while candle stays on CPU. The workflow builds all three versions by default. None has been built yet. The CUDA version compiles ggml-cuda and will be noticeably slower, so the workflow's 150-minute timeout may not be enough; decide after one real run. Only cross-compiled on macOS (`x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`) with the package structure checked. The MSVC build, CUDA, the NSIS install, and starting the Runtime and each Worker on Windows have not been tested yet; `check:file-fonts` has also not been run against the packaged app |
| Windows app updates (§2.6) | Where the update source and installer live; how the v3 build number continues from v2; how the v2 Inno installation is migrated | Done: an installed copy is recognised when an NSIS `Uninstall CucCut.exe` sits next to the exe (the registry InstallLocation is not read, since that would mean parsing `reg.exe` output, which cannot be tested here; a copy installed elsewhere and later moved would be misidentified, which can be fixed later). The installer starts after the app has exited normally (the Runtime is stopped through IPC in `before-quit`), at `will-quit`. "Restart and update" uses `/S --updated --force-run`; when the update was already downloaded and the app exits normally, `/S --updated` is used (it does not open the app after installing). An installer run with `--updated` waits only about 1.3 seconds before ending processes still running in the install directory, so starting it too early would cut off the Runtime's cleanup; a Runtime not started by this app (for example one left over from a previous crash) is forcibly ended. After "Restart and update" installs, the app reopens with `--updated`, and one minute later it clears the installer cached from before this launch; an installer left by an exit-time install is cleared when a later check finds the app already current. The zip version falls back to manual download. The manifest is still schema-1, `format: exe`, split by variant (`appcast-x86_64-pc-windows-msvc[-cuda\|-vulkan].json`; the three installers share one appId; a manifest that states `variant` must match this build). The packaging script writes the build number and variant into the app according to `--build`, puts `build.<n>` in artifact names, writes a release report (file names, sizes, and sha256 of the installer and zip), and when `--download-base-url` is given also writes the update source and reads it back with the app's parser; packages without a build number do not check for updates (if `CUCCUT_UPDATE_APPCAST` is set, checks run as usual, for rehearsals; it also accepts Windows drive paths and `file:///C:/…`). Unsigned: authenticity relies only on HTTPS and the sha256 in the manifest; installers the app downloads itself carry no network origin mark, so a silent run should not pass through SmartScreen (not tested). Decided: the v3 update source and download page use GitHub (§2.6), and the build number keeps incrementing from the existing App releases; an update manifest is published only for platforms that are actually built. The v2 Inno installation lives at `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\CucCut`, which is the same directory as NSIS's `…\Programs\cuccut` when case is ignored; the two installers' silent switches do not recognise each other (v2 installers use `/SILENT`, NSIS recognises `/S`), so migration needs a separate design. In-app update has not been tested on Windows. The native candidate workflow includes a same-version overwrite with `/S --updated` and a self-check; `desktop-windows-publish` accepts only candidates that pass the native job, cross-checks them against the source commit of the published Mac build, then appends immutable Windows assets, and writes the Windows update manifest only after a public read-back passes. Mac assets and the historical skill Latest are unchanged (desktop README, "GitHub Actions release") |
| Stopping the Runtime on Windows | Shutdown of the Runtime when the desktop app exits | Changed: the main process sends `{ type: 'stop' }` over an IPC channel; the Runtime shuts down as in §2.4 and is force-killed if it has not exited after 8 seconds (§2.2). The Runtime stops Workers by closing stdin and `cancel`, not by signals. Not yet tested on Windows. Aborting ffmpeg / yt-dlp uses `SIGTERM`, which is a force kill on Windows; their output is discarded anyway |
| Supervision and priority of the Model Worker on Windows (§6.5) | Windows implementation of parent-process supervision, lowering the inference thread priority, and memory pressure signals | Supervision and priority lowering exist (§6.5) but have only been cross-compiled and linked, never run on Windows. The memory pressure signal does not exist yet (the earlier version had it only on macOS): when it cannot be read it is always normal, and memory is estimated from `os.totalmem()`. Candidates are the system low-memory notification (`QueryMemoryResourceNotification`), or a threshold set from the usage rate of `GlobalMemoryStatusEx`; measure on Windows first |
| Hints when external tools are missing | Installation hints for external tools such as `ffmpeg` are given per platform | Changed: the Runtime gives hints for the platform it runs on (`process-host`'s `ffmpegMissingRemedy`: macOS `brew`, Windows `winget`, Linux `apt`, others point to the official site); the interface shows the sentence the Runtime provides, and when there is none it only says what to install. On Windows, PATH is fixed at startup (the login shell is not read), so after installing, CucCut must be reopened; the hint says so. yt-dlp is downloaded by CucCut and is not involved |
| Local speech synthesis and text-to-image (§6.1, §6.3) | Choice of models and backends; model pack sizes and resource admission | Decided: ported unchanged from v2 (six speech synthesis engine families and the text-to-image model Qwen-Image), MLX path first; backends that depend on libtorch and non-macOS backends come later; batches and progress are in "Porting local models". The speech synthesis skeleton is complete: ten synthesis model packs are registered in the catalog (each file has sha256 and size; Qwen3-TTS's codec is shared as one copy), and they can be listed, downloaded, repaired, deleted, set as default, submitted, and checked. The Worker contract (`job.run`'s `synthesize`, stages and `steps` progress, `cuccut.speech-wav/v1`) is consistent on both sides, and the local Provider runs in the Worker process and reuses the validation of generation tasks. The Worker is connected to synthesis for all six engine families (Qwen3-TTS, IndexTTS2 and 2.5, VoxCPM2, OmniVoice, GPT-SoVITS); all ten model packs have been synthesized with real weights and pass checks. `worker.hello` declares the model families per engine in `synthesizeFamilies`; the Runtime hands the Worker only the listed families; unlisted ones fail with `capability-missing` without disabling the model pack; pronunciation annotations that cannot be read are reported as `reading-dropped` warnings in the task. The "Speech synthesis" page in settings (shared by Electron and Web) can download (with license confirmation), audition, set a default, and manage voices; the model catalog can be changed in settings (§6.3). Peak output of local synthesis is limited to −1 dBFS and is not amplified (Model Worker protocol spec §5.1); checks fail when clipped samples exceed 0.1%. Qwen3-TTS Base's loudness comes from the x-vector path (speaker embedding only, without the reference recording's transcript): with the 0.6B Base measured on built-in voices, the raw x-vector peak is about 3–5 dB above full scale; after limiting, the gate level stays at −10 to −12 dB. ICL, which includes the reference transcript, follows the reference recording's level (zh-female −26.8 dB, recording itself −24.9 dB; en-female −18.4 dB, recording itself −19.1 dB), with the same generation speed. v2 uses x-vector for built-in voices on Base; here the built-in voices are passed to the Worker together with their recording transcripts and go through ICL, so built-in voices are not loud, and only cloning without a transcript remains loud. Decided: built-in voices stay on ICL and do not return to v2's x-vector; 1.7B Base has not been measured. Options deliberately omitted relative to v2, with no interface in the prototype: per-request sampling parameters (temperature, top_k, top_p, repetition_penalty, max_tokens), each engine uses its own defaults; the tone note that VoiceDesign adds when naming a built-in voice (v2 concatenated two sentences); Qwen3-TTS's Beijing and Sichuan dialects (the language table has only ten languages). To add them back, change the prototype first. The target duration is not yet in the Worker contract. Undecided: IndexTTS 2.5's auxiliary weights borrow the whole IndexTTS2 repository, so installing only 2.5 downloads about 2.2 GB of IndexTTS2 main weights extra; whether to split out a manifest containing only the auxiliary files is open. The per-call limit of 2000 code points for local synthesis awaits measurement; emotion control is not yet in requests. Text-to-image (Qwen-Image-2.1, MLX 4bit) is connected: the pipeline is ported unchanged from v2, the model pack is registered in the catalog (all 20 files have sha256 and size), it can be downloaded, checked, and set as default, and the `local` Provider of `models.generateImage` is available; the Worker generated images with the real weights on this machine and reproduced them byte for byte. The candle path is ported unchanged from v2 (`image/qwen_image/candle/`, with unit tests) and registered as `qwen-image-2.1@candle`; on this machine's CPU it ran with real weights: 256² in two steps (the two runs are byte-identical) and 1024² in one step; peak and timing are in §6.5; `cuda` has only v2's measurements. To do: on CPU, the whole DiT is dequantized at every step and `proj` transposes and copies its weights every time, single-threaded (as in v2); this can be sped up by caching the dequantized and transposed matrices per layer, or by dequantizing in parallel. The candle path for synthesis is also complete: v2's `synthesize::tensor` facade and candle adaptation layer, with unit tests, were ported unchanged; all six engine families compile against the facade (the MLX path's numerics are unchanged, and the real-weight tests of the ten model packs were rerun and passed); each MLX model pack registers one `…@candle` variant; the Worker estimates resident amounts by parameter count. On this machine's CPU, real weights ran for Qwen3-TTS 0.6B (Worker end-to-end and engine tests), GPT-SoVITS, OmniVoice, and IndexTTS 2.5; IndexTTS2 and VoxCPM2 did not run for lack of memory; `cuda` has not been compiled or run. To do: candle synthesis on CPU uses only 1–2 cores and runs 6–145 times slower than real time. Model descriptions now state timing honestly, as text-to-image does: when the device is `cpu`, `notes` states per model how many times slower than real time it is (see §6.5); the sentence shown before auditioning in settings uses it, with no new interface element added. This sentence does not appear on the row before download (text-to-image is the same; that row has no place for a note) |
| Porting local models (§6.5, §13.6) | Batching; how the ported engines find model files | Decided: batching comes first (restoring the interfaces that v3 removed, pure-logic modules, manifest adaptation); then alignment and speakers; more recognition models (Qwen3-ASR 1.7B, Whisper, MOSS); vocal separation; local speech synthesis; local text-to-image. v2 engines find files by "model directory + relative path"; during porting this is replaced with manifest queries in `bundle.rs`: fetch a file set by component, fetch a file by relative path, fetch by directory and extension (not recursive), and take a subdirectory view. Only files the manifest lists are considered; the directory is not scanned. A missing file is `MODEL_NOT_INSTALLED`, a missing component is `MODEL_UNSUPPORTED`; backend load failures pass through unchanged and are not reported as load failures. Status: the front batch is complete. `model-runtime` regained the 1.7B configuration of the Qwen3 text decoder and its forward pass without KV cache (including the pre-final-layer, layer-fused, and layer-by-layer streaming paths), the 1.7B audio tower and forced-aligner configuration, filtered and streamed quantized reads of the Thinker text backbone, safetensors reads filtered by key, the Metal probe, MOSS's mel front end, and Silero whole-segment detection; `Qwen3Config` recognises the 1.7B structure. The pure-logic modules `refine`, `timestamp`, `speaker_cluster`, `vad_binarize`, `moss_parse`, `hint`, and the input layout for forced alignment came with their tests. `vad_binarize` is the whole-segment batch binarization and coexists with the `vad_stream` state machine used by streaming transcription; there is one struct only. The contract batch is also complete: `worker.hello` declares `transcribeFamilies` per model family; model packs check components by the family of `asr` (Whisper requires a tokenizer, MOSS does not require VAD); added the `tokenizer` component, a manifest query that hands out `.mlmodelc` by directory, a placeholder `coreml` backend, self-segmenting execution, and optional components. Language tables are one per model family (Qwen3-ASR 30, Whisper 100, MOSS 101, taken from v2), with one copy each in Rust and TypeScript. Qwen3-ASR 1.7B (MLX 8bit) is registered; the manifests for the aligner, the speaker model, and the Whisper and MOSS repositories are pre-registered. 0.6B and 1.7B have been run with real weights on this machine through the Worker transcription tests. The Whisper batch is complete: `whisper.rs` and its tests were ported unchanged to `backend/coreml/`; `objc2-core-ml` sits behind the `backend-coreml` feature and compiles only on macOS Apple Silicon; `whisper-large-v3@coreml` (the `openai_whisper-large-v3_947MB/` subdirectory of `argmaxinc/whisperkit-coreml`) and `whisper-large-v3-turbo@coreml` (with a prefix prefill model) are registered; the tokenizer comes from `openai/whisper-large-v3`; both were run with real weights on this machine through the Worker transcription tests and self-tests. Turbo's context prefill model is looked up by "language × 2 + task": row 0 of each language is transcription and row 1 is translation. v2 passed `task` as 1, which looked up the translation row (Turbo rarely translates, so the output showed no error); here it is changed to 0, and checked value by value against the transcription prefix seeded token by token. The alignment and speaker batch is also complete: the forced aligner and the WeSpeaker embedding were ported unchanged to `backend/mlx/` (`aligner.rs`, `wespeaker.rs`, with the shared fbank and alignment parts in `speech/`); the aligner is registered as an optional component of the four recognition model packs, and both the MLX and Core ML backends load it; the pipeline aligns segment by segment, and on error falls back to estimates with `alignment-failed` (v2 failed the whole task; here step 5 of this section applies); the Worker provides the `align` capability. The four recognition model packs were run on this machine with real weights through the transcription test with the aligner. WeSpeaker was ported for loading and embedding (MOSS's speaker merge uses it); Pyannote segmentation was not ported at that time and was later ported with the speaker diarization batch (see below). The more-recognition-models batch is also complete: MOSS's MLX engine (`backend/mlx/moss.rs`), chunked generation and repetition detection (`speech/moss_common`), and cross-chunk speaker merging (`speech/moss_speakers.rs`) were ported unchanged with their tests, thresholds unchanged; `moss-transcribe-diarize@mlx-8bit` is registered with quantization fixed at 8 bits; the optional aligner and WeSpeaker load only when used and are unloaded when the task ends; when system memory is tight, MOSS is unloaded first (as in v2); it was run on this machine with real weights for transcription, two-speaker separation, and self-tests. The non-macOS candle batch is complete: v2's candle backend (Qwen3-ASR, Silero, the aligner, MOSS, WeSpeaker), with its cross-checks and end-to-end tests, was ported unchanged to `backend/candle/`; see "Inference backend matrix on non-macOS". The text-to-image candle path (the `candle` module of `ccut-image-local`) was ported to `image/qwen_image/candle/`, sharing tokenization, scheduling, and the RoPE table with MLX; see "Local speech synthesis and text-to-image". The non-macOS Whisper batch is complete: v2's GGML backend (whisper.cpp) with its tests was ported unchanged to `backend/ggml/`; see that row; it has not yet been run with real weights. The MLX path of the vocal separation batch is complete: HTDemucs-FT (the combination of four sub-models, STFT and Transformer encoder/decoder) with v2's unit tests was ported unchanged to `separate/` in `model-runtime`; the Worker provides the `separate` capability and `separateFamilies`; `htdemucs-ft@mlx` is registered (`aufklarer/HTDemucs-FT-MLX`; the manifest's sizes and sha256 were checked against the repository installed on this machine) and is listed only on macOS Apple Silicon; the separation tests and Worker tests were run with real weights on this machine. The candle path for vocal separation is also complete: v2's candle tensor adaptation layer, with unit tests, was ported unchanged and shares one model graph with MLX (each backend compiles its own copy, so both can be built into the same binary); `htdemucs-ft@candle` is registered (same repository), listed only on platforms other than Apple Silicon, where it is the default for vocal separation; the separation tests were run on CPU with real weights, and the output was cross-checked against MLX (same mix, vocal and background SDR about 125 dB). Not implemented: the term-hint budget per model (in the prototype, prompt-prefix models get 180 characters, context-style models 1200, and models without a prompt channel 0; now the Runtime and interface use 1200 characters for terms across the board, and the Worker handles them per model family, see Model Worker protocol spec §2.5.1). The candle path for the local speech synthesis batch is also complete, see "Local speech synthesis and text-to-image": the candle synthesis model packs estimate the Worker's resource needs by parameter count. The speaker diarization batch is also complete: v2's Pyannote segmentation (MLX and candle versions, with shared post-processing and clustering) and the projection of speaker intervals onto words, with unit tests, were ported unchanged to `backend/mlx/pyannote.rs`, `backend/candle/pyannote.rs`, `speech/pyannote_common.rs`, and `speech/speaker_projection.rs`, thresholds unchanged; the "Speaker diarization" model pack is registered (§6.3); Qwen3-ASR and Whisper can separate speakers on all four backends (§6.6 step 7). Run on this machine with real weights: two-speaker diarization for MLX Qwen3-ASR 0.6B, Core ML Whisper large-v3, and candle CPU Qwen3-ASR 0.6B, with candle and MLX segmentation cross-checked. Speaker diarization of existing transcripts (the AI tool "identify speakers", §6.6) is also complete: the Worker installs this model pack alone to diarize existing transcripts; the `speakers` flow produces proposals and `edits.applySpeakers` applies them (undoable); the desktop app's tools page uses it; run with real weights on this machine for two-speaker diarization on MLX. Deferred: MLX synthesis model packs estimating the Worker's resource needs per model pack. Display of component licenses (WeSpeaker's CC-BY-4.0 attribution) is decided: it goes in the "License" row of model details, listing the weight license, plus a separate line for each component whose attribution or license differs from the weights; the prototype is done (the MOSS and speaker diarization packs list WeSpeaker's attribution); model pack status components already carry `license` (§6.3). The desktop app and Web interface will change only after the user confirms the prototype |
| Whisper backend on Apple Silicon (§6.5) | Replace Core ML Whisper with MLX, or keep both | Undecided. The MLX Whisper is implemented (`backend/mlx/whisper.rs`, written with mlx-rs following the structure of mlx-examples' `mlx_whisper` (MIT); decoding is shared with Core ML via `speech/whisper_decoding.rs`: same prefix handling, suppression, repeated-word detection and prompt budget). `whisper-large-v3@mlx` and `whisper-large-v3-turbo@mlx` are registered but not listed for now. Measured on this machine (M4, 16 GiB, `--release`, 161-second sample, turbo): whole-clip direct recognition MLX 6.8 s, Core ML 6.7–6.9 s, a tie; the Worker transcription pipeline (VAD cuts 18 segments) `asrMs` MLX 16.2–16.4 s, Core ML 12.3–12.4 s, so MLX is about 1.3× slower. The difference is in the encoder: each segment shorter than 30 s is still padded to a full 30-second window; on the GPU MLX takes about 750 ms per window (on par with Python's mlx-whisper at 728 ms), while Core ML is faster on the Neural Engine. Raising the segment length cap from 14.5 s to 29 s (15 segments) makes both faster and leaves the ratio unchanged (about 1.33×). Both backends' pipelines are nearly twice as slow as whole-clip direct recognition, because the pipeline pads each segment to a full 30-second window. Packing adjacent short segments into one 30-second window and splitting the text back per segment (this requires timestamp tokens or the aligner's word timings) would cut encoder runs by more than half on both sides, and MLX would likely catch up. This is a pipeline-layer change and will be reviewed separately. Loading: MLX 0.6–1.9 s plus about 1 s warm-up; Core ML after a new build needs about 95 s to compile on first load, then 0.6–3.6 s. Transcript text is essentially the same on both (on the short fixture each gets the brand name wrong once). MLX large-v3 has not been run with real weights (3 GB not downloaded). MLX Whisper needs `generation_config.json` from the tokenizer repo, so the manifest for `openai/whisper-large-v3` gains this file. Whether an install is complete is judged by the on-disk manifest: earlier installs whose tokenizer components are already present still count as installed (Core ML is unaffected) but lack this file, so when handed to the MLX package the Worker reports `MODEL_NOT_INSTALLED`. Before the MLX package is listed, existing installs must be made to download this file. | 
| Landing of generated assets (§6.1) | Whether synthesized audio and generated images may go straight onto the timeline in one step | Import only as candidate assets; later editing places them |
| Pairing and encryption of remote nodes (§6.7) | Whether to add encryption and certificate pinning later | Decided: first release is plaintext, same threat model as LAN printers; the protocol has a version gate, so these can be added later |
| Warm-up after importing assets (§6.6) | Whether importing automatically starts the Worker and pre-decodes | No warm-up; start on demand |
| Relationship between `cuccut.asr-result/v1` and `cuccut.speech/1` (§6.6) | Field mapping when applied; unifying with the §5.2 model of the format spec (format spec §9) | Results and documents are two schemas; the document only references the raw result in the extension |
| Strict offline, background-exit policy, diagnostic data retention | See product design §12 | Same as above |
| Performance and third-party capabilities | Target numbers and the actual capabilities of each adapter | Measured results on pinned versions govern (acceptance and testing §4, §5) |
| Types named only by name in the interface | Fields of `AgentInput`, `AgentEvent`, `RunContext`, `SampleRequest`, `FrameSurface`, `VerifiedCapabilities`, `FrozenCompositionInput`, `ResourceHandle`, `GrantScope`, etc. | The body describes in prose what they must contain; full schemas are generated from the single contract source (§13.2) |
| Task-level budget policy (§7.8, §12.5) | How much a task (task contract) may spend in total and how spending is totaled across providers; how a `taskId` on a Grant corresponds to the task contract | Decided: the task contract's `budgetPolicyRef` points to one task budget, recording call count and amount caps, totaled across providers. Every outbound action within a task (an agent's Job, flows started within the task and their sub-steps) must pass this budget in addition to the Grant, reusing the Grant ledger's reservation, start and settlement, and settled exactly once after a crash. The amount cap only accepts calls in the same currency whose cost can be estimated; unknown amounts or mixed currencies count as unable to guarantee (§7.8). A Grant's `taskId` only scopes matching and does not carry the task budget (§12.5) |
| Execution of acceptance checks (§3.2, §9.8) | How `quality`-kind checks are executed by QualityService, when, and how results are written back; whether a task may settle as complete when a "must-pass" check has not passed | Define and record only: checks and results live in the task contract, and results are recorded by the user or agent; QualityService does not execute checks, and task settlement does not consider check results |
